# SwiftUI Compatibility

A guide for porting SwiftUI code to TUIkit, and a record of **where** TUIkit's
public API matches or diverges from SwiftUI, **why**, and **whether the
divergence should change**.

TUIkit's stated rule (see `.claude/CLAUDE.md` → *SwiftUI API Parity*): public
APIs match SwiftUI signatures exactly **unless terminal constraints require
deviation**, and any deviation is documented. This file is that documentation.

Where code that compiles against both frameworks deliberately **behaves**
differently, [Deliberate deviations from SwiftUI.md](<Deliberate deviations from SwiftUI.md>) lists it, one entry
per difference: what SwiftUI does and how that is known, what TUIkit does, why,
and where it was decided. Read it before "fixing" a difference from SwiftUI.

---

## Two rules that decide every case

**1. The target is _source_ compatibility, not identical signatures.** SwiftUI
source should compile and mean the same thing. The declaration it compiles
against need not be the same declaration: extra *optional* parameters, an added
overload, a TUI-only modifier alongside the SwiftUI one — all fine, because none
of them changes what the SwiftUI spelling does. That is what licenses
`LocalizedStringKey`/`String` overload pairs (§4a), `Int` cell geometry instead
of `CGFloat` points (below), and the TUI-specific knobs that hang off controls
as modifiers rather than initializer arguments.

**2. Anything TUIkit cannot honour must FAIL TO COMPILE.** A stub that accepts a
request and quietly drops it is worse than an absence: the code builds, the
developer never learns the affordance is missing, and the defect surfaces to
their users instead. So an API whose meaning a character grid cannot carry is
removed, not stubbed — the compile error is the notification, and the developer
then decides what to do (drop it, or ask for a terminal-appropriate
substitute). `Font` is the pattern for when a substitute *does* exist: eleven
semantic styles are kept because "this is a heading" survives the medium as
intensity, while `Font.system(size:)` is absent because a point size does not.
`.submitLabel(_:)` was the pattern for when it does not, and was removed once it
was noticed — it stored a value nothing read.

The line between the two is worth stating because it is not "does the parameter
get used": `.monospaced()` and `.autocorrectionDisabled()` ignore their argument
and are correct, because the grid *already guarantees* what they ask for (and
`.compositingGroup()` returns the view, because a container's children are
already composited into one picture before an opacity above them applies)
(`Sources/TUIkitFoundation/Modifiers/MediumGuaranteedModifiers.swift`). A guarantee the
medium keeps for free is honoured; a request it silently discards is not.

---

## How this was determined

- **TUIkit side:** read from source (`Sources/…`, cited as `file:line`).
- **SwiftUI side:** the installed SDK's authoritative module interfaces —
  `SwiftUI.swiftinterface` **and** `SwiftUICore.swiftinterface` (most everyday
  `View` modifiers, `Color`, `Font`, layout, and `onChange` live in
  **`SwiftUICore`**) — with `@available(… deprecated:)` declarations excluded,
  and existence/deprecation confirmed by a `swiftc -typecheck` probe.
- **Version pinned:** Xcode 26.3 · Swift 6.2.4 · SwiftUI module 7.2.5 ·
  macOS 26.2 SDK. SwiftUI evolves; re-run against the current SDK when revising.

> A note on "deprecated": SwiftUI marks superseded API two ways — a real version
> (`deprecated: 14.0`, which the compiler warns on) and a "soft" forever-marker
> (`deprecated: 100000.0`, often with `renamed:`, no warning yet). **Both** mean
> "not the current spelling," so TUIkit omitting them is correct, not a gap.
> Examples correctly omitted: `foregroundColor` (→ `foregroundStyle`),
> `cornerRadius` (→ `clipShape`), `NavigationView` (→ `NavigationStack`/
> `NavigationSplitView`), `onChange(of:perform:)`, the old
> `ScrollView(_:showsIndicators:content:)` initializer. Deprecated spellings
> TUIkit once had are gone too (owner's ruling of 2026-10-12: no deprecated
> SwiftUI API): `Text + Text` (→ interpolating a `Text`, which keeps its
> styling), `UnitCurve.easeInEaseOut` (→ `easeInOut`),
> `WindowGroup.init(content:)` (→ `init(makeContent:)`; `WindowGroup { … }`
> binds either), and the `CoordinateSpace`-enum overloads of
> `GeometryProxy.frame(in:)`, `onTapGesture(count:coordinateSpace:perform:)`,
> `onContinuousHover(coordinateSpace:perform:)`, `DragGesture.init` and
> `SpatialTapGesture.init` (→ their `some CoordinateSpaceProtocol` forms, which
> `.local`, `.global` and `.named(_:)` bind to).

**This file is about vocabulary; behaviour is audited separately.**
[SwiftUI semantic audit (2026-08).md](<SwiftUI semantic audit (2026-08).md>) compares
what each shared API *means* against Apple's own doc comments, family by family.
Several rows below are contradicted by it — a ✓ here has never implied that the
semantics were checked, and now there is a place where they were.

---

## The guiding principle: measurement vs. data

The single most common divergence — `Int` instead of `CGFloat` — follows one
rule that resolves most of the numeric differences below:

| Kind of number | Type in TUIkit | Why |
|---|---|---|
| **An interface measurement** — a width, an x/y position, padding, spacing, a tap location | **`Int`** (and integer geometry, never `CGPoint`/`CGSize`) | A terminal is a grid of whole character cells. There is no "half a column." Floating-point here is not just unnecessary, it is *meaningless*, and inviting it leads to rounding bugs at every boundary. **Intentional divergence.** |
| **A value in your data model** — a `Stepper`'s count, a `ProgressView`'s fraction, a `Slider`'s position | **floating-point–capable** (generic over the value type, exactly like SwiftUI) | The number means something in *your* domain (a temperature, a price, a ratio). The renderer's cell-grid nature must not leak into your model. TUIkit is a faithful pass-through here. |

So: `.padding(8)` is `Int` and always will be; `Stepper(value: $temperature℃)`
accepts a `Double`. Keep these two ideas separate while reading the rest.

---

## Categories

1. [**Match** — ports cleanly, same API](#1-match)
2. [**Intentional divergence** — different on purpose; keep as-is](#2-intentional-divergence)
3. [**Open divergence** — known, documented, currently kept](#3-open-divergence)
4. [**No overlap** — one framework has it, the other doesn't](#4-no-overlap)
   - [4a. SwiftUI has it, TUIkit should add it](#4a-swiftui-has-it--tuikit-should-add-it)
   - [4b. SwiftUI has it, TUIkit won't (bitmap vs. text-cell)](#4b-swiftui-has-it--tuikit-wont-bitmap-vs-text-cell)
   - [4c. TUIkit-only (no SwiftUI equivalent)](#4c-tuikit-only-no-swiftui-equivalent)
5. [Summary](#5-summary)

---

## 1. Match

These port with no source change (modulo the `Int`-measurement rule in §2.1).
Code that uses them compiles and behaves the same.

| Area | API | TUIkit | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Core | `View`, `some View`, `@ViewBuilder`, `ViewModifier` (`body(content:)`, with `@State`/`@Environment` of its own, `Animatable`, `concat(_:)`; its app properties' `update()` runs once per body, where it is applied, as SwiftUI's does, measured — not again for a copy stored in a field, wrapped by `.transaction(_:)` or `.animation(_:)`, or joined by `concat(_:)`, decisions-DYNVFIX B-DYNVFIX-4), `ModifiedContent`, `EmptyModifier`, `EnvironmentalModifier`, `AnyView` (`init(_:)` and `init(erasing:)`), `TupleView` (`init(_:)`, `value`), `EquatableView` | ✓ | identity & composition match; an empty `@ViewBuilder` closure builds an `EmptyView`, as SwiftUI's does. TUIkit's cell-level transform is the separate, TUI-only `BufferModifier` (it was named `ViewModifier` until 2026-10-03, when SwiftUI source written against SwiftUI's did not compile) |
| State | `DynamicProperty` (an app's own wrapper built from `@State`/`@Environment` keeps them per view, found from each type's declared fields once per type, as SwiftUI finds them, so a custom `Mirror` changes nothing, a field that cannot hold a property is never read (a dead `unowned` reference among them), and a field declared as a plain class, held strongly, `weak` or `unowned`, is looked into by its object's class each body, where SwiftUI never looks (measured; decisions-DYNVFIX B-DYNVFIX-1), while an existential field's property is found each body whatever the field held first; `update()` runs before each `body`, inside the same observation scope (what it reads of an `@Observable` object redraws the view when it changes, as SwiftUI's does, measured), and what it changes in the property's own stored values is written back into the view `body` reads, as SwiftUI does — through the runtime's field metadata, for a struct's own fields, and only for a view type that holds an app property (decisions-DYNP B-DYNP-1, which supersedes decisions B4); an enum's payload — an enum view's or an enum property's — is written back too, projected through the enum's value witnesses, as SwiftUI writes it back (measured, B-DYNP-1), except an `indirect` case's, whose payload every copy shares (decisions-DYNVFIX B-DYNVFIX-2); what a class property holds is installed as the object's, where SwiftUI installs nothing inside a class (measured; decisions-DYNVFIX B-DYNVFIX-3): its `@State` is the object's own, so two views showing one object read and write one state, which no change of the views holding it moves or drops, and a write redraws every view drawn holding it, one served from the memo included; an object made anew with each value of the view holding it keeps that view's state from frame to frame; its `@Environment` is each holding view's while that view's body and content run, and the first view's drawn holding it at rest, in closures; its nested properties are updated in place, and its own `update()` runs before each holding view's body, as SwiftUI's does; a property held `unowned` is updated and one held `weak` is not, as in SwiftUI, measured; a property in an optional field has its environment resolved and its `update()` run and written back, where SwiftUI does neither (measured; decisions-DYNP A-DYNP-1); a `@State` or `@FocusState` inside a property is keyed by where it is declared in the view, so an optional, an existential or an enum case changing never hands one property's state to another; when an enum's case — an enum view's, or an enum property's — replaces another, the new case's `@State` starts at its defaults, and so does the old case's when it comes back, as SwiftUI's does, measured; an optional property that comes back finds the state it left, as SwiftUI's does with `Optional` made to conform, measured; a property beside other values in an enum's case has its `@State` bound, as SwiftUI's is (measured), and its environment resolved and its `update()` run (unmeasured on SwiftUI); decisions-SKEY), `@State` (`init(wrappedValue:)` + `init(initialValue:)`, and `init()` for an optional that starts `nil`; one view value drawn at two places, or one property value handed to two views, keeps a state for each place, as SwiftUI's does, measured; an `@Environment` there is resolved for each place too, so a closure made in one place's body reads that place's environment), `@Binding` (`init(get:set:)`, `.constant`, `init(projectedValue:)`, **dynamic-member lookup**, `.animation(_:)`, `.transaction` + `.transaction(_:)`, **`Collection` / `BidirectionalCollection` / `RandomAccessCollection` where the value is a `MutableCollection`, and `Identifiable` where the value is**), `@Environment(\.key)`, `@Namespace` (`Namespace.ID`) | ✓ | `$model.field` and `initialValue:` both work. A `@Namespace` id is derived from the holding view's identity and the property's place among its dynamic properties, so it is stable from frame to frame and never shared; read before the view first renders it is a placeholder unique to that property value, as SwiftUI's is outside a body. A binding's `transaction` is `nil` internally until one is stated, and only then does a write wrap itself: an empty `Transaction` is the statement "this change is not animated", so storing one by default would make every plain binding silently override the `withAnimation` its caller is inside. `.animation(_:)` is the same mechanism under a name — `withAnimation(a)` is `withTransaction(Transaction(animation: a))` — and `.animation(nil)` remains an explicit refusal that survives an enclosing animation. A `Binding` to a collection is a collection OF BINDINGS — `$rows.count`, `for $row in $rows`, `$rows.last` — which is the mapping `ForEach($items)` was already doing privately, now reachable by anything and built from one place. Its element subscript is deliberately not a plain forward: an element binding outlives the frame that made it, so a read past the end answers with the value that was there rather than trapping, and a write past the end is dropped |
| Observation | `@Observable` + `@Environment(Type.self)` (and `@Environment(Type.self) var x: Type?`, which reads `nil` where none was set, where the non-optional form traps) + `.environment(obj)` (an optional object, `nil` clearing one placed further out, as SwiftUI's is; the semantic audit's items 87 and 88, 2026-10-14) | ✓ | modern reference-type state ports as-is |
| Env / prefs | `EnvironmentKey`, `EnvironmentValues`, `PreferenceKey`, `.environment(_:_:)`, `.preference`/`.onPreferenceChange`/`.transformPreference`, `.overlayPreferenceValue`/`.backgroundPreferenceValue`, `.anchorPreference(key:value:transform:)`/`.transformAnchorPreference(key:value:transform:)` with `Anchor`, `Anchor.Source` and `GeometryProxy`'s anchor subscript | ✓ | custom keys work the SwiftUI way: a scope's reduction starts from the first value set (the default is only what an unset key reads), and `transformPreference` rewrites the subtree's reduced value — the default, if nothing below set one — before the ancestors see it; `overlayPreferenceValue`/`backgroundPreferenceValue` build a layer from what the view's subtree published and lay it in the view's frame as `.overlay`/`.background(alignment:content:)` do — the view's size, and both views' values reach the parent. **Anchors (2026-10-05, decisions A2):** `Anchor<CellRect>` and `Anchor<CellPoint>` are SwiftUI's `Anchor<CGRect>` and `Anchor<CGPoint>` in cells, with every `Anchor.Source` — `.bounds`, `.rect(_:)`, `.point(_:)`, `.unitPoint(_:)` and the nine named points, arrays and optionals; `proxy[anchor]` resolves one relative to the READING proxy's origin, as measured on SwiftUI (probes L1 and L2). A reader in an `overlayPreferenceValue`/`backgroundPreferenceValue` layer resolves the anchors its base made exactly, in the frame that draws them: the base is drawn before the layer is built, so where each anchored view is is already known. A reader that fills the layer — the usual shape — needs nothing more; one placed elsewhere in the layer is answered with the layer's origin the first time and costs the layer one more drawing that frame (the base is never drawn again). A unit point is floored once, from the whole product — the centre of an 8-wide view is column 4. **Where it differs:** an anchor kept elsewhere — in `@State`, read by a reader in another subtree — resolves through the global space, from where the anchored view was when the last frame was drawn. A view inside a `Table` cell makes an anchor that names no place (the table composes its cells as text) |
| Stacks | `VStack` / `HStack` / `ZStack` / `LazyVStack` / `LazyHStack`, `LazyVStack`'s and `LazyHStack`'s `pinnedViews:` with `PinnedScrollableViews` (`.sectionHeaders`, `.sectionFooters`) | ✓ | a `Section` in a stack is its header's, content's and footer's views, the stack's own children (Sections row); pinned section headers and footers → §2.8 (a `LazyHStack`'s pin to a horizontal scroll view's leading and trailing edges); `spacing:` is `Int?` → §2.1; `nil`, the default, → §2.1 note (a stack uses the views' default preference between every pair, where SwiftUI asks each pair — the two differ only between two custom layouts that prefer no space, see the decisions doc); a row too narrow for its children shares its width among those of one priority as SwiftUI's does — the least flexible first, each offered an equal share of what is left and asked what it takes of it, spacers what the others leave — and places them in order only where even their minimums do not fit; a column places them in order (Deliberate deviations, Slated to change); a `Spacer` asks for no space beside it, its `minLength` reads and sets under SwiftUI's name (in cells, `Int?` → §2.1), and with no `minLength` it is one default gap long at least — a column in a row, none in a column — as SwiftUI's is 8 points, so a row with no room to spare is as wide with one as without (decisions A-BITEMS1-1); lazy-stack semantics differ → §2.8 |
| Iteration | `ForEach` (`id:` keypath, `Identifiable`, `Range<Int>`, over a `Binding` to a collection — `ForEach($items) { $item in … }`, with and without `id:` — and over a view's subviews or sections, `ForEach(subviews:content:)` and `ForEach(sections:content:)`, see Subviews and Sections below), `Section` (header/footer/title, and `Section(_:isExpanded:content:)` / `Section(isExpanded:content:header:)`: the header behind a `▼`/`▶` triangle and one control — a Tab stop that Return or Space toggles, Right opens and Left closes, a click toggles — and a collapsed section's content not drawn, its rows leaving a list, in every list style, as measured on macOS 15.8 (which collapses in every style; its sidebar shows the toggle only under the pointer)), `Group` | ✓ | all seven `ForEach` forms present. A row of several views hands each of them to the container, as SwiftUI's does (measured): `ForEach(0..<2) { _ in Group { a; b } }` is four children of a stack, four subviews of a `Layout` and four rows of a `List`, each tagged with its element; a row of a loop is the loop's rows, a row of a section its parts (a `List` reads it as a section), an `EmptyView` row nothing. A row whose view count its type states (`Group { a; b }`, a tuple) keeps a lazy stack lazy — a child's element is its ordinal divided by the count — and one whose count only the row knows (a loop in it, an `if` whose arms differ, an `if` without `else` among its views — `Text(name); if flagged { Text("!") }` is two views for some elements and one for others, as SwiftUI counts it) is built to be counted on every walk, each of its views still selected and tagged by its element; an `if` without `else` that is a row's only view is one child, empty while false, where SwiftUI counts none; a `List` deletes the element any of its rows draws, as SwiftUI's does (measured), and a reorder picks up all of an element's rows and lands them only between elements, `.onMove` handed the elements' offsets (by Ctrl-R, Ctrl+Up/Down or a drag; SwiftUI's drag could not be driven offscreen to measure). The binding forms hand each row a `Binding<Element>`, which is what a row of editable controls needs — a `Toggle` binds to `$item.enabled` rather than to an index-keyed lookup. Note that `$dictionary[key]` is a `Binding<Value?>` and `$dictionary[key, default: x]` does not compile at all (the `default:` autoclosure cannot form a key path) — both are equally true of SwiftUI. TUIkit adds `Binding.defaulted(to:)` for that case (§4c); SwiftUI's answer is an explicit `Binding(get:set:)`. **SwiftUI's own `Binding(_:)` ships too** — the failable unwrap, `if let name = Binding($profile.nickname)` — and its other two forms (2026-10-14, the semantic audit's item 86): `Binding(_:)` projecting a non-optional binding to an optional one, whose written `nil` leaves the source as it is, and `Binding(get:set:)` whose setter is handed the write's `Transaction` (the binding's own, else the one in force, else an empty one), and (2026-10-18) `Binding<AnyHashable>(_:)` erasing a `Hashable` binding, whose write of another type is reported and dropped where SwiftUI traps (Deliberate deviations S7). The two are the same question answered for different interfaces: `defaulted(to:)` decides what `nil` *draws as* and always shows the control; `Binding(_:)` fails, so the view hierarchy can branch and give the absent case an interface of its own. Emptiness is checked once, at construction: a source emptied afterwards reads back the value it had rather than trapping, and the `if let` re-runs on the next frame — SwiftUI behaves the same way, for the same reason |
| Data | `List` — content-closure **and** data-driven `List(_:id:selection:rowContent:)` (the selection bindings optional, as SwiftUI's are: `nil` selects nothing; and SwiftUI's non-optional `Binding<SelectionValue>` form, a selection that cannot be emptied, `@_disfavoredOverload` as SwiftUI declares it so that `$selection` for an optional or a `Set` keeps its own form — every form but the two over a binding to a tree, which have no optional twins either; the semantic audit's item 76), `Table` with SwiftUI's column surface: `TableColumn<RowValue, Sort, Content, Label>` in every form but `LocalizedStringResource` (key path, view content, `comparator:`, `sortUsing:`, a `Text` label), `TableColumnContent`, `TableColumnBuilder` (an `if`, `Group`, a builder property; no `for`, as in SwiftUI), `TableColumnForEach`, `TupleTableColumnContent`, `TableColumn.width(_:)` and `width(min:ideal:max:)` in cells (each bounded column starts at its ideal and shares the rest evenly, SwiftUI's macOS rule as measured; `.width(.fit)` and `.width(.ratio(_:))` are TUIkit's own; a column is resized by dragging the gap after its header — see column customization), column customization — `TableColumnCustomization` (Codable, in an encoding of TUIkit's own; `@SceneStorage`/`@AppStorage` of one with no default written), `TableColumnCustomizationBehavior`, `.customizationID(_:)`, `.defaultVisibility(_:)`, `.disabledCustomizationBehavior(_:)` and every `columnCustomization:` initializer; a hidden-by-default column is hidden with or without a binding, as measured; the user customizes it as on macOS: a right-click (or Ctrl-click) on the header opens the column menu — Hide for the column clicked, then every column marked while shown, as measured on macOS 15.8 — dragging a title moves its column, dragging the gap after it resizes it within its declared bounds (a `width(_:)` column, fixed, does not resize, as SwiftUI's does not), and the resized width is kept in the customization and its encoding, as SwiftUI keeps it; from the keyboard, Ctrl-K (`RowAction.customizeColumns`) picks a column, drawn picked out in the header, ←/→ pick another, `<`/`>` move it, `-`/`+` resize it, Return opens the menu on it and Escape lets it go; `disabledCustomizationBehavior(_:)` takes away what it names; only a column with a `customizationID` takes part, and a table with no binding takes no gesture (SwiftUI resizes any column, and reorders with a binding; decisions-D4T B-D4T-7) — `.tableColumnHeaders(_:)` (hidden headers give the rows their line, and a horizontally compact size class hides them and shows the first column alone, across the table, as SwiftUI's does on iPhone, decisions B3; the keyboard still sorts), `TableStyle` and `.tableStyle(_:)` — `.bordered` is TUIkit's rounded box, `.inset` draws no border (as measured, SwiftUI's has none), TUIkit's own `.surface` (a prototype) draws `.inset` on a surface of its own with the header a step stronger (decisions D6L A-D6L-1), a custom style draws through its body and can compose a built-in one's; `.automatic` is bordered where SwiftUI's macOS one is inset (decisions A29), `.alternatingRowBackgrounds(_:)` and `AlternatingRowBackgroundBehavior` on tables and lists alike (the stripe a striped `ListStyle` always drew; `.automatic` stripes a table under every style and leaves a list plain, as measured on macOS, decisions A29; the second row is the first striped, as measured), `TableColumnAlignment` (all six) and `.alignment(_:)` on any column content — the nearest statement wins, and `.numeric` is trailing for every numbering system, as measured on macOS; `Table(_:selection:sortOrder:columns:)` over any `RandomAccessCollection` and any `SortComparator`; and the row surface — `Table(of:selection:sortOrder:columns:rows:)` and `Table(selection:sortOrder:columns:rows:)`, `TableRowContent`, `TableRow`, `TableRowBuilder` (an `if`, `if`/`else`, `Group`, row content of an app's own type), `Section` among the rows with `TableHeaderRowContent` (every row form: a title, a title and `isExpanded:`, a header view with or without one, none) — a header row across the table above the section's rows, styled as a `List`'s section header, which the cursor steps over, select-all leaves out and a click does not select, as measured on macOS 15.8; a collapsible section's header is its disclosure control (a Tab stop, Return/Space/arrows and a click toggle) and a collapsed section's rows leave the table, its header staying; a section with no rows still draws its header, in a table with no rows at all too (its headers, as many as fit, in place of the placeholder); a hierarchical table draws its sections' rows without their headers; a sectioned table lays its rows out as a multi-line one does, `ForEach` of rows (`ForEach(data)` and `ForEach(data) { TableRow($0) }`, with `data`/`content` public as SwiftUI's are), `DynamicTableRowContent`, `TupleTableRowContent`, `TableForEachContent`, `EmptyTableRowContent`, `ModifiedContent` of rows (a row modifier's result, a `DynamicTableRowContent` when its content is one), `TableRowContent.selectionDisabled(_:)` (the row refuses the selection, the cursor steps over it and it draws faint, as a `List` row does; the modifier nearest the row wins), `TableRowContent.onHover(perform:)` (told on entering and leaving any line of the row; an `.onHover` around the whole table takes the pointer from its rows, as an outer one does from any view); context menus — `TableRowContent.contextMenu(menuItems:)` and `(menuItems:preview:)` (`_ContextMenuTableRowModifier`, `_ContextMenuPreviewTableRowModifier`; the preview not shown, as on macOS) give a row its own menu, as `.contextMenu(menuItems:)` on a `List` row's view does — no focus stop of its own there, the list's Shift+F10 opening the cursor row's — and `contextMenu(forSelectionType:menu:primaryAction:)` on a `List` or `Table` gives its rows one menu built from the rows it acts on, as measured on macOS 15.8: a secondary click on a selected row acts on the whole selection, on an unselected row on that row alone, on empty space on none (a table's column header is not empty space: a right-click there goes on to a menu around the table, as measured); the selection menu wins over a row's own and over a `.contextMenu` on a row's view; a secondary click is a right-click, or a Ctrl-click (Option-click still toggles a row's selection); Shift+F10 (or a PC keyboard's Menu key, `Key.menu`, where the terminal reports it — under the kitty keyboard protocol) opens either menu on the cursor row; a menu that builds nothing does not open, its click spent all the same; one whose item type is not the container's selection type never activates, nor does one around a `List` with no selection binding (measured: SwiftUI's opens no menu and runs no primary action), and neither stops a row's own menu. `primaryAction` runs on a double-click on a row and on Return (Space still selects), with the set that rule gives for the row — the cursor row on Return, where macOS acts on the selection, a TUIkit list's cursor being a row apart from it; row drag and drop — `TableRowContent.draggable(_:)` and `.itemProvider(_:)` (`ItemProviderTableRowModifier`) carry a row's payload out, its line following the pointer, a motionless press still a click; `TableRowContent.dropDestination(for:action:)` takes a drop onto the row, drawn as selected while the drag is over it; `DynamicTableRowContent.dropDestination(for:action:)` and `.onInsert(of:perform:)` (`OnInsertTableRowModifier`) take payloads between a loop's rows, the gap opening as for `Table.dropDestination`, at offsets into the loop's own collection — payloads need no `Transferable`, as for every TUIkit drag; a draggable row is not picked up by `Table.onMove`; a hierarchical table's loops take no insertions (decisions B80); hierarchical tables — `Table(_:children:selection:sortOrder:columnCustomization:columns:)` in all six forms, `OutlineGroup` of table rows (all six initializers), `DisclosureTableRow` (with or without an `isExpanded:` binding) and `TableOutlineGroupContent`: the first column draws the tree, a click on a triangle or Right/Left on the focused row opens and closes it, ⌥ carries that through the subtree, as in an outline `List` | ✓ | data-driven `List` routes through the windowed `ForEach` path (O(visible)). `Table` keeps ONE generic parameter, `Table<Value>` where SwiftUI's is `Table<Value, Rows, Columns>`: the columns — and a `rows:` closure's rows — are flattened when the table is built, and no SwiftUI source names the full type. Flattening runs a `ForEach`'s content once per element per build, where SwiftUI's rows are lazy; a tree's closed branches are never walked. A cell is a `String` key path's value, or the view its content closure builds — a plain `Text` drawn exactly as the key-path string is, anything else rendered at the column's width. A cell is one line unless its column's `lineLimit(_:)` allows more, where SwiftUI grows each row to its tallest cell (decisions B44); the TUI-specific `.tableRowHeights(.automatic)` opts in to SwiftUI's growth for content-closure columns with no stated limit (a view at its natural height, measured at the column's width; a `Text` wrapped there, where SwiftUI's truncates), and `.automaticExceptText` grows only the cells that are not a `Text` — at the price of the multi-line layout, which measures rows to learn their heights. A control in a view cell takes the keyboard — a Tab stop, pressed with Return or Space — and the pointer: a click on a `Button` in a cell presses it, a `.onTapGesture` on a cell's view fires and a `.contextMenu` on one opens, while a click anywhere else on the row selects it. The TUI-only options are modifiers: `.focusID(_:)`, `.tableEmptyPlaceholder(_:)`, `.tableColumnSpacing(_:)`. **Type select** works in both: with a list or table focused, typing a row's first characters — within a second of each other, ignoring case — moves the cursor to the first row after it whose text starts with them (a list row's first line of text, a table row's first column), or to the text a row states with `.typeSelectEquivalent(_:)` (`""` takes the row out); Return or Space then selects, as after an arrow key. A prefix's first character gives way to a bare-key binding that claims it — `q`, `t`, `a`, the help key, an app's bare shortcut (decisions B77). `\.defaultMinListRowHeight` and `\.defaultMinListHeaderHeight` (in lines) give a list's rows and section headers a least height, the content centred in it as a macOS list centres a short row. `.sectionActions(content:)` on a list's `Section` draws its controls one cell after the header's title, always shown (macOS shows them under the pointer) and reached by Tab and a click; a custom container reads them as `SectionConfiguration.actions` (decisions B78) |
| Scrolling | `ScrollView(_:content:)` (its `axes`, `content` and `showsIndicators` settable, as SwiftUI's are), `ScrollViewReader` / `ScrollViewProxy.scrollTo(_:anchor:)` (reaching a `List`'s or a `Table`'s rows too, by their `ForEach` or data ids, without moving the focus or the selection), `defaultScrollAnchor(_:)` and `defaultScrollAnchor(_:for:)`, `scrollContentBackground(_:)`, `scrollClipDisabled(_:)` (§2.9), `scrollTargetBehavior(_:)` with the `ScrollTargetBehavior` protocol, `ScrollTarget`, `ScrollTargetBehaviorContext`, `ScrollTargetBehaviorProperties` and its context, `AnyScrollTargetBehavior`, `.paging` (`PagingScrollTargetBehavior`) and `.viewAligned` (`ViewAlignedScrollTargetBehavior`, every limit and anchor), `contentMargins(_:_:for:)` ×2 and `contentMargins(_:for:)` with `ContentMarginPlacement`, `ScrollGeometry` + `onScrollGeometryChange(for:of:action:)`, `ScrollPhase` + `ScrollPhaseChangeContext` + `onScrollPhaseChange(_:)` ×2, `scrollBounceBehavior(_:axes:)` + `ScrollBounceBehavior` + `\.verticalScrollBounceBehavior`/`\.horizontalScrollBounceBehavior`, `onScrollVisibilityChange(threshold:_:)`, `scrollTransition(_:axis:transition:)` and `scrollTransition(topLeading:bottomTrailing:axis:transition:)` with `ScrollTransitionPhase` and `ScrollTransitionConfiguration` (and its `Threshold`), `scrollEdgeEffectStyle(_:for:)` with `ScrollEdgeEffectStyle` (`.automatic`, `.hard`) and `scrollEdgeEffectHidden(_:for:)` | ✓ | **scroll edge effects (2026-10-14, refusal review O7)**: scrolled content never passes under a bar here — a `safeAreaBar`, a toolbar or a navigation bar is subtracted from what the view beside it is offered — so it stops at the bar's edge, which is what `.automatic`, `.hard` and a hidden effect all describe; the modifiers return the view, and the tests assert that guarantee. `.soft`, content seen faded through a bar, is the one style that cannot be drawn and is left out, so code asking for it fails to compile. **the top-row hold (2026-10-12, decisions B30)**: with nothing else holding it — no anchor declared, or away from the edge a declared one names — a scroll view keeps the row at the top of its viewport, and the rows on screen with it, where they are as rows are inserted above it, as SwiftUI's lazy stacks do (measured, `Tools/SwiftUIProbes/Scroll anchoring/hold.swift`): in a lazy stack on each of its three paths, and in `List` and `Table`, whose cursor moves with the rows. As in SwiftUI it holds nothing at the top, where the new rows show, nor for a change made under `scrollContentOffsetAdjustmentBehavior = .disabled`, nor through a reorder that parts the top row from the rows either side of it (a sort keeps the offset), nor in an eager `VStack`; and it holds nothing while following the tail or for a seek, which place the frame themselves. Two differences are deliberate: `List` and `Table` hold, where macOS's `List` does not (R13), and a removal above is held as an insertion is (R14). `.scrollWindowHold(.offset)` (§4c) keeps the offset in lines instead (`Documentation/Layout and scrolling/Scroll anchoring.md` §5). **scroll transitions (2026-10-05, decisions A2, the owner's answer 5)**: a view's transition is drawn from where it is in the scroll view's viewport — predicted before it is drawn (the scroll view places its content at minus its offset, the stacks place their rows), so the rows of a stack scroll through without a second drawing, and held to by the scroll view, which draws its content once more when the prediction missed. The phase is the edge's (`.topLeading` above the middle, `.bottomTrailing` below) until the view is in as far as the configuration's threshold, then `.identity`; `.interactive` interpolates the EFFECT between the two by how far in the view is, paced by its curve, `.animated` switches at the threshold and animates there, `.identity` never changes. Distances are cells: a one-row view goes from hidden to visible in one step. A view in no scroll view is drawn in the identity phase. **Where it differs:** SwiftUI's default threshold is not measured — a pixel probe of an offscreen host disagreed with itself (decisions D5G B-D5G-8) — and is taken as `.visible`, the point the configuration's `threshold(_:)` calls "fully visible". A `List` does not own `.scrollView` (decisions D5G B-D5G-9), so a transition in a list's row follows a scroll view around the list, or is drawn in the identity phase where there is none; SwiftUI's list is the scroll view. Effects are the ones a cell can show (`VisualEffect`). **scroll visibility (2026-10-05, decisions A7)**: as measured on SwiftUI (macOS 15), an observed view reports once when it first appears, shown or not, then at each crossing of its threshold by the fraction of it the nearest scroll view (or `List`) shows — a fraction AT the threshold counts as shown, so `threshold: 0` reports every view shown — and a view in no scroll view is reported shown wherever it is. Measured against what the scroll view draws once its offset is final, and reported when the frame is done, so a view drawn twice in one frame reports once. One addition: a row a lazy stack stops drawing because it scrolled out of the stack's window is reported hidden — it is still in the tree here, where SwiftUI's lazy stack destroys it and reports nothing until it is made again; a view removed from the tree is reported in neither. A view inside a `Table` cell is not seen (the table composes its cells as text). **scroll target behaviours (2026-10-05, decisions A6)**: a terminal's scroll gestures end at every step — a wheel tick, an arrow or page key, a scrollbar arrow or the "N more" lines — so each step is a gesture of its own: its landing is the proposed target (`ScrollTarget`, in cells), the behaviour moves it, and the view goes where the rect meets the visible area at the target's anchor; a released scrollbar thumb or page track is a gesture with no velocity, and a `ScrollView` whose viewport changes size consults the behaviour too, as SwiftUI's does, unless it rests at an edge. Home and End go to the edges, and programmatic scrolls (`scrollTo`, a `ScrollPosition`, the focus reveal) are not gestures, as in SwiftUI. A step the behaviour lands on the other axis (a two-axis `.paging` view settling its rows on a Left key) is a user scroll of that axis too — moving the rows releases a bound `anchorPosition` to `.window`, as any user scroll does — and such a step, or one held short of an edge, is spent on the view: the key is consumed and the wheel does not pass on to an enclosing scroller, which it does only at an edge; a view asks its behaviour only along the axes it scrolls. The scrollers the framework puts around a tall menu, a sheet's or a framed container's content take no behaviour — they scroll a line a step whatever the page states — while the scrollables in that content do. The context's `velocity` is the step in cells, not points per second. A `ScrollView` consults any behaviour on both axes; `.paging` moves every step to the next page boundary in its direction (at least one page, the boundary at or past the step), the last page ending at the content's end, which is a boundary too for a gesture with no direction (a released thumb, a resize, a step along the other axis rests on the nearest one); the visible area a behaviour is handed is the content window, and under a behaviour the "N more" lines (the `.text` style) are kept out of it at every offset wherever the content overflows (decided once per layout; content that fits reserves nothing and shows no "▲ 0 more"), as `.scrollIndicators(.visible)` keeps them, rather than drawn over its first and last line — so every line is read at some page, `.viewAligned` lands a row below "▲", and pages framed with `containerRelativeFrame` are the pages `.paging` steps (decisions-D2S B-D2S-6); `.viewAligned` settles on the rows of a vertical stack or the columns of a horizontal one — lazy or eager, marked with `scrollTargetLayout()` — that is the content (or under a `Section`'s header or the content margins), at the furthest view a step reaches and at least the next (a gesture with no direction at the nearest view, or at the content's end when that is nearer), with SwiftUI's limits (`.alwaysByOne` one view, `.alwaysByFew` three, `.always` a screenful, `.automatic` a screenful only along a compact size class) and anchors; unmarked, as in SwiftUI, there is nothing to settle on. `List` and `Table` honour the two built-in behaviours — `.paging` as a page a step from where the view is, `.viewAligned` as whole-row steps (the old `ScrollGranularity.row`) with its limits, its anchor not honoured (decisions-D2S B-D2S-3) — and consult no behaviour of the app's own, as SwiftUI's `List` on macOS consults none at all (measured; B-D2S-2). **scroll content background (2026-10-05, decisions A9)**: none of the five scrollables (`ScrollView`, `List`, `Table`, `Form`, `TextEditor`) draws a background of its own here — a `List` paints its row highlights and nothing behind them — so `.automatic` and `.hidden` leave them as they are and SwiftUI's commonest use, `.scrollContentBackground(.hidden).background(…)`, draws as it does there; `.visible` paints the palette's background (`.windowBackground`) behind the whole view, border and bars included, which is the page's own colour and what makes a scrollable opaque over a coloured container (macOS's reading: `.visible` is what gives a `ScrollView` the background a `List` has by default). The scrollers the framework puts around a menu, a sheet's or a framed container's too-tall content are chrome and do not paint; the scrollables in that content do. **bounce behaviour (2026-10-03)** decides WHEN a scrollable may be pushed into its overscroll allowance (`.scrollOverscroll`, TUI-specific, which decides how far): `.basedOnSize` only while the content overflows, `.always`/`.automatic` whatever its size, which is TUIkit's default as it is SwiftUI's; a push into an allowance the app asked for stays rather than springing back, TUIkit's deliberate-push signal; **`.always` bounces (2026-10-13)**: an end the app gave no allowance may be pushed two lines (one in a view under eight lines tall), held while the steps keep coming and sprung back a line a frame once they have been quiet 250 ms (timed from the frame that drew the last step, so within a frame of the scroll going idle), in `ScrollView`, `List` and `Table`; an arrow or page key at the end pushes it as the wheel does, as it pushes any allowance, where SwiftUI's keyboard scrolling does not bounce (unmeasured; B-SCROLLB-3); under `.automatic` (the default) with no allowance nothing is pushed at all, as nothing bounces on macOS under a mouse wheel; the allowance is vertical only, so a horizontal behaviour is recorded (decisions B31, B-SCROLLB-2). **scroll phases (2026-10-03)**: a terminal scrolls in discrete steps and TUIkit never animates a scroll, so a view is `.idle` or `.interacting` — from the user's first step until the steps have been quiet 250 ms (15 frames), and while a scrollbar is held — and `.tracking`/`.decelerating`/`.animating` are never produced; a programmatic scroll changes no phase (SwiftUI's unanimated one does not either, measured), the first frame reports `.idle` to `.idle` (measured), and the context's `velocity` is `(0, 0)` at every report, never `nil`, as SwiftUI's is at its appear call and at both changes of an animated `scrollTo` (measured; decisions B27). **scroll geometry (2026-10-03)** reads as SwiftUI's does, in cells: `contentOffset` sits before the origin by the top/leading margin (and further out while pushed into an overscroll allowance, as SwiftUI's does while rubber-banding), `contentSize` excludes the margins, `containerSize` is the visible area less them, `bounds` is the container at the offset and `visibleRect` the whole visible area at it; the action runs on the first frame with old == new and then on each change of the derived value, and only the first scroll view below the modifier reports — all measured. The visible area is the viewport less what the scroll bars take (a column, a row), since here they are cells of it and not overlays. **content margins (2026-10-03)** are cells a scroll view keeps inside its scrollable area, so they scroll with the content (a lazy stack under them still windows, seeks and reports its row — the window is relayed below the top margin, as a `Section` relays it below a header); `containerRelativeFrame` in the content sizes against the viewport less them (and less the two "N more" lines when they are reserved, drawn at every offset under `.scrollIndicators(.visible)` with the `.text` style, and less a horizontal bar's row where the bar is `.visible`: the window the content is seen through, which is also `ScrollGeometry`'s container and a scroll target behaviour's, so a page sized against it is the page `.paging` steps — except a two-axis pager whose horizontal bar is `.automatic`, which comes with overflow and is decided after the container, so its container-sized pages are a row taller than the step and drift by it at each stop); under `.automatic` and `.scrollIndicators` a bar is shortened along its axis and moved in across it (a vertical bar by top/bottom and trailing, a horizontal one by leading/trailing and bottom), while the "N more" lines, which are lines of the viewport, do not move. They combine edge by edge, the specific placement beating `.automatic` wherever written, and a scroll view's content does not inherit them — all measured on SwiftUI. The scrollers the framework puts around a tall menu, a sheet's or a framed container's content take none and pass them on, so that content lays out as it would without the scroller, which is there only while it is too tall (an inline menu took the page's margins once it scrolled, inside a width planned without them, and its labels wrapped). So a `ScrollView` or a grouped `Form` inside a `Dialog` or a sheet takes the page's `.contentMargins`, tall or not — a visible change from before a8d3bed7c, when it lost them once the dialog scrolled — as SwiftUI's presented content inherits the presenter's environment. Likewise they take none of the page's other settings for its scroll views — a scroll target behaviour, `.scrollClipDisabled`, `.scrollOverscroll` and `.scrollBounceBehavior` (a page's `.always` pushed a tall menu two lines past its end), `defaultScrollAnchor`, `.anchorPosition`, `.scrollPosition`, and the `onScrollGeometryChange` / `onScrollPhaseChange` observers, which they leave for the app's own scroll views to claim (a page's `.defaultScrollAnchor(.bottom)` opened a tall inline menu at its last item, and its observers heard the menu's scroller when it drew first). A grouped `Form` is padded by them; `List`, `Table`, `TextEditor` and a columns `Form` ignore them, as macOS's do (measured). the soft-deprecated `showsIndicators:` variant is omitted, per the rule above — `scrollIndicators(_:)` is the current spelling; `scrollTo` targets `ForEach` row identities and `.id(_:)` tags on the content stack's own children — not a `.id(_:)` inside a `ForEach` row, whose identity is its element's `id`; nor a `.tag(_:)` inside one, which since 2026-09-20 names that row's `List` SELECTION value rather than an address, identity staying the element's `id` — and any scroll view whose axes include `.vertical`, resolving in O(window) even at millions of rows. On a purely horizontal `ScrollView(.horizontal)` the request names a COLUMN of the `HStack` or `LazyHStack` that is its content, and the anchor's `x` places it (`nil` moving only as far as shows it); a view the stack has no column for — a vertical stack's row — is not sought there. **Ideal size (2026-09-23):** asked with no width, a `ScrollView` answers its content's width plus one column exactly when it will draw a vertical scrollbar (`.automatic` and overflowing, or `.visible`; never `.hidden`/`.never` or the `.text` style, which spends lines, not a column); asked with no height, a horizontally scrolling one likewise adds its bottom bar's row. SwiftUI's overlay scrollers take no space, so its ideal size is the content's alone — here a bar is a column or row OF the viewport, and a parent that sized to the content's size (a `TabView` panel, a lazy stack's row) squeezed the content or clipped the bar |
| Adaptive | `ViewThatFits(in:content:)` | ✓ | |
| Layout | `GeometryReader` / `GeometryProxy` (`frame(in:)` in every space, `bounds(of:)`) / `CoordinateSpace` (`.local`, `.global`, `.named(_:)`) and `CoordinateSpaceProtocol` (`.local`, `.global`, `.named(_:)`, `.scrollView`, `.scrollView(axis:)` — `NamedCoordinateSpace` — the form SwiftUI moved `frame(in:)` to), `coordinateSpace(_:)`; `GeometryProxy.containerCornerInsets` with `RectangleCornerInsets` in cells, always zero because a container's corner is a glyph in its border cell and no chrome overlaps the content), `AlignmentID` / `ViewDimensions` / `HorizontalAlignment(_:)` / `VerticalAlignment(_:)` (and their `combineExplicit(_:)`), `.alignmentGuide(_:computeValue:)` | ✓ | sizes are whole cells → §2.2 (`CellSize`/`CellRect`, no `CGSize`); `GeometryProxy` omits `safeAreaInsets` (§4b); its `Anchor` subscript ships with the anchor preferences (Env / prefs, above). **Coordinate spaces (2026-10-05, decisions A2):** a reader is drawn from where it is, in every space it asks about, on the frame that draws it — in a presented surface and in a window bar's `.toolbar` items too — but for a view no owner can find: in a `Table` cell, or under a container that drops the markers its content drew (below, **Where it differs**). TUIkit renders bottom-up, so a reader is answered while it builds its content with a prediction: the containers that know where they will place a child before drawing it (`VStack`, `HStack`, their lazy forms and windows, `Grid`, a custom `Layout`, a scroll view, `.padding`, a border or box, `.frame`, `.overlay`/`.background` and the preference layers, `ZStack`, `.offset`, `.position`) carry its origin down, and what the others add is taken from where the space's owner — the `coordinateSpace(_:)` view, the nearest scroll view, the scene root for `.global` — found it last frame. The owner finds it again once it has drawn, and when the prediction missed (a reader's first frame under a container the prediction does not follow, or a frame on which such a container moved it) draws its subtree once more in the same frame (decisions A2: G4, rungs 1–3 — so a row inserted above a reader, a lazy row entering the band, a scroll-offset tracker while its scroll view scrolls, and a reader under an animated `.offset` cost nothing: a scroll view places its content at minus its offset, and an offset moves the views under it in every space, as SwiftUI's does). A `.gradientExtent(.subtree)` ramp is placed by the same containers, so content under padding, a border, a frame's alignment, an overlay's or a `ZStack`'s takes the ramp's colour where it is drawn; under `.offset` and `.position` it keeps the colour of where the layout put it. `.global` is the screen, the window's bars included — the space pointer locations are reported in. As measured on SwiftUI (macOS 15): a name nothing around the reader declares — like `.scrollView` outside any scroll view, or `.scrollView(axis:)` with none along that axis — answers in the GLOBAL space, and `bounds(of:)` answers `nil`; `.scrollView` is the scroll view's viewport, which the content moves through (a reader forty rows down a view scrolled forty rows is at `y == 0`), and the innermost scroll view's. `onGeometryChange` whose transform asks another space reports once the frame is done, with where the view was found; a tap or drag located in a named space (`SpatialTapGesture`, `DragGesture`, `onTapGesture(coordinateSpace:)`, `onContinuousHover(coordinateSpace:)`) converts with where the space's owner found the view on the frame the event arrives over. `CellRect` has `minX`/`minY`/`origin`/`size` under `CGRect`'s names (still no `midX`/`midY`, see `AlignmentID`). **Where it differs:** a reader whose prediction missed costs its owner one more drawing of its subtree that frame (the Stress `geometry` scenario counts them); side effects in that subtree — an `onChange`, a task — run for both drawings, as they did under the app header's correction; the scene root's correction, for `.global`, walks the whole scene again. A reader inside a presented surface (a sheet, a popover) asking `.global` is placed by the compositor, which the prediction does not follow, so the frame the surface first shows or moves costs that second walk (decisions D5G B-D5G-3). A lazy stack past a few hundred rows draws a band, and a row's place in a named space around the stack is an estimate there, as the scrollbar's is (`.scrollView` stays exact). A view inside a `Table` cell is located where its row draws it (the table carries a view cell's markers up with its regions), but a cell carries no origin down, so a reader there costs the scene a second walk on its first frame; and a `ScrollView` in a cell does not predict for its own `.scrollView` readers, which it corrects — a second drawing — on every frame they move. Nor is a view under a container that redraws its content's lines into a new buffer without the hit-test regions in them, which carry the markers (a bare `FrameBuffer(lines:)`, in an app's own `Renderable` as in the framework): a reader there is told where the containers above it would place it, and nothing checks it (decisions D5G B-D5G-2). `CoordinateSpace` and `NamedCoordinateSpace` are not `Sendable`, as SwiftUI's are not; nor is `GeometryProxy` `Sendable` or `Equatable`, as SwiftUI's is not (it answers from the frame that made it, so `onChange(of: proxy)` and sending one to a task fail to compile). Guide *values* are `Double` even though sizes are `Int`, because a guide is only ever subtracted from another guide and then floored; see `AlignmentID`. In a stack, each view sits at the largest guide less its own, and the stack grows to fit, for every alignment, as SwiftUI's does (measured 2026-10-11): in `VStack(alignment: .trailing)` a 3-wide view whose trailing guide is 0 hangs past a 6-wide one and leaves it at 0. A guide's value is used as given, inside its view or not: a 1-wide view whose leading guide is 5 indents a 6-wide sibling by 5, one with guide −10 sits 10 to the right of it (measured; 135 placements, `AlignmentGuideParityTests`). Only the push is bounded: one guide moves a view at most 1,048,576 cells (2^20) along (or as far as its region's extent or the widest child, if further), so a `ZStack` in a scroll view can place items by guide down a canvas a million rows long, as SwiftUI's can (measured to 100,000 rows). A push past the bound, and a guide that is not a number on a view with a width and a height, is a logic error: it is reported through `SoftTrap` (an assertion in a debug build, once in a release build) and the view is placed as if its guide were at its own origin; `TUIKIT_MAX_ALIGNMENT_PUSH` raises the bound, up to 2^30 (decisions B-GUIDE-2, deviations L13). SwiftUI places a finite guide at any magnitude without a word, stops on `+∞` or NaN, and places a view whose guide is `-∞` at an infinite x without stopping (measured; TUIkit reports all three). A region bigger than the run (a `.frame`'s, a lazy column's window) moves the whole run toward the region's own guide only as far as all of it still fits. A region that cannot grow and is smaller than the run (a column a flexible child fills, a lazy column's window, the space a stack was given) keeps every guide on one line and draws what hangs past its edge there, as content past its region is (§2.9), off the screen where the edge is the screen's — SwiftUI's stack reports the larger size instead (measured: 403 wide in a 400-wide host; decisions-GUIDE B-GUIDE-1). `ZStack` sizes to its run as a stack does (measured). One child whose guide puts it outside its `.frame`, `.overlay` or `.background` is drawn there, as SwiftUI's is (measured: a 6-wide view with leading guide 5 in `.frame(width: 20, alignment: .leading)` is at −5, and a badge hung off a label's top-trailing corner by its guides sits a row above it and a column right, not on its last letter): the part the region holds in it, the rest past it, at the view's place in the stacking order — over the views before it, under the views after it, clicked where it is drawn (§2.9; decisions-GUIDE B-GUIDE-3). A cell has no transparency, so what it draws past the region hides what is beneath completely. A guide travels up as SwiftUI's does through `VStack`, `HStack`, `ZStack`, `.padding` and a custom `Layout` (Custom layout, below), each moving it by where it put its content and merging several by `combineExplicit(_:)` (measured on SwiftUI, including `.padding(0)`): `.alignmentGuide(.decimalPoint)` on a `Text` inside each row of a `VStack(alignment: .decimalPoint)` aligns the rows on the point. **Gap:** it stops at any other modifier written after it (`.border()`, `.frame`, `.offset`) and at a view type of the app's own — a guide inside `struct Row: View`'s body does not travel out of `Row`, because the column cannot know to ask it without evaluating every such body; put the guide on the view the stack places. Unlike SwiftUI's, whose lazy stacks read no explicit guide (measured), TUIkit's lazy stacks honour a row's guide, a deliberate deviation (owner, 2026-10-12; decisions A-GUIDE-1): a lazy row type that carries one turns off the uniform and anchored windows, so every row is measured every frame. A lazy stack reports none of its rows' guides. A container's run is decided on the values its children give for its own alignment, so a guide for another alignment changes nothing |
| Geometry vocabulary | `Edge` / `HorizontalEdge` / `VerticalEdge`, `HorizontalDirection` / `VerticalDirection` (each with its `.Set`), `Axis` / `Axis.Set` | ✓ | raw values, set bits, `Codable` encodings and `Axis.description` are SwiftUI's, read off a SwiftUI build, so a value persisted by one reads back in the other. Leading is left: TUIkit lays out left to right |
| Grid | `Grid(alignment:horizontalSpacing:verticalSpacing:)`, `GridRow(alignment:)`, `.gridCellColumns(_:)`, `.gridCellAnchor(_:)`, `.gridColumnAlignment(_:)`, `.gridCellUnsizedAxes(_:)`, `GridLayout(alignment:horizontalSpacing:verticalSpacing:)` (+ `Cache`, `AnimatableData`) | ✓ | `GridLayout` is the same lattice as a `Layout`, so `AnyLayout(isWide ? GridLayout() : VStackLayout())` keeps every cell's identity across the switch, the cells lining up under the grid and stacking under the stack. Spacings are `Int?` cells, and `nil`, the default, is **1 column / 0 rows** rather than SwiftUI's platform metrics — a zero column gap would run adjacent text together, while rows are already separated by being different lines. A non-`GridRow` child spans every column (which is how a `Divider` between rows works). A `GridRow` is not a container: as in SwiftUI it hands its cells to whatever lays them out — `VStack { GridRow { a; b } }` is `VStack { a; b }`, a custom `Layout` over two rows of two sees four subviews — and a `ForEach` of rows is cells too, in a grid, a stack or an app's own `Layout` alike (`GridTests`). A cell marked `.gridCellUnsizedAxes(_:)` adds nothing to its column's width or its row's height on those axes, and is drawn at the extent the other cells decide — for a fill or a rule inside a row; a child in no row already spans the grid without sizing a column |
| Lazy grids | `LazyVGrid(columns:alignment:spacing:pinnedViews:content:)`, `LazyHGrid(rows:alignment:spacing:pinnedViews:content:)`, `GridItem` | ✓ | rows and columns in cells; `spacing:` is `Int?`, and `nil`, the default, is no blank rows between a `LazyVGrid`'s rows, as `Grid`'s vertical spacing, and one blank column between a `LazyHGrid`'s columns, as an `HStack`'s; a `GridItem`'s `nil` spacing is likewise one column between a `LazyVGrid`'s columns and no row between a `LazyHGrid`'s rows (the gaps were the other way round in a `LazyHGrid` until 2026-10-14); every subview is measured. A `Section` in a `LazyVGrid` is laid out as SwiftUI's: its header starts a row and spans the columns, aligned by the grid's `alignment` (measured), its views flow from the row below and its footer spans after them, and with `pinnedViews:` they pin as a `LazyVStack`'s do → §2.8. A sectioned grid fills the width it is offered, and its `spacing` falls between a header and its first row as between any two rows, as SwiftUI's does. A `LazyHGrid` lays its sections out on their side — the header down the rows at the start of a new column, the views after it, the footer after them — and with `pinnedViews:` pins them to a horizontal scroll view's leading and trailing edges (A44) |

It degrades with the terminal: the full trail, then the middle elided (`Planets › … › Deimos`), then **‹ Back** + the truncated title when even that will not fit. TUI-specific: SwiftUI has no breadcrumb, but a terminal has no swipe-back gesture either, and one Back button at depth 4 is four presses and no idea where you are. Back is also **Escape** or `\.dismiss` (which pops inside a stack instead of quitting) — after the screen's own `.onKeyEvent`, which hears Escape first, so a screen that must not be left can consume it. A sheet or alert presented from a pushed screen takes Escape to dismiss itself instead, and the status bar says dismiss rather than go back. The root keeps rendering while a screen is pushed — isolated from focus and key events like a modal's backdrop, and off-screen — which is what keeps its `@State` alive to come back to and its `navigationDestination` closures current. **Each depth is its own focus section**, so coming back lands on the control you left rather than on whichever registered first: pushing remembers, popping restores, exactly as a dismissed modal does. `NavigationPath` has no `CodableRepresentation` (§4b-adjacent: it needs a runtime type-name lookup TUIkit does not have everywhere). `NavigationLink(destination:)` pushes a token keyed on the link's identity, so it needs a path that can hold one — the default stack or a `NavigationPath`; a *typed* path binding can only carry its own element type, so use `NavigationLink(value:)` with those. **The binding-driven destinations ship** — `navigationDestination(isPresented:)` for a screen with no value behind it, and `(item:)` for one that is about something. The binding is genuinely two-way: clearing it pops, and popping (Back, Escape, a crumb) clears it. A second focus stop per branch would have taken the row's Space with it. One cell is a poor mouse target, so the triangle's button covers the blank cell either side of it as well. It emits one row per **visible** node to whatever contains it, exactly as `ForEach` emits one per element: the depth is each row's leading padding, not a nest of views, so a closed branch's descendants are neither built nor measured and a deep tree is not a deep view hierarchy. Two consequences worth knowing. A refusing row opts out of the row value-memo — otherwise it would be reported on the frame it appeared and forgotten on every frame after, and the row would quietly become deletable again — while a row refusing NOTHING reports nothing, declares nothing and memoizes exactly as before, which is the case most rows in such a list evaluate to. And only rows that were drawn can report, which is sufficient because only drawn rows can be edited: Delete acts on the focused row and a drag on the grabbed one. `Table` has no counterpart, as in SwiftUI — its rows are values, with no view to write a modifier on. **`ForEach($items, editActions:)`, `List($items, editActions:rowContent:)` (and its `id:` and `selection:` forms) and `EditActions` ship** — the `ForEach` performing the edits on its own binding instead of you writing the two closures that would have contained exactly `move(fromOffsets:toOffset:)` and `remove(atOffsets:)`, and the `List` forms being that `ForEach` inside a data-driven list. The loop's data is an `IndexedIdentifierCollection`, as SwiftUI's is: the collection's positions, each with its element's identity, read lazily, so a loop over ten thousand elements builds nothing up front. `EditActions` is generic over the collection as SwiftUI's is, so `.move` needs a `MutableCollection` and `.delete` a `RangeReplaceableCollection` — the cases are constrained separately, which is what stops `.delete` being offered for a collection that cannot shrink. An action NOT in the set is not merely inert: the `List` is never told the rows can move or be deleted, so no drag-reorder gesture arms and no delete key is claimed. **One deviation:** the collection must be `Int`-indexed. That is not a choice about editing — it is the constraint TUIkit's own `move(fromOffsets:)`/`remove(atOffsets:)` already carry so they out-specialise SwiftUI's overlay versions of those names on Apple platforms; without it this compiles and then fails to LINK against SwiftUI symbols TUIkit does not link |
| Presentation | `sheet(isPresented:onDismiss:content:)`, `sheet(item:onDismiss:content:)`, `alert` (Boolean, `presenting:` and `error:` forms), `confirmationDialog` (Boolean and `presenting:` forms), `dialogSeverity(_:)` (`DialogSeverity`), `dialogIcon(_:)`, `dialogSuppressionToggle` (all four labels), `dialogPreventsAppTermination(_:)`, `presentationPreventsAppTermination(_:)`, `dismissalConfirmationDialog` (with and without `message:`), `presentationBackground(_:)` and `presentationBackground(alignment:content:)`, `presentationCornerRadius(_:)` (a sheet's outermost box takes the corners: zero square, any other radius rounded, `nil` the appearance's — light lines only; decisions W1, B-W1-14), `presentationDragIndicator(_:)` (a detented sheet's grabber; see the `presentationDetents` row), `presentationBackgroundInteraction(_:)` (`.automatic`, `.disabled`, `.enabled`, `.enabled(upThrough:)`; a live page beside a docked sheet — see the `presentationDetents` row), `presentationContentInteraction(_:)` (`.automatic`, `.scrolls`, `.resizes`; what the wheel does over a detented sheet's grabber — see the `presentationDetents` row) | ✓ | presented as a centred, dimming overlay; a `sheet` in a vertically compact size class, which TUIkit's is only where the app writes one, is a full-screen cover, as SwiftUI documents for an iPhone in landscape, unless its content's `presentationCompactAdaptation` says `.none` or `.sheet` (decisions A21). An `alert`/`confirmationDialog` dismisses when any of its actions is chosen (SwiftUI's behaviour — the action closure does not flip the binding itself); an alert with no actions has a standard OK and a confirmation dialog with no `.cancel`-role button a standard Cancel, as SwiftUI documents both; a dialog finds its buttons wherever the actions builder wrote them — in an `if`/`else`, a `Group`, a `ForEach`, a view of the app's own, behind modifiers (which the dialog's own button drawing does not keep) — and leaves out what is not a button; and claims Escape on the status bar while it is up, so a page's own `⎋ back` cannot navigate out from under it. Escape *is* the `.cancel`-role button (macOS gives Cancel the Escape key equivalent): it runs that action if there is one — a disabled one is skipped — and closes the dialog either way. A `sheet`/`modal` deliberately does NOT auto-dismiss when one of its buttons is pressed: its content owns its own close button. That button reaches for `@Environment(\.dismiss)`, which **means the presentation** — every presentation modifier (`sheet`, `modal`, `fullScreenCover`, `popover`, `alert`, `confirmationDialog`) publishes a `DismissAction` that closes it, the same act as its Escape route, so `onDismiss` fires either way. `DismissAction` is `Equatable` (a TUIkit addition: SwiftUI's is not), by identity: an action is equal to itself, so a view that writes one into its content's environment from a box it keeps (`@State`) leaves the memos below on (decisions B37). Previously none of them did, and the presented content inherited the top-level meaning — quitting the app — so a `Button("Done") { dismiss() }` in a sheet terminated the program. **The data-driven `alert` spellings ship too** — `alert(_:isPresented:presenting:actions:)` (with and without `message:`) and `alert(isPresented:error:actions:)` (likewise). What `presenting:` adds over closing over the value is the CONDITION: the alert's buttons act on something, that something is usually the same state its presence is derived from, and taking it as a parameter means `nil` withholds the alert instead of drawing one about nothing. The `error:` forms take the title from `errorDescription` and the message from `recoverySuggestion` — which is what those properties are for — and an error with no suggestion gets no message line rather than a blank one. SwiftUI's asymmetry is kept verbatim: `alert(isPresented:error:actions:)` hands the error to `message` but not to `actions`, while the two-builder form hands it to both. `confirmationDialog` takes `presenting:` on the same terms, `titleVisibility` included. **The `item:` forms key the presented content on the item's id** — `sheet(item:)`, `fullScreenCover(item:)` and `popover(item:)` alike — so changing the item makes the content a different view (fresh `@State`, lifecycle re-fired) rather than the same view handed a new value, which is what `item:` means in SwiftUI and the only thing it offers over presenting on a flag. It is the same `.keyed` identity step `ForEach` gives its rows, for the same reason; the `isPresented:` forms keep their positional slot, since nothing about them varies. **The dialog modifiers are written outside the presenting modifier, as SwiftUI's are, and reach every alert and confirmation dialog presented inside.** A `.critical` severity — macOS's large caution symbol — starts the dialog's title with the ⚠️ emoji (U+26A0 U+FE0F, two cells, compensated on the hosts that advance it one — `Terminal compatibility survey.md`) and draws its border and title in the palette's warning colour, unless the alert was given colours of its own; `.automatic` is critical for the `error:` forms and standard otherwise, as SwiftUI documents. A `dialogIcon` sits at the leading edge of the message: an SF Symbol as its glyph, an image fitted into eight columns by four rows (about square in two-to-one cells); `nil`, SwiftUI's "the app icon", is no icon, a terminal app having none. A `dialogSuppressionToggle` draws a checkbox above the dialog's buttons, bound to the app's flag and titled "Do not show this message again" (localized) unless given a title; as on macOS nothing is suppressed for the app, which decides from the flag whether to present next time. The checkbox comes after the buttons in the focus ring, so the first button keeps the focus the dialog opens with. **Preventing termination** stops the quit key (and takes it off the status bar) while the dialog, or the sheet, cover or popover whose content says so, is drawn; a signal — Ctrl-C, `kill` — still ends the app, as a force quit does on macOS. `nil`, the automatic behaviour, is macOS's rule for a dialog: it prevents quitting unless it has a single button that is not destructive (no actions counting as SwiftUI's one OK button), is not critical (an `error:` alert under `.automatic` severity is), and holds no text field (a TUIkit dialog holds none) — so a one-button notice leaves the quit key working and a question holds it. For a sheet, cover or popover `nil` prevents nothing: macOS's rule reads the sheet's toolbar, which a terminal sheet has none of, and would otherwise take the quit key away behind every sheet (decisions B36). A **`dismissalConfirmationDialog`** on a sheet's, cover's or popover's content puts its question in the way of that presentation's own ways out — Escape, an outside click, the status-bar item — while `shouldPresent` holds, and on an app's top-level content in the way of the quit key (macOS's window close, in a terminal app). Cancel (the standard one the dialog adds when the app gave none, which Escape chooses) keeps the presentation; any other answer runs and then lets the dismissal go ahead. Where a sheet's, cover's or popover's content draws a cancel button — `.keyboardShortcut(.cancelAction)`, as a `.cancellationAction` toolbar item's is — Escape presses it, as macOS does, in place of the presentation's own dismissal: its action is the app's, and dismisses or not as it says, and the status bar names the key `⎋ cancel` for it rather than `⎋ dismiss` (a popover's `⎋ close popover`); a held Escape's repeats press it again only if the button repeats (`.buttonRepeatBehavior(.enabled)`), as its own shortcut would — except while a `dismissalConfirmationDialog` would ask, when Escape asks first and the button waits, since its action would run before its `dismiss()` asked. An app's own `dismiss()` asks too, as SwiftUI's does: the dismissal confirmation writes its content's `\.dismiss`, from a box it keeps, so the memos inside stay on (B37). A **`presentationBackground`** on a presentation's content fills the whole presentation — a sheet's box at its detent's height, a cover's whole content area, a popover's panel in place of the page's background — and is found through the other presentation traits in either order. A translucent style composites over the page beneath (macOS draws a sheet's background opaque). `presentationBackgroundInteraction(_:)`'s `.automatic` and `.disabled` leave the page behind inert; `.enabled` and `.enabled(upThrough:)` leave it live beside a detented sheet docked under it (the `presentationDetents` row; decisions A19). `presentationContentInteraction(_:)` decides what the wheel — a terminal's swipe — does over a detented sheet with a grabber: `.automatic` leaves it the content's (SwiftUI's iOS default grows the sheet first; a terminal app's wheel scrolls), `.scrolls` moves the sheet a detent where the content can scroll no further, and `.resizes` grows the sheet before the content scrolls, one detent at most every quarter of a second. |
| Help | `help(_:)` in SwiftUI's three forms: a `LocalizedStringKey`, a `Text`, a `StringProtocol` | ✓ | On **any** view, focusable or not. A literal is a localization key, a computed `String` is shown as written, as SwiftUI's disfavoured `StringProtocol` overload makes them. It shows once the pointer has rested on the view for `tooltipDelay(_:)` (0.6 s by default), and, for the control holding the focus, when the reader presses the help key `?` (`TooltipState.helpKey`), the keyboard's stand-in for the pointer; inside a text field `?` is typed, so there hover is the route. A terminal has no single tooltip surface, so where and when are the app's choice through TUI-only modifiers: `tooltipStyle(_:)` draws the help as a row of the status bar (the default) or as a popover beside the view, and `tooltips(_:)` shows it `.automatic`ally (hover or `?`), also whenever a control takes the focus (`.onFocus`), for every view at once (`.always`, the app-wide help: each panel beside its own view, none over another (a presentation's among themselves), in reading order, the hovered and the focused view's placed first, and one with no room left out), or `.never`. A `Text`'s colour and weight have nowhere to land and only its string is used. Under the status-bar style the row a hover tooltip takes can move a page laid out in the middle of the window out from under the pointer; the tooltip stays until the pointer moves (decisions-HELP A-HELP-1). See [Tooltips and help in a terminal](<../Controls and presentation/Tooltips and help in a terminal.md>). |
| Search | `searchable(text:placement:prompt:)` and `searchable(text:isPresented:placement:prompt:)`, `searchSuggestions(_:)`, `searchCompletion(_:)` (a string or a token), `\.isSearching`, `\.dismissSearch`, `searchFocused(_:)` and `searchFocused(_:equals:)`, `searchSelection(_:)`, `searchScopes(_:scopes:)` and `searchScopes(_:activation:_:)` (`SearchScopeActivation`), `searchToolbarBehavior(_:)` (`SearchToolbarBehavior`), `searchPresentationToolbarBehavior(_:)` (`SearchPresentationToolbarBehavior`), `SearchFieldPlacement.toolbarPrincipal`, the `tokens:`, `suggestedTokens:` and `editableTokens:` forms of `searchable` (with and without `isPresented:`) | ✓ | The field is drawn at the top of the searchable subtree whatever placement is preferred (`.toolbarPrincipal` included) — SwiftUI documents the placement as a preference — unless a `DefaultToolbarItem(kind: .search)` places it in a bar, and nowhere under `.toolbar(removing: .search)` (§2.6). **Searching is the field holding the focus**: `isSearching`, and `isPresented` with it, are `true` while it does. Setting `isPresented` to `true` moves the focus to the field, and `false` ends the search as `dismissSearch` does (the query emptied, the focus moved on); both act just after the frame, since the focus move needs a complete focus ring. Tabbing out of the field is not cancelling the search: the query stays. `searchFocused` is written after the `searchable`, as in SwiftUI, and binds that searchable's field, not one in its content; `searchSelection` is written and binds the same way, from macOS 15 (SwiftUI's is macOS 26's), as a `TextField`'s `selection:` does. **Scopes** are a segmented picker under the field. The field is always on screen here, so "presented" means it holds the focus and "cancelled" means `dismissSearch` or leaving an empty field: under `.onSearchPresentation` (macOS's `.automatic`) they show while the field has the focus or the query is not empty, under `.onTextEntry` while the query is not empty, and under both while the focus is in them, so Tab from the field reaches them. The two toolbar behaviors return the view unchanged and are honoured, not stubbed: `.automatic`, the only search toolbar behavior macOS has, is how the field behaves, and presenting search hides nothing around it, which is what `.avoidHidingContent` asks (tested as that guarantee). **Tokens** draw in the field before the text, each as the app's `token` builder makes it, in brackets in the accent colour (`[Apple] [Pear] app`); Backspace in an empty field removes the last, as on macOS. **Suggested tokens are entries in the field's suggestions menu**, the one `searchSuggestions` fills: while nothing is typed they come first, as a group of their own above any `searchSuggestions`, each in brackets (`[Fig]`) so a row that adds a token reads differently from one that completes the text. The menu opens and is walked as for text suggestions (Down at the caret, or the `▾`), and choosing a token appends it to `tokens` and submits, as choosing a text suggestion does; one already chosen is not offered again. `searchCompletion(_:)` given a token puts the same kind of entry among the `searchSuggestions`: choosing it empties the field and adds the token (SwiftUI's "replace the partial text"), a token already chosen is ticked and not added twice, and the entry is left out where the searchable takes no tokens or tokens of another type. An editable token's view is handed a binding to its token, found by id, and is a focus stop of its own before the field. The `LocalizedStringResource` prompts, here as everywhere, are absent. |
| Lifecycle | `onAppear`, `onDisappear`, `task` (incl. `task(id:)`, and `task(id:name:executorPreference:priority:file:line:_:)`, whose nonisolated work runs on the preferred executor and whose task is named where the runtime names tasks, macOS 26 and Swift 6.2's elsewhere), `onChange(of:initial:_:)` (both current forms), `onHover`, `onContinuousHover(coordinateSpace:perform:)` + `HoverPhase`, `onInteractiveResizeChange(_:)` | ✓ | matches the *current* `onChange`; deprecated `perform:` correctly absent. A terminal reports each new size and never a drag, so `onInteractiveResizeChange` reads a burst of resizes as one: `true` at the first frame drawn at a new size after a quiet period, `false` once the size has held for `\.interactiveResizeTimeout` (200 ms; §4c). A font-size change or a tmux pane resize is a burst too; a size report that leaves the grid as it was is not. `HoverPhase.active` carries a `CellPoint` where SwiftUI's carries a `CGPoint` (§2.2), in the view's own cells (`.local`) or the terminal's (`.global`); `coordinateSpace:` takes `CoordinateSpace`, which has no `.named` here, so a named space fails to compile. A move is the pointer reaching another cell, which is all a terminal reports.
