Nested `onHover`s each hear the pointer enter and leave their own region, as in SwiftUI, outermost first in and innermost first out, and the control inside keeps its own hover (semantic audit item 31; until 2026-10-18 only the outermost heard it); a region in front that takes the hover — a sheet's backdrop, an open menu — still hides the ones behind it. |
| Key presses | `onKeyPress` — all five: `(_:action:)`, `(_:phases:action:)`, `(keys:phases:action:)`, `(characters:phases:action:)`, `(phases:action:)` — and `KeyPress` (`key`, `characters`, `modifiers`, `phase`), `KeyPress.Result`, `KeyPress.Phases` (`.down`, `.repeat`, `.up`, `.all`) | ✓ | SwiftUI's semantics, measured on macOS 15: a handler hears a press only while the focus is on its view or inside it, the handlers on the focus path are asked outermost first, and they are asked before the focused control (a `TextField` does not type a handled letter, a handled Tab does not move the focus) and before `.keyboardShortcut`s. Phases are what the terminal reports: where it speaks the kitty keyboard protocol, which TUIkit turns on wherever the terminal answers its query, `.down`, `.repeat` and `.up` are SwiftUI's; in the legacy encodings every press is `.down` (a held key's repeats arrive as more presses, so a `.down` or `.repeat` handler hears them all) and an `.up` handler never runs, because a release is never sent — `\.keyboardReporting` says which, so an app can offer a fallback (`Terminal compatibility survey.md`, "The kitty keyboard protocol"). `modifiers` is Control, Option and Shift, Shift on a typed character being its case on every terminal (`A` has it, `!` has not, where macOS reports Shift on `!`); a key the terminal composed arrives composed (Option-A as "å" without Option-as-Meta). The focus-blind terminal handler is the TUI-only `onKeyEvent` (§4c). |
| Undo | `\.undoManager` (`UndoManager?`, read-only as SwiftUI's) | ✓ | One per app, made by the run loop, `nil` outside a running app as SwiftUI's is "in a context that doesn't support undo". Each event's registrations are one step, as `UndoManager` groups them on macOS. A terminal has no Edit menu and no ⌘Z, so the keys are TUIkit's, and data (`UndoCommand.defaultShortcuts`): undo on Ctrl-Z, Ctrl-minus and Ctrl-/ (and Ctrl-_ and Ctrl-Shift-Z in a legacy encoding, which most terminals send as Ctrl-minus's and Ctrl-Z's bytes), redo on Ctrl-Shift-Z and Ctrl-_ where the kitty keyboard protocol tells them apart and on Option-_ where the terminal sends Option as Meta; an app's own shortcut on the chord wins, and the TUI-only scene modifier `.undoShortcuts(_:)` replaces either list app-wide — taking Ctrl-Z out of undo makes it the shell's suspend key again. A bound key is its command's with or without a step, so Ctrl-Z with nothing to undo does nothing. `TextField` and `TextEditor` register their own edits, a run of typing as one step as the macOS text system makes it (characters, spaces, newlines and tabs — Option-Return's, Ctrl-O's and Option-Tab's among them — and Backspace at the caret the last left, ended by a caret moved elsewhere, any other edit, or a step registered in the app's undo manager since the run's own — another control's typing, an action of the app's — from macOS 15; measured on macOS 15.8, decisions-D1K B-D1K-6), so undo means something by default, and a focused one's Ctrl-Z (⌘Z's stand-in) and Ctrl-Shift-Z are the app's undo and redo; a control's steps leave the history when it leaves the screen (and one its own history lets go of, past its 50, leaves with it), and a `SecureField` registers none. The status bar shows "undo" and "redo" while each has a step, naming the first of its keys the terminal can deliver, and a click performs it — the route on every terminal (`StatusBarState.showUndoItems`). Foundation on Linux has no `UndoManager`, so TUIkit provides one there with Foundation's Swift surface, less the selector and invocation registrations and the notifications (decisions A1; `Terminal compatibility survey.md`, "Undo and redo") |
| Modifier keys | `onModifierKeysChanged(mask:initial:_:)`, `modifierKeyAlternate(_:_:)` | ✓ where reported | An alternate is drawn in place of the modified view while all its keys are held, the one on the most held keys prevailing among several in a chain; the modified view keeps its `@State` and its lifecycle meanwhile (it is walked unseen: no `onDisappear`, no `.task` restart), and a focus it held comes back to it as the keys are released unless the user moved it elsewhere, a Tab in the same burst of input included; in a menu it replaces the row while held, and takes no shortcut of its own (SwiftUI's menus give an alternate item the modified view's shortcut plus its modifiers — decisions-D1K B-D1K-2). `onModifierKeysChanged` Runs its action with the old and new state, both masked, on every change of the held modifier keys — reported between frames, as a key is, so a change no frame sees still reaches it — and once with the held keys when the view appears if `initial` is `true` and any are held. Shift, Control, Option, Caps Lock, and ⌘ where the terminal passes it as the kitty protocol's Super key; never `.numericPad`. A terminal reports a modifier key on its own only under the kitty keyboard protocol's "report all keys as escape codes" enhancement, which TUIkit turns on where the terminal answers its query; elsewhere the action never runs and the alternate is never drawn, and `\.keyboardReporting.reportsModifierKeys` is `false` so an app can offer another route (decisions B7, A4). |
| Commands | `onMoveCommand(perform:)` + `MoveCommandDirection`, `onExitCommand(perform:)`, `onDeleteCommand(perform:)`; `copyable(_:)`, `cuttable(for:action:)`, `pasteDestination(for:action:validator:)` and `PasteButton(payloadType:onPaste:)` everywhere — with no `Transferable` requirement, as `draggable(_:)` has none, since CoreTransferable is Apple-only and a terminal's clipboard holds text: a copy writes its values' text (a string, or a description) and the app keeps the values, so a paste here gets them back, while text copied elsewhere pastes as a `String` or a `LosslessStringConvertible` value read from it; `PasteButton` is titled from the framework's tables and always enabled, since watching a terminal's clipboard means running a program; on Apple platforms `onCopyCommand(perform:)`, `onCutCommand(perform:)`, `onPasteCommand(of:perform:)` and `onPasteCommand(of:validator:perform:)` | ✓ | SwiftUI's semantics, measured on macOS 15: heard while the focus is on the view or inside it, after the focused control lets the key go (a `List` keeps its arrows, a `TextField` its Backspace) and before the keyboard shortcuts (an exit command takes Escape from the cancel button); the innermost handler runs and consumes the key, a `nil` action included. Any arrow is a move whatever is held; Escape, and Backspace or forward Delete, count alone or with Shift. A move command comes before the TUI-only arrow-key focus navigation. ⌘. is not an exit command here: a terminal cannot send it. The focus-blind handlers ahead of the focused control (`onKeyEvent`, `NavigationStack`'s Escape-to-pop) take a key before a command does, unless a text field holds the focus. Copy, cut and paste are SwiftUI's ⌘C, ⌘X and ⌘V resolved through `\.commandKey` (Control by default), and a paste is also the terminal's own bracketed paste; only text crosses, since a terminal's clipboard holds a string — a copy writes the items that read as strings, one per line, and a paste offers the clipboard's text and triggers only for a type that accepts text. They take `NSItemProvider` and `UTType`, which Linux does not have, so there they are absent (`#if canImport(UniformTypeIdentifiers)`). |
| Gestures | the `Gesture` protocol (a gesture of your own composes others in its `body`), `TapGesture(count:)`, `SpatialTapGesture(count:coordinateSpace:)`, `LongPressGesture(minimumDuration:maximumDistance:)`, `DragGesture(minimumDistance:coordinateSpace:)` and its `Value` (`location`, `startLocation`, `translation`, `velocity`, `predictedEndLocation`, `predictedEndTranslation`, `time`); `onEnded`, `onChanged`, `map`, `modifiers(_:)`; `updating(_:body:)` with `@GestureState` (all eight initialisers) and `GestureStateGesture`; `simultaneously(with:)`, `sequenced(before:)` and `exclusively(before:)`, with `SimultaneousGesture`, `SequenceGesture` and `ExclusiveGesture`; `AnyGesture`, and an optional gesture; `gesture(_:including:)`, `highPriorityGesture(_:including:)` and `simultaneousGesture(_:including:)`, each also in its `isEnabled:` and `name:isEnabled:` forms, with `GestureMask`; `onTapGesture(count:perform:)` (count defaulting to one) and `onTapGesture(count:coordinateSpace:perform:)`; `onLongPressGesture(minimumDuration:maximumDistance:perform:onPressingChanged:)` | ✓ | the left mouse button, as measured on macOS 15.8 (decisions W17g). Geometry is in cells (§2.2): a location is a `CellPoint`, a translation a `CellSize`, a velocity a `CellVector` in cells per second estimated from the times the motion reports arrive; `minimumDistance` and `maximumDistance` count cells along either axis, one by default where SwiftUI counts ten points (§2.1). **Recognition:** a tap ends on the release, which has to land on the view within five columns and two rows of the press (macOS allows 40 points) after a press of at most 0.75 s, a multi-tap's next click released within 0.35 s of the release before it (a press that comes in time but is held past that gives the tap up, as on macOS); a spatial tap reports the release's cell and, as on macOS, nothing before its end. A long press is `true` from the press — `onChanged` hears it then — and ends at its minimum duration with the button still down, on a frame the run loop wakes for; with no minimum duration it ends at the press, every press. A drag starts once it is `minimumDistance` from the press, keeps the press as its start, and ends only if it started. `modifiers(_:)` reads Shift, Option and Control from the SGR bits at the press (what each terminal forwards is in `Terminal compatibility survey.md`), and more keys than it asks for satisfy it; Command is never reported. `@GestureState` follows the gesture through `updating` and resets when the gesture ends, fails or is cancelled, before `onEnded` runs. **Composition:** a sequence's second gesture starts with the event that ended the first — a tap's release, a zero-duration long press's press — so a tap before a tap ends at one click, or, after a first gesture that ended on its clock, with the next event; a release starts it as a press already over, of which it reports only how it ends, never to `onChanged`, so a tap before a drag or a long press fails at the tap's release and never ends — holding nothing back, so inside a tap every click runs both taps — while a tap before a zero-duration long press ends there, `false`, and one before a zero-distance drag ends there too, at every click's release (all measured); a second gesture that is itself a sequence hands its own second that press, still over, so after a tap two zero-duration long presses in sequence both end `false` (measured); an exclusive gesture holds its second back until the first fails, and cancels it if the first succeeds; a simultaneous gesture reports `nil` for a part that failed and ends once neither part is undecided. An `AnyGesture` whose gesture changes type starts afresh; a `nil` gesture recognizes nothing. **Arbitration:** a view's own gestures — a subview's, and a control's own press, a `Button`'s included — come before one attached with `gesture`, `onTapGesture` or `onLongPressGesture`, which gets the pointer only if they fail; `highPriorityGesture` comes before them, and `simultaneousGesture` runs beside them with its callbacks first. A gesture still undecided holds up the ones after it: a double tap inside a single tap delays the single tap by the double-tap interval, so a double-click runs only the double tap; and a gesture held up keeps what it recognized meanwhile, so when a second click gives the double tap up (held too long, or strayed) the single tap runs once, for the first click, and the second click runs nothing. A press elsewhere does not give a waiting gesture up, undecided (a double tap waiting for its next click) or recognized and not over (a tap and a double tap together, clicked once; a sequence waiting for its second gesture): it waits out its interval, a sequence until its second gesture ends it, still holding up the gestures behind it, while the new press's own gestures run; a gesture held up by the gestures of both presses runs once neither holds it up, so a single tap around two double taps, a click on each, runs once, for the first click, when the second double tap gives up; a gesture under both presses carries on where it is, and one that finished, ended or failed, stays finished — a simultaneous tap that ran at the first click of a double click does not run at the second — until the ranked gestures have all decided. A gesture whose view leaves the tree hears nothing more and runs nothing, still holding up the gestures behind it, until the next left-button event — a press anywhere, or the move or release of a drag under way — which is decided as if the gesture were still there and then gives it up, not at a frame, so what a click does never depends on whether a frame came first: a row removed while its double tap waits runs no single tap, and a tap around the row runs the row's click at the next press, wherever it lands — beside the row, inside that tap, the press starts nothing of its own; on a button or another view's tap it runs that at its release; a sequence removed or disabled while it waits for its second tap still keeps the tap around it, which it cancelled, off the next click beside it, but not off the click after, nor off a click on another view; a drag removed or disabled mid-drag reports neither its next move nor its release (all measured on macOS 15.8). A disabled view's gestures are given up at the disable instead, as on macOS: a `@GestureState` resets then, and a row disabled while its double tap waits runs no single tap, but the tap around it runs the row's click then, on the frame after the one that disabled it, and the next click runs as usual; a gesture that had won its press (a sequence waiting for its second gesture, a drag under way) still holds the next left-button event, as above (all measured; decisions B-F2-2). The masks turn the gesture or its subviews' gestures off; a control ranked after a gesture is given the press only once the gesture fails, and cannot itself wait (decisions B89). `onTapGesture` is a `TapGesture` (given the location, a `SpatialTapGesture`) attached with `gesture`; until 2026-10 it took the press outside-first and tapped wherever the release landed. `onLongPressGesture` is a `LongPressGesture` attached the same way; its `onPressingChanged` hears `true` on the press and `false` when the press is over — just before the action when it succeeds, macOS's order. **No keyboard, nothing when disabled:** a gesture is the pointer's API, on macOS too — a control keeps its own keyboard route (a `Button`'s Return), and a gesture on a plain view needs one of the app's. Nothing hears anything inside `.disabled(true)`, nor do `onMouseEvent` or `onScrollGesture`; attached outside the disabled scope a gesture still fires, as in SwiftUI. Declined: `MagnifyGesture` and `RotateGesture` (a terminal reports one pointer, no pinch or twist), `SpatialEventGesture`, `WindowDragGesture`, and `gesture(_:)` taking an `NSGestureRecognizerRepresentable`. |
| Drag and drop | `draggable(_:)` (+ `preview:`), `dropDestination(for:isEnabled:action:)`, `DropInfo.location`; on Apple platforms `onDrag(_:)` (+ `preview:`), `itemProvider(_:)` (a `nil` provider starts no drag), `onDrop(of:isTargeted:perform:)` (both closures), `onDrop(of:delegate:)` + `DropDelegate`, `DropInfo.hasItemsConforming(to:)` / `itemProviders(for:)`; `DropOperation` (+ `.Set`) and `DropProposal` | ✓ | within the app: a terminal has no system pasteboard to drag through, so a payload needs no `Transferable` and never leaves. The action is handed a `DropInfo` where SwiftUI's is handed a `DropSession`, which TUIkit has as a value and hands out nowhere yet (decisions A53); `location` is on both, as a `CellPoint` (§2.2), and a `DropInfo` also carries the modifiers held at release. While `isEnabled` is false the view is no destination and a drag over it goes home. TUIkit's own `dropDestination(for:action:isTargeted:)`, whose action answers whether it took the drop, stays beside it. The item-provider forms take a drag whose providers conform to their types; a `draggable` string or URL counts, wrapped as SwiftUI wraps a `Transferable`. A `DropDelegate` is called in SwiftUI's order (validate once, enter, update on each move, then exit or perform); a proposal of `.cancel` or `.forbidden` refuses the drop and the drag goes home, while the other operations accept it and differ only in the badge the drag's preview wears in its top-right cell while it is over the view, as macOS's pointer wears one: `+` for `.copy` (what a view without a delegate proposes), `↗` for `.alias`, `⊘` for `.forbidden`, none for `.move`, `.delete` or `.cancel` (decisions B10; `↗` is East Asian Ambiguous, measured one cell in Terminal compatibility survey.md). A drag never leaves the app. `dropDestination` and a list's own drops propose no operation and show no badge. They take `NSItemProvider` and `UTType`, which Linux does not have, so there they are absent. |
| Spring loading | `SpringLoadingBehavior` (`.automatic`, `.enabled`, `.disabled`), `View.springLoadingBehavior(_:)`, `TabContent.springLoadingBehavior(_:)`, `\.springLoadingBehavior` | ✓ | **(2026-10-04)** while a drag carrying a payload is in flight, holding the pointer still on a spring-loaded control for half a second — macOS's default spring-loading delay, `com.apple.springing.delay` — activates it as a click would, and the drag carries on. Which controls, measured on macOS 15.8: a `Button` in any style, a `NavigationLink`, a `Link`, a `.button` `Toggle`, a segmented `Picker`'s segments and a `DisclosureGroup`'s header (opening or closing it) spring-load under `.enabled` only; a `TabView`'s tabs (a strip's chips and a sidebar's rows, selecting the tab) unless `.disabled`, as SwiftUI documents; a `Menu`, a menu or radio-group `Picker` and a checkbox `Toggle` never (nor a switch: AppKit's `NSSwitch` has no spring loading). The modifier overrides an ancestor's value, and a tab's own beats the tab view's. A move to another cell restarts the pause, and a control activates once per arrival — a disclosure opened by a pause does not shut on the next. A row reorder does not spring-load: its rows land only in their own list. Not drawn: macOS highlights a spring-loadable control under the drag and flashes it as it activates (decisions B82). The keyboard needs no pause — mid-drag, Tab moves the focus and Return or Space activates the focused control, the drag still in hand. Force Click, macOS's other trigger, has no terminal equivalent |
| Drag and drop sessions | `DragSession` (`id`, `phase`, `location`, `draggedItemIndex`, `draggedItemIDs(for:)`, `ID`, `Phase`), `DropSession` (`id`, `phase`, `location`, `size`, `itemsCount`, `localSession`, `suggestedOperations`, `ID`, `Phase`, `LocalSession`), `DragConfiguration` (+ `OperationsWithinApp`, `OperationsOutsideApp`), `DropConfiguration`, `DragDropPreviewsFormation` | ✓ | **(2026-10-04)** macOS 26's values, as SwiftUI documents them — nothing about them could be measured on macOS 15.8, and their descriptions are TUIkit's own spelling. **Values only:** the modifiers that hand sessions out and apply configurations and formations (`onDragSessionUpdated`, `onDropSessionUpdated`, `dragConfiguration`, `dropConfiguration`, `dragPreviewsFormation`, `dropPreviewsFormation`, `dragContainer`, `dragContainerSelection` and the container `draggable` forms) are an open question (decisions A53) and do not compile. `location` is a `CellPoint` and `size` a `CellSize` (§2.2). A drop's `localSession` is never `nil`: a terminal app's drags all start inside it, and `OperationsOutsideApp` is recorded and offered to nothing. `DragSession.ID.matches(_:)` and `DropSession.ID.matches(_:)` compare with AppKit's dragging objects, which a TUIkit drag never is, and are declined |
| Controls | `Button` (string **and** `Button(action:label:)`, `buttonSizing(_:)` and `\.buttonSizing` — `.flexible` widens a button's face to the width it is offered and centres the label, `.fitted` and `.automatic` fit the label; a `.plain`, `.borderless` or `.link` button has no face to widen and keeps its width; `controlSize(_:)`, its range form and `\.controlSize`, read as density — a bordered button drops its padding at `.small`, its caps too at `.mini` (its label then breathes the focus), and is three rows at `.large` and `.extraLarge`, breathing whole while focused, while a faceless one is unchanged (O14, 2026-10-15; the other controls follow); `buttonBorderShape(_:)` and `\.buttonBorderShape` (`ButtonBorderShape` is a `Shape` too since lane W1, drawn as the capsule, rounded rectangle, square or circle it names, `.automatic` and `Shape.buttonBorder` deferring to the shape set here) pick the member of a FAMILY the `Appearance` in force draws in, so that every shape on a page looks of a piece (owner's choice, 2026-10-15): under `.rounded` (the default), `.line`, `.heavy` and `.doubleLine` a button is drawn in lines with no field — a capsule `( OK )`, a rounded rectangle `│ OK │`, radius zero `[ OK ]`, a circle `(★)`, and on three rows the same in a box of the appearance's own lines — the lines and the label breathing the focus together; under `.block` a face is filled — a capsule between half-block caps `▐ OK ▌`, a rectangle with square ends, a circle `▐★▌`, and on three rows the shape drawn in quadrants, or in whole cells on Apple Terminal, which draws block elements short of the line; under TUIkit's `.braille` (and `.braille(thickness:)`, bolder dots) a button is a dotted outline of its shape at any size — `⢎ OK ⡱`, `⣏ OK ⣹`, on three rows the whole outline — and boxes take dotted borders to match; under `.blank` a button is its label; TUIkit's own `.brackets(_:_:)` draws a lines face between any two one-cell glyphs (`.brackets("<", ">")`, `⌜ ⌟`, `▕ ▏`, `❨ ❩`; a wider pair draws the rectangle's sides), and its own `.underline` a lines face that is a line under the label alone, in the lines' colour where the terminal colours an underline; no `Shape` conformance until O0 (O15) — and `Button(role:action:)` showing the title its role implies — Cancel, Close, Done for `.confirm`, Delete — in the framework's localized words, where SwiftUI's toolbar draws a glyph for some; `ButtonRole` has `.close` and `.confirm`, and `Button(_ configuration:)` inside a primitive style), button styles under SwiftUI's names — `PrimitiveButtonStyle` (`.automatic`, `.bordered`, `.borderedProminent`, `.borderless`, `.plain`, `.link`, `.accessoryBar`, `.accessoryBarAction`, `.glass` and `.glass(_:)` (`GlassButtonStyle`) and `.glassProminent` (`GlassProminentButtonStyle`) — the bordered button on glass in its border shape, a lines face's lines the glass's highlighted rim, the prominent one on glass tinted the accent with a bold label, a solid face the bordered one's (decisions B-GLASS-7) — and custom primitive styles whose body is the whole button) and `ButtonStyle` (whose `configuration.label` is the label as a view) —, `Toggle(sources:isOn:)` and its three titled forms (one toggle for a collection — on when every source is, mixed when some are, drawn `⊟`/`[-]` as a checkbox and as off as a switch, which has no mixed position on macOS either; activating a mixed toggle turns every source on), toggle styles (`.automatic`, `.checkbox`, `.switch`, `.button`; `configuration.isOn` is the state and `$isOn` its binding; a built-in style's `makeBody` draws that style; `Toggle(configuration)` draws the toggle a custom style was applied over, as one focus stop) — `Toggle`, `Slider`, `Stepper`, `ProgressView`, `Gauge`, `TextField`, `SecureField` (empty, either shows its prompt, or with none its title, as SwiftUI's does), `TextEditor`, `.textContentType(_:)` with every `NSTextContentType` spelling (`.URL`, `.creditCardNumber`, `.postalCode` and the rest), a filter on what a field, a secure field or an editor takes, as there is no autofill (Deliberate deviations T8), `Picker` (`.automatic`, which is `DefaultPickerStyle` as in SwiftUI, `.menu`, `.inline`, and the TUI-specific `.radioGroup`), `DatePicker`, `Link`, `ColorPicker`, `Divider`, `Spacer`, `EmptyView`, `AnyView`, `ContentUnavailableView` (its `.search` is SwiftUI's `SearchUnavailableContent` views, naming the query of the `searchable` it is in), `RenameButton` (with `RenameAction`, `\.rename` and both `renameAction(_:)` forms; titled from the framework's own tables, and disabled where no action is set), `HelpLink` (a `?` button, by action or by `destination:` through `\.openURL`; the Apple Help book `anchor:` forms are absent), `.buttonRepeatBehavior(_:)` (`.enabled`: holding the mouse on a button acts at once and then at the auto-repeat cadence until the release, and a held Return, Space or keyboard shortcut acts on the press and each repeat; otherwise each acts once. The keyboard half needs the terminal to report repeats — the kitty keyboard protocol, `\.keyboardReporting.reportsRepeats`; in the legacy encodings a held key's repeats arrive as presses and act under every setting — decisions B12) | ✓ | `Slider`/`ProgressView`/`Stepper`/`Gauge` are floating-point-capable; `TextEditor(text:selection:)` and `TextField`'s three `selection:` initialisers (with `prompt:` and `axis:`, as SwiftUI's) exchange the caret and selection with a `TextSelection?` binding both ways — the control writes them as they move, a selection the app writes moves them at the next frame, and a multiple selection selects its first range — with `\.textSelectionAffinity` reported, and drawn by a vertical field or a wrapping editor where its text wrapped (`.upstream` at the end of the earlier row, otherwise the start of the later — so upstream a caret that Up, Down or a click puts on a wrapped row lands after its first character, while a drag or a Shift-click may still start or end a selection before it, macOS keeping an affinity per caret where TUIkit keeps one per view); `Slider`'s macOS 26 initialisers ship — `neutralValue:` fills from that value to the knob, on either side; `enabledBounds:` keeps the keyboard, wheel, pointer and the app's own value inside them and draws the track beyond them as a disabled slider's; `currentValueLabel:` stands in the read-out's place; ticks (`SliderTick`, `@SliderTickBuilder`, `SliderTickContentForEach`, or `tick:` per step) are a `╵` under the track at each value, with labels under those where they fit without touching; `sliderThumbVisibility(.hidden)` draws no knob (decisions B33); `ProgressView(timerInterval:countsDown:)` and its labelled forms follow the clock through the interval a whole second at a time, the bar showing what is left (counting down, the default) or what has passed, and `DefaultDateProgressLabel` reading out `m:ss` (`h:mm:ss` from an hour); a finished timer stops asking for frames; `ProgressViewStyle` is SwiftUI's open protocol — `.automatic` and `.linear` the bar, `.circular` a ring while determinate (the gauge's circular capacity style, three rows) and a spinner while not, a custom style its body, and `ProgressView(configuration)` drawing as the style outside — and the bar's track is TUI-specific, `ProgressView.trackStyle(_:)` as `Slider`'s is (it was spelled `.progressViewStyle(_:)` until the protocol arrived); `Divider` draws across the MINOR axis of the stack containing it, as SwiftUI's does — `─` in a column and anywhere that is not a stack, `│` spanning the height in a row — and it takes one cell across rather than reporting itself width-flexible, which in a row would have made it absorb all the slack and push its siblings to the two ends; `Gauge` has SwiftUI's four type parameters, `MarkedValueLabels` included, and its two `markedValueLabels:` initialisers — the labels are kept and not drawn, as SwiftUI documents its own gauge ignoring them; `Gauge` takes `.gaugeStyle(_:)` with terminal-native `.linearCapacity`/`.accessoryLinear`/`.accessoryLinearCapacity`/`.accessoryCircular`/`.accessoryCircularCapacity`/`.accessoryCircularTiny` (the `Capacity` styles fill min→value cumulatively, the others mark only the value's position; circular styles draw a ring dial, `…Tiny` a single pie glyph). **`GaugeStyle` is an open protocol**, as SwiftUI's is — `makeBody(configuration:)`, a `GaugeStyleConfiguration` carrying SwiftUI's five properties (`value` normalized to `0…1`, `label`, and the three optional value labels), and the six SwiftUI built-ins under SwiftUI's own type names (`DefaultGaugeStyle`, `LinearCapacityGaugeStyle`, `AccessoryLinearGaugeStyle`, `AccessoryLinearCapacityGaugeStyle`, `AccessoryCircularGaugeStyle`, `AccessoryCircularCapacityGaugeStyle`). It was a closed `enum` until 2026-09-20, on the reasoning that a terminal cannot host user-defined gauge GEOMETRIES — true, and never what the API depended on: conforming to `GaugeStyle` is returning a view, which is exactly what TUIkit already accepted from user code for `ButtonStyle`, `ToggleStyle` and `LabelStyle`, so a house style (a `▁▂▃▄▅▆▇` sparkline cell, an "N of M" readout) had no route to be written. `.accessoryCircularTiny` (`AccessoryCircularTinyGaugeStyle`) stays a TUI-only addition beside the parity set: the ring is three rows and a list row is one. The built-in styles do not implement `makeBody` — they render procedurally, and the framework reads only the style's dynamic TYPE to pick the shape — so a custom style draws the whole gauge, including whichever of the five labels its body places and whether it honours `\.labelsVisibility`; custom `ButtonStyle`/`ToggleStyle`/`LabelStyle`/`GaugeStyle` via `makeBody`; **`Stepper` draws its value where SwiftUI's draws nothing**, which makes the `onIncrement:`/`onDecrement:` init — the one whose steps are not arithmetic on a number — the case with a slot and nothing to put in it. It used to fabricate a `0` from the throwaway binding those callbacks never touch, so the read-out sat at 0 however many times you pressed; it now collapses to `◀▶`, which is SwiftUI's own answer (no value, so none shown). TUI-specific **`.stepperValueText(_:)`** fills the slot for a stepper over colours, fonts or enum cases — a plain `String`, re-evaluated every frame like any other body expression, and it overrides a value stepper's formatting too. `Stepper(…, format:)` — SwiftUI's six spellings, with a title key, a title string or a label, bounded or not — shows the value through a `ParseableFormatStyle`; the value is still changed with the arrows rather than typed, there being no field to type it into. `Slider(value:in:step:label:minimumValueLabel:maximumValueLabel:onEditingChanged:)` and its stepless twin draw the two value labels either side of the slider, a cell apart — the maximum one after the value read-out, which TUIkit draws beside the track and SwiftUI does not; `Picker(sources:selection:)` and its titled forms select for a collection — showing the value every source holds, marking none where they disagree, setting every source when an option is picked, and drawing the label and an empty disabled control over no sources; `Picker`'s `currentValueLabel:` initialisers draw that label in a menu picker's collapsed control in place of the selected option's (the inline and radio-group styles show every option and do not use it); `.horizontalRadioGroupLayout()` lays a radio-group picker's options out in a row, walked by Left and Right; `.segmented` and `.palette` draw one control face, `▐ S │ M │ L ▌`, the selected option in the accent and bold, as a prominent button's label — one focus stop, as macOS's segmented control is: measured on 15.8, the keyboard cursor starts on the selected segment, Left and Right move it without choosing and wrap at the ends, and Space (here Return too) chooses the segment under it; the cursor breathes as a list's cursor row does, a click chooses a segment (the end caps are the outer segments'), and a drag paused over one chooses it under `.springLoadingBehavior(.enabled)` — and `.paletteSelectionEffect(_:)` decides a palette's marking (`.automatic` in the accent, `.custom` none of its own, `.symbolVariant(_:)` the selected item's symbols in that variant); `PickerStyle` is a marker protocol, matching SwiftUI; **`TextFieldStyle` + `.textFieldStyle(_:)` ship**, with all four of SwiftUI's names: `.roundedBorder` and `.squareBorder` frame the plain field in box-drawing characters, rounded `╭╮╰╯` or square `┌┐└┘` — three rows tall, which is what a box is here (they were refused once as indistinguishable, and a terminal tells the two corners apart as plainly as anything); `.automatic` draws the field — a coloured surface between half-block caps — and `.plain` draws neither, leaving the text on whatever is behind it, which is what an editable value inline in a row or a sentence wants. `.plain` emits **no** background rather than the palette's, so a plain field inside a tinted container picks up that tint the way ordinary text does. The caps are two real cells, so the style changes the field's WIDTH: both passes take that number from one helper (`FieldChrome`), because the arithmetic otherwise appears four times — each of `TextField` and `SecureField`, measure and render — and the leading cap is likewise what the click-to-caret map subtracts, so a plain field's column 0 is its first character. **Omitted: `.roundedBorder` and `.squareBorder`** — each names a shape of border around the same field, and a cell grid draws one rectangle; the spellings would compile and then be indistinguishable. A field wanting a real frame composes one (`TextField(…).border()`). `Link` opens via `@Environment(\.openURL)`, and on a terminal measured to honour them **also emits a real OSC 8 hyperlink**, which tells the host where the label points — so the URL can be shown on hover and copied even though it appears nowhere on screen, which is the one thing neither keyboard nor mouse activation can give a link. TUI-specific: a link that is a control (the default `LinkDisplay.popover`) has a context menu — Show Link (the popover), Copy Link, Copy Label where its words are not its address, and Copy as Markdown — on a right-click, or Shift+F10 on its focus stop, which stays its only one; a link that writes its URL on the row has the copies on a right-click, and its destination's popover offers Copy Link on a right-click; TUI-specific `.linkActivation(.copy)` (`\.linkActivation`, `LinkActivation`) makes activating a link copy its destination, announced in the status bar, instead of opening it; each copy is announced in the status bar, as a Mac's text view offers Open Link and Copy Link on a link in it. Whether the terminal also OPENS it on a modified click is host-specific and partly open (a TUIkit app holds mouse reporting, and iTerm2 is measured to forward ⌘-click to the application instead). TUI-specific `.terminalHyperlinks(false)` turns the escape off for a subtree, and an app that intercepts its own URL scheme should: wherever the terminal opens a link itself it does so over the top of `OpenURLAction`. Which hosts get it is measured, not assumed — `Documentation/Terminals/Terminal compatibility survey.md`, "OSC 8 hyperlinks" — and **`OpenURLAction.Result` ships** with SwiftUI's `(URL) -> Result` initializer beside TUIkit's own `(URL) -> Void` one. The reason a handler returns something is that it can DECLINE: `.systemAction` hands the URL back to the system opener, so an app can intercept its own scheme and leave every other link alone without re-implementing the opener for the ones it never wanted. **The system opener itself is OFF by default** (3a96d8fc): the process may be on the far side of an ssh hop, where `open`/`xdg-open` would run on the wrong machine, and no signal says which side it is on — so a default activation raises the destination popover (and the OSC 8 hyperlink lets the terminal, which IS on the user's machine, open it), and a tool that knows it is local opts in with `TerminalClient.urlOpeningSupport` or the user with `TUIKIT_OPEN_URLS=1`. Only a running app reaches the opener at all: it is injected, and the app's run loop is what installs it (`SystemURLOpener`) and publishes it for the run, so an action read from the environment and one the app makes itself and calls directly both reach it, while a headless run, a `renderOnce` snapshot or a test hands a deferred URL to an in-memory opener and launches nothing, whatever the setting. That is the one accepted-then-inert spelling on this row, and it is deliberate. `.systemAction(_:)` rewrites the URL on the way. **Omitted: `prefersInApp:`** on both the `Result` case and `callAsFunction` — it names an in-app browser view, and a terminal has no window to put one in, so the argument could only ever be ignored; `TextEditor` draws a find and replace bar above its text under `findNavigator(isPresented:)` or its own Ctrl-G (Ctrl-F is the editor's "forward a character", as on macOS): a search field with the match count, previous and next, Done, and a replacement field with Replace and Replace All unless `replaceDisabled()`; matching ignores case, Return finds the next match, Escape closes the bar, and the current match is shown by selecting it; `findDisabled()` keeps it away when it is closer to the editor than the `findNavigator`, and `\.findContext` reports the state to a text view of the app's own. `TextEditor` wraps its long lines at its width as SwiftUI's does (decisions A43; the macOS text system's breaks, a tab's stop counted from its row's start; Up, Down, Ctrl-P, Ctrl-N and the page keys move by rows, Up from the first row to the start and Down from the last to the end, as an `NSTextView`'s do, measured on macOS 15.8; Ctrl-A and Ctrl-E keep the paragraph), scrolls by rows to follow the cursor, and with the TUIkit-only `textEditorWrapsLines(false)` keeps each line on one row and scrolls sideways instead; it takes `.textEditorStyle(_:)` — `.automatic` on its field surface, `.plain` with none, and a custom style's body in its place, SwiftUI's configuration having no members to place the editor with; `DatePicker` draws through `DatePickerStyle` (`.datePickerStyle(_:)`; a custom style builds the picker from its configuration's label, `$selection`, bounds and components; `.automatic`, `.field`, `.stepperField` and `.compact` draw the field below; a `.stepperField` draws its stepper — `▲▼` after the field, each a click target that steps the selected component and repeats while held — only under TUIkit's `.datePickerShowsStepper()`, since the pair costs three columns and Up/Down and the wheel already step; Return or a click on a `.compact` field's date opens the `.graphical` calendar in a popover under it with the keys in it (Return closes it on the day it is on, Escape or a click outside just closes it; a time-only compact picker has no calendar to open, and a click on the time chooses that component and opens none, as macOS draws the time as a field of its own that opens no calendar), as macOS's compact picker opens its calendar overlay; `.graphical` draws a month calendar, one focus stop where Left/Right move the selection a day, Up/Down a week and Page Up/Down a month, a click picks a day and ◀/▶ turn the month, with the hour-and-minute field under it where SwiftUI draws a clock), and is otherwise an inline numeric field (fixed numeric format; Left/Right pick a component, Up/Down adjust it by one, Page Up/Down by a coarse step — a decade, a quarter, a week — and Home/End send it to the ends of its own range; the wheel steps whichever field it is pointing at — the one thing the keyboard cannot do without first walking to that field — in the framework's usual direction, wheel-up towards earlier; and the field lifts under the pointer like every other control, unless it already holds the focus and is saying so with its pulsing active block); multi-selection is `List(selection: Binding<Set<…>>)` (SwiftUI has no multi-select `Picker`). `ColorPicker`'s swatch does what SwiftUI's does — a click, `Return` or `Space` opens the full colour editor (SwiftUI's platform colour panel; here `ColorPickerPanel`, as a modal on the same binding) — and, because a terminal row has width SwiftUI's control does not, the rest of the row edits R/G/B in place. The swatch marks focus and hover in its CENTRE CELL (a bullet, pulsing while focused) rather than by re-colouring itself, because unlike every other control its colour is its content; the gradient editor's stop chips share that style. TUI-specific `.colorPickerLabelWidth(_:)` sets the label column a stack of pickers lines up on — SwiftUI sizes a picker's label to its text and aligns a column of them with a `Form` or a `Grid`. `ColorPicker(selection:supportsOpacity:label:)` takes a label built from views, drawn in that column as the title would be. **`TextField(_:value:format:prompt:)` and its two siblings ship** — a field bound to a typed value rather than a `String`, through a `ParseableFormatStyle`. The substance is the draft: a field cannot format on every keystroke (typing `1` into a `.currency` field would put the caret behind `$1.00` and send the next character somewhere nobody asked for) and cannot parse on every keystroke either (`1.`, `-` and `""` are all things you must be allowed to have typed on the way to a number, and none of them parses). So the field holds the text as typed for exactly as long as it is being typed, and reconciles at the two moments the user says they are finished: Return, and leaving the field — the second is `TextFieldHandler.onFocusLost`, so tabbing away commits what Return would have. Text that does not parse is discarded silently, because a terminal field has nowhere to report an error, and dropping the draft makes the field snap back to what the value actually is; the same mechanism is what makes a *successful* commit visible, since `1e3` in a `.number` field lands as `1,000` once the value is being formatted again. An untouched field never writes to its binding, so focusing and leaving one republishes nothing. The draft lives in a `@State` on a bridging view rather than in `_TextFieldCore`, because `@State` is bound by the view's own identity at render time and that is exactly the lifetime the draft needs; everything below it is the plain string-editing field, unchanged and unaware. **Omitted: the `formatter:` spellings** (and `Text(_:formatter:)`) — `Formatter` is the pre-`FormatStyle` class API, parsing through an untyped `AnyObject` out-parameter and, in SwiftUI's `Text` overloads, constrained to `NSObject`/`ReferenceConvertible`; the typed form is the same feature with the round-trip statically guaranteed, so shipping both would only add the unchecked one. **`TextField`'s `axis:` initialisers ship (2026-10-03)** — all five shapes. On `.vertical` the text soft-wraps over rows as the macOS text system wraps it (measured on macOS 15.8: after a run of spaces, which hangs at the end of the row it breaks, between the characters of an over-long word, and at a newline), keeping the row's last cell for the caret, and the field grows a row at a time with no limit, stops at `lineLimit`'s cap, keeps a floor that `reservesSpace:` or a range sets, and then scrolls to keep the caret's row in view; empty, it grows to show its whole prompt; unfocused it shows its first rows. It keeps a text field's keys — SwiftUI's vertical field is the same field editor, so Return and Shift-Return submit — with Up/Down and Ctrl-P/Ctrl-N moving a row (off the first row to the start, off the last to the end, keeping the column through short rows), Option-Return inserting a newline where the terminal sends Option as Meta, Ctrl-O opening a line on every terminal, and Ctrl-A, Ctrl-E and Ctrl-K acting on the caret's line, all measured; Ctrl-A and Ctrl-E keep the key from an app's shortcut there, as the editor's do. A paste keeps its line breaks, a click lands on the row and column it hit, and a combo box's `▾` sits on the first row, opened by Down from the last. macOS's Ctrl-Return (a line separator) arrives from a terminal as Return, and Page Up/Down, which only scroll there, are left to the page (decisions B62, B63). `.horizontal` is the field on one line. **`.labelsHidden()` and `.labelsVisibility(_:)` ship**, with `\.labelsVisibility`. A control's label is the text it draws BESIDE itself, and hiding it takes the label's CELLS — the row closes up rather than keeping a blank gap where the words were, which is the whole point when a `Form` or a `Grid` is doing the labelling itself. Every labelled control honours it: `Toggle`, `Picker`, `Slider`, `Stepper`, `DatePicker`, `ColorPicker`, `LabeledContent`, `ProgressView`, `Gauge`. Three of those route through the shared `_CollapsingLabel` unit ("label, one separating space, or nothing") and `DatePicker` and `LabeledContent` were moved onto it here, so the meaning of *hidden* lives in one place rather than in each control's stack. A `Form` loses the label PILLAR, not just the captions — a column sized to labels nobody draws would indent every control by the width of the longest invisible one. **Deliberate split:** it takes the caption and leaves the READOUT, so a `ProgressView`'s or `Gauge`'s `currentValueLabel`, and a gauge's bound labels, stay — they state the value the control is showing rather than name the control. Ordinary content is untouched: a `Text` sitting beside a control in a stack is content, not that control's label. SwiftUI keeps the hidden label for accessibility; a terminal has no separate accessibility tree, so here it is simply not drawn — write one anyway and the same source reads correctly on both |
| Modifiers | `padding` (and `scenePadding` — one cell per edge, the `.padding()` default, which is what SwiftUI documents scene padding as off watchOS; `ScenePadding.minimum` likewise), `frame`, `overlay(alignment:content:)`, `safeAreaInset(edge:alignment:spacing:content:)` and `safeAreaBar` on all four edges (the bar BESIDE the view, which is proposed what the bar leaves — measured spacing `nil` is none, a bar wider than the view sets the width, two bars stack outward; there is no safe area for content to run under, so a scroll view stops at the bar and a style background does not extend beneath it — decisions B6), `safeAreaPadding` ×3 (padding for ordinary content; on a `ScrollView`, itself or behind modifiers that only set the environment, a content margin along the axes it scrolls — so it scrolls with the content and adds to `contentMargins` — and padding across them, which is SwiftUI's geometry, measured; nested calls add; `nil` is one cell), `containerRelativeFrame` in all three forms (the length is the nearest container's — the window's content area, a `ScrollView`'s viewport, a `List`'s (a row's width, the height inside the border), a `TabView` tab, a `NavigationStack` (less the bar over a pushed screen) or a `NavigationSplitView` column — the containers SwiftUI names; a frame between is not a container, as measured; `count`/`span`/`spacing` in whole cells, dividing last), `containerBackground(_:for:)` and `containerBackground(for:alignment:content:)` with `.window`, the one placement macOS has (the background fills the scene's content area under the centred content — the window's bars keep their own surfaces; the last one set wins, which SwiftUI leaves unspecified), `background(alignment:content:)` (`.overlay`'s mirror: laid out in the view's frame and cut to it, the view's glyphs in front; every cell the view does not colour shows the background's colour, and a background glyph shows only where the view draws no cell), `fixedSize` (a view at its ideal size on the fixed axes, asked once with nothing proposed there, so a `Text` keeps its one line and overflows a frame narrower than it, cut at the frame's edge; a view with no ideal size — a `Color`, a `.frame(maxWidth: .infinity)` — keeps what it is offered, as does a `List`, which hugs its widest row instead, a TUIkit addition: SwiftUI's has no ideal width), `foregroundStyle` (any `ShapeStyle` → §3), `background` (any style, or none — §3), `backgroundStyle`, `disabled` (on any `View`), `tint`, `tag`, `zIndex`, `badge` and `badgeProminence(_:)` (a list reads the prominence from its own environment: `.decreased` draws in the palette's quaternary ink, `.increased` as `.standard`, which is how macOS 15.8 draws it; decisions B72), `listStyle` (SwiftUI's macOS styles: `.automatic` — a bordered box, where macOS's default draws none, decisions A46 — `.bordered`, `.inset` with no border and the rows a cell in from each edge, `.plain`, and `.sidebar` with no border and its rows' `Label` icons in the list item tint, as macOS's sidebar draws them; with iOS's `.insetGrouped` as TUIkit has always had it; and TUIkit's own `.surface`, a prototype: no border, the rows inset on a surface of their own, the title and section headers a step stronger, decisions D6L A-D6L-1; under every style but `.sidebar` a list pins the header of the section its rows are scrolled into over its first line, the keyboard cursor kept below it and the line the header's to click, as macOS floats its group rows under those styles — measured, `NSTableView.floatsGroupRows`, decisions A45 — and no footer pins), list separators (decisions A47) — `listRowSeparator(_:edges:)`, `listRowSeparatorTint(_:edges:)`, `listSectionSeparator(_:edges:)`, `listSectionSeparatorTint(_:edges:)` and the `listRowSeparatorLeading`/`listRowSeparatorTrailing` guides: a faint `─` rule on a line of its own under a row (its `.bottom` edge; a row's `.top` is declined, as SwiftUI documents a style may, the line above a row being the row before it's), under a section's header and under its last row, never below the list's last row, in the border's ink unless tinted and from the row's own leading guide to its trailing one; none by default, where macOS draws a hairline under every style but `.sidebar`, because each costs a line of height (decisions D6L A-D6L-2); written on the list, every row's or section's, a row's own still winning, `listItemTint(_:)` and `ListItemTint` (a sidebar list's label icons; the other styles leave icons in the row's foreground, as macOS's do; `.preferred` draws as `.fixed`, there being no system accent to override it), `formStyle`, `lineLimit` (on `Text` **and** on any `View`, with `reservesSpace:` and SwiftUI's three range forms), `truncationMode`, `multilineTextAlignment`, `opacity`, `redacted(reason:)` (a shade glyph per cell; `.privacy` hides only what is marked `privacySensitive()`, as in SwiftUI), `geometryGroup()`, `containerCornerOffset(_:sizeToFit:)`, `flipsForRightToLeftLayoutDirection(_:)`, `scrollIndicatorsFlash(onAppear:)`, `scrollIndicatorsFlash(trigger:)` | ✓ | units are `Int` → §2.1; `geometryGroup()`, `containerCornerOffset(_:sizeToFit:)` and `flipsForRightToLeftLayoutDirection(_:)` return the view, because what each asks for already holds — an animation drives modifier values and re-lays the tree, so children move locked to their parent; a container's corners take border cells, never content ones; and the layout is always left-to-right, where SwiftUI's flip does nothing either (it must start mirroring when right-to-left lands); **`scrollIndicatorsFlash` (2026-10-13, decisions B28)** highlights instead of revealing, since a terminal's indicators are drawn whenever the content overflows: for half a second (ten 50 ms steps) the thumb and arrows of a bar, or the "N more" lines, brighten to a step past a focused bar's breath and fade back, in `ScrollView`, `List`, `Table` and `TextEditor` — on each one's first frame under `onAppear: true`, and on every change of a `trigger:` value after its first; only the indicators drawn flash, as in SwiftUI; the scrollers the framework puts around a tall menu, a sheet's or a framed container's content do not flash (SwiftUI's menus and sheets are not scroll views), while the scrollables in that content do; `lineLimit`/`truncationMode` on a `View` cascade to every `Text` below, and one written on a `Text` wins there — `.lineLimit(nil)` means *unlimited*, so it is also how a branch opts out of an inherited cap; `disabled` cascades via `\.isEnabled`; `tint` overrides the accent role (§2.5); `frame` default alignment is `.topLeading` → §2.7; **`overlay` never resizes the view it is laid over**, as in SwiftUI — the base is laid out first, the overlay is then offered the size the base came back at and positioned in it, and the result is the base's size, so a badge wider than the thing it badges no longer widens it and every sibling in the stack with it (it sized to the larger of the two until 2026-09-20, which is a `ZStack`'s rule, not an overlay's). **One deviation:** what does not fit the base's box is CUT, where SwiftUI lets an oversized overlay draw outside it — a terminal buffer has no outside, and a view that reported one size and drew another would leave every container downstream guessing. An overlay positioned by `.offset()` still hangs off the corner: that is a floating layer, composited at the root, and the cut does not touch it. The exception on the other side is a base with no extent at all (`EmptyView`, or a base whose whole payload floats): there is no frame to lay the overlay in, so the overlay is drawn whole and is what the modifier measures; `multilineTextAlignment` aligns a wrapped `Text`'s lines within its own block width (single-line text unaffected), and a frame larger than the `Text` places that block as one, as SwiftUI does (measured, `Tools/SwiftUIProbes/Ragged text in a larger frame/ragged.out`; until lane BUGS13 a centred or trailing frame placed each line on its own width); **`opacity` is real compositing** — the subtree renders to its own layer and each of its cells is resolved against the cell beneath where the layer lands, so a view fading over a coloured panel moves toward the panel's colour rather than the page's, and nesting multiplies as in SwiftUI (`0.5` inside `0.5` is `0.25`). Hue is preserved (a red heading at `0.5` still reads red), and the view keeps its space and its hit-test regions at every alpha. **The mix is in encoded sRGB, matching SwiftUI** — measured through `ImageRenderer`, which composites `Color.opacity(a)` over a ground to the same byte an encoded lerp gives at every alpha tried and never to the linear-light one. TUIkit blended in linear light until 2026-09-04 on the argument that it is physically what a translucent layer does; it is, and it is also 22× more sensitive near transparent than near opaque, which showed up as a spring transition whose fade-out visibly bounced and whose fade-in did not. See `Documentation/Colour and styling/Opacity as composition.md`, rule 5. Colours compose exactly at every alpha; **characters cannot** — two cannot share a cell at half strength each — so alpha is a decision for the glyph, taken only where two characters contest a cell: **over anything blank the subtree's characters draw at every alpha and fade all the way out; where a character sits underneath, the subtree's draws at or above `0.5` and the one behind shows below it**, keeping its own foreground while its field carries the veil (matching characters never snap — they cross-fade exactly). Text over different text therefore swaps characters at the midpoint instead of dissolving, which is the one visible departure from a graphical compositor; in exchange `opacity(0)` genuinely reveals what it covers rather than painting an invisible-coloured rectangle over it. Two rules make fading a container behave: a source SPACE composites its background and keeps what is behind it (so fading a `VStack` does not blank its rectangle), and the destination's foreground is never tinted (a translucent pane over text leaves the text legible). A repeating fade still costs no render passes — the compositor colours every phase of the cycle once and the run loop replays them |
| Animation | `withAnimation` (and with a `completion:` and `completionCriteria:`), `withTransaction`, `AnimationCompletionCriteria`, `Animation` (curves, springs — `spring(_:blendDuration:)`, `spring(duration:bounce:blendDuration:)`, `spring(response:dampingFraction:blendDuration:)`, `interactiveSpring`, `interpolatingSpring` with an initial velocity, `smooth`/`snappy`/`bouncy` — `timingCurve` from four control points or a `UnitCurve`, `delay`/`speed`/`repeatCount`/`repeatForever`/`logicallyComplete(after:)`, `debugDescription`, `customMirror`), `CustomAnimation` with `Animation(_:)`, `Animation.base`, `animate`/`velocity`/`shouldMerge(value:time:context:)`, `AnimationContext`, `AnimationState` and `AnimationStateKey` (a custom animation is integrated once on the 60 Hz frame grid as it is asked for and every value kept, so the walks of a frame agree and, once it returns `nil`, it repeats, reverses, waits and speeds up as a built-in curve does; it is asked for the fraction of a change of 1, so components move alike, in a default environment; a change it takes over restarts from rest, as TUIkit's springs do, where SwiftUI asks `shouldMerge` — decisions O3), `UnitCurve` (the named curves, `bezier(startControlPoint:endControlPoint:)`, `value(at:)`, `velocity(at:)`, `inverse`), `Spring` (every initialiser, parameter, preset and query, SwiftUI's numbers), `VisualEffect` and `visualEffect(_:)` with `EmptyVisualEffect` and the effects `offset`, `opacity`, `brightness`, `contrast`, `saturation`, `grayscale`, `hueRotation`, `KeyframeTimeline` with `Keyframes`, `KeyframeTrack`, `KeyframeTrackContent`, both builders and `LinearKeyframe`/`CubicKeyframe`/`SpringKeyframe`/`MoveKeyframe`, `PhaseAnimator` and `KeyframeAnimator` with `phaseAnimator(_:content:animation:)`, `phaseAnimator(_:trigger:content:animation:)`, `keyframeAnimator(initialValue:repeating:content:keyframes:)` and `keyframeAnimator(initialValue:trigger:content:keyframes:)` (live, 2026-10-14, refusal review O2 — as SwiftUI was measured doing, `Tools/SwiftUIProbes/Animation curves, springs and keyframes/p9.swift`, where its documentation differs: a trigger runs a phase animator through every phase and back to the first, each step animated as the animation closure says for the phase it enters, the next starting when it is logically complete; with no trigger it cycles while drawn, and a cycle with nothing animated stops at the first phase; keyframes play on the frame clock, rest at their end, and replay from the initial value — part-way, from the value reached, though without carrying velocity, TUIkit's keyframes taking none; a running animator draws every frame, as any running animation does; the keyframe modifiers' `content` closures are `@MainActor @Sendable` where SwiftUI's are `@Sendable`, since a TUIkit view is made on the main actor), `ContentTransition` (`.identity`, `.opacity`, `.interpolate`, `.numericText`) with `contentTransition(_:)` and `\.contentTransition`, `Animatable`, `VectorArithmetic`, `AnimatablePair`, `AnimatableValues` (any number of values, a parameter pack), the `@Animatable` and `@AnimatableIgnored` macros **in the opt-in `TUIkitMacros` product** (`import TUIkitMacros`; a compiler plugin built against swift-syntax, so only an app that depends on that product builds it — a client of `TUIkit` alone resolves swift-syntax's repository but compiles none of it, measured; decisions A52: the synthesized `animatableData` is an `AnimatableValues` of each typed stored `var`'s own data, main-actor isolated as TUIkit's `Animatable` is, and a property whose type is inferred is an error that names it), `Transaction` (`animation`, `disablesAnimations`, `isContinuous`, `tracksVelocity`, `addAnimationCompletion(criteria:_:)`, and custom values through `TransactionKey` and its subscript), `withTransaction(_:_:_:)` with a key path, `.animation(_:value:)`, `.animation(_:)` on an `Equatable` view (its own value is the one watched), `.transaction(_:)`, `.transaction(value:_:)`, `.transaction(_:body:)` and `.animation(_:body:)` (the modifiers built in the closure render under the transform, the view they wrap under the transaction it had), `ViewModifier`'s `animation(_:)` and `transaction(_:)`, `TimelineView`; `.transition(_:)` with an `AnyTransition` **or any `Transition`** — `AnyTransition` (`.opacity`, `.move(edge:)`, `.offset`, `.scale` and `.scale(scale:anchor:)`, `.slide`, `.push(from:)`, `.identity`, `.asymmetric`, `.modifier(active:identity:)`, `combined(with:)`, `animation(_:)`, `init(_:)` from a `Transition`), the `Transition` protocol (`body(content:phase:)`, `properties`, `apply(content:phase:)`, `combined(with:)`, `animation(_:)`), `TransitionPhase`, `TransitionProperties`, `PlaceholderContentView`, and the built-in types `OpacityTransition`, `MoveTransition`, `OffsetTransition`, `ScaleTransition`, `SlideTransition`, `PushTransition`, `IdentityTransition`, `AsymmetricTransition`; `matchedGeometryEffect(id:in:properties:anchor:isSource:)` and `MatchedGeometryProperties` | ✓ | **Transactions:** a frame draws every change made since the last under one transaction, and an `@Observable` change made inside `withAnimation` animates whichever write fired the observation of the body that reads it, where SwiftUI updates once per write (measured, `Tools/SwiftUIProbes/Transactions of observed changes/txn.out`; deviations A7, decisions B-APPSTATE-2). Each app — each test's — keeps its own pending transaction (decisions APPSTATE). A transition is applied to the view's rendered cells, not its layout (§2.9 and `AnyTransition`). **Insertion and removal in a stack** (owner ruling FB16 (a′), 2026-10-18; SwiftUI measured on macOS 15.8, five transitions, `.identity` included): the layout changes at once, and under the animation every child of an eager `VStack`, `HStack` or `ZStack`, and every subview of a `Layout`, whose place changed glides from its old place to its new one in whole cells — the siblings of a view coming or going among them, whatever its transition; a child seen for the first time is placed directly. A view leaving gives its room back at once in an eager stack or a `ZStack` and plays its removal where it was, beneath the views around it unless a `.zIndex` lifts it, as SwiftUI draws it; the TUI-specific `.transitionSpace(.held)` keeps the room until the removal has played (§4c). A glide is interrupted as any animation is (merged or added, O3), jumps under reduce motion, and holds a `withAnimation` completion until it lands. A lazy stack, a `Grid`, and a `List`'s or a `Table`'s rows move their children at once and keep a removal's room while it plays, as do a `Layout` and a `nil` rendered on its own; a container's own size — its border, its background — changes at once where SwiftUI's frame glides (decisions FB16, deviations A6). A `Transition` of the app's own is drawn by scrubbing its body: rendered at the end phase to note every animatable value, then at `.identity` with each value interpolated by the frame's presence — SwiftUI's per-modifier interpolation, computed rather than animated. Three deviations, each because a frame is rebuilt from the tree: a departing view's content is the picture it last drew, so the body moves around those cells; the view keeps its `.identity`-phase size while it comes and goes; and the body is drawn at rest every frame (two renders a frame while it plays). `.scale` uncovers the view from its anchor a cell at a time, and a scale above 1 draws it whole. A view an `if` puts in or takes out under an animation with no `.transition` fades (`.opacity`, SwiftUI's default); an `if`/`else` whose branch flips, a `switch` and a `ForEach`'s rows change at once (Deliberate deviations, Slated). **Reduce motion** (decisions B53): `\.accessibilityReduceMotion` is `true` when the user started the app with `TUIKIT_REDUCE_MOTION=1`, or the app asked for less motion with the TUI-specific `Scene.reduceMotion(_:)` — which can turn it on and never off, the user's ask standing. Only an app run in a terminal reads the variable; TUIkit's headless renders and its tests draw as if it were unset, so they draw the same wherever their process was started. **The operating system's Reduce Motion counts too, but only where TUIkit is sure the user is at this machine** (refusal review O1, 2026-10-14): the clipboard's locality test (`SessionLocality`) — no ssh variable, no remote-session daemon or session keeper among the app's parents, and inside tmux every attached client passing the same test — since the machine an app runs on need not be the one its user sits at (over ssh it would be the server's). It is macOS's Reduce motion, or GNOME's animations switched off, read off the main actor at launch and again whenever the terminal regains the focus, so a reading lands a frame or two after launch; only the app runner reads it. A wrong "local" can only add reduced motion. Where it is on, a transition with motion (`TransitionProperties.hasMotion`: every built-in but `.opacity` and `.identity`, a combined or asymmetric pair if either half, a `Transition` of the app's own unless its properties say otherwise) plays as `.opacity`, keeping its own animation, as SwiftUI's does. TUIkit goes further, holding still what moves in X and Y (owner decision A-D3M-1, 2026-10-05): an animated change of `.offset`, `.position`, padding or a fixed `.frame`, an offset in a `visualEffect` or an `.animated` `scrollTransition`, and a child gliding to a new place in its stack, jumps to its end (a `withAnimation` completion waiting on nothing else fires at once); a drag's preview neither lifts out of its row nor flies home (Drag preview flights, below). Colour and opacity animations, the focus breath, the caret, spinners, progress indicators and symbol effects are not that kind of motion and keep moving; scrolling never animates here anyway. An app's own `Animatable` view, a keyframe animator's tracks and a custom `Layout`'s data animate as they did — read `\.accessibilityReduceMotion` to hold them, as in SwiftUI. See `SwiftUI API completion decisions.md` B52. `isContinuous` is set by a `Slider` track drag and a held arrow's repeats; `tracksVelocity` is carried and tracks nothing, TUIkit's springs retargeting at rest (B55). A completion fires when the animations the change's frame started are done; a spring logically completes at its pace (B56). A spring retargeted mid-flight restarts from where its picture is, at rest, so `blendDuration` blends nothing and an interpolating spring's starting velocity is not inherited by the next change (B58). A visual effect draws what the `View` modifier of its name draws; its geometry proxy answers every space as a `GeometryReader`'s does (decisions A2, 2026-10-05), so SwiftUI's own example — an offset that follows the view's `.global` position — draws its offset, in the frame that draws it; the rotation, scale and transform effects wait on Wave 16 (B59). A keyframe timeline samples SwiftUI's curves (measured) and is main-actor isolated, as `Animatable` is; the animator views that play one are O2 (B60). A content transition dissolves everything the modifier wraps, through the opacity compositor; the unset default draws a change at once (B61, A40). **Matched geometry** (refusal review O4, ruled 2026-10-12; shipped 2026-10-18): a member of a group that is not its source is drawn at the source's geometry — its anchor on the point the source's anchor names, at the source's size — and keeps its own place in its parent's layout, as SwiftUI's does (measured, `Tools/SwiftUIProbes/Matched geometry/`); its drawing stands at its own place in the stacking order, and floats over every view while an animation carries it. Where the views are is found by the scene root once the frame is drawn, and a member that drew from somewhere else is drawn again in the same frame (design study A2's correction). Under an animation, a view inserted as another of its id is removed comes from where that one was drawn, and a follower whose source changes is carried to it; it is laid out again at each frame's size, and only moved, a whole cell at a time, while its size is its own. SwiftUI scales a follower's drawing there instead (measured); a removed view stays where it was while it leaves; a source its layout moves is not animated by the effect (Deliberate deviations A8). Under reduce motion the change is drawn at once. |
| App | `App`, `Scene`, `WindowGroup`, `SceneBuilder`, `@main`, `@AppStorage`, `@ScaledMetric`, `@Environment(\.dismiss)`; on a scene, `.environment(_:_:)`, `.environment(_:)` (an `@Observable` object, or `nil` to clear one), `.transformEnvironment(_:transform:)`, `.defaultAppStorage(_:)` and `.onChange(of:initial:_:)` (compared once a frame for the scene, before its windows draw, however many of them are open — decisions O9, B-O9-14) | ✓ | `@ScaledMetric` reads its base value, as macOS's does at every `\.dynamicTypeSize` (measured), and takes any `Numeric`, so it can hold the `Int` cells TUIkit's dimensions are. `@AppStorage(_:store:)` and `@SceneStorage(_:)` hold an optional value, `nil` until something is stored. `store:` takes a `UserDefaults` on Apple platforms, as SwiftUI's does, and a TUIkit `StorageBackend` on every platform; `defaultAppStorage(_:)`, on a view or a scene, takes either and sets the store an `@AppStorage` that names none reads and writes — the nearest one above it, found before its view's body runs. A write through any store over a defaults domain redraws the views that read the key through another. `@AppStorage` and `@SceneStorage` take a `RawRepresentable` value whose raw value is `Codable` — an `enum Mode: String { … }`, SwiftUI's commonest — stored as its raw value, and its optional, as SwiftUI's take `String` and `Int` raw values (2026-10-14, the semantic audit's item 85); a raw value that is no case reads as nothing stored, and a type that is `Codable` too keeps its `Codable` form, which stores the same raw value. In `UserDefaults` a property-list value is stored as itself, as SwiftUI stores it — a `Bool`, an `Int`, a `Double`, a `String`, a `Date`, `Data`, a `URL` (through `UserDefaults`' URL methods), a raw-representable's `Int` or `String` raw value, and their optionals, whose `nil` removes the key — so `UserDefaults`' own getters read what `@AppStorage` wrote and `@AppStorage` reads what they set (2026-10-14); any other `Codable` value, a TUIkit extension, is stored as its JSON encoding, in `UserDefaults` and in every other store. A scene's environment values are written into the frame's root environment, so they reach the window's bars as well as the content, as SwiftUI's reach a window's toolbar; the scene closest to the content wins. `@AppStorage` is *enhanced* (pluggable backend). `\.isPresented` says whether a view is inside something presented (a sheet, modal, cover, popover, alert or dialog) — `false` for a pushed `NavigationStack` screen, which was navigated to rather than presented. `\.dismiss` means the nearest thing that can be dismissed: a presentation closes, a pushed `NavigationStack` screen pops, and only at the top level — where a terminal app has nothing enclosing it — does dismissing mean quitting. A **custom `Scene`** conformance is a real one: `Scene` declares `associatedtype Body: Scene` and a `@SceneBuilder var body`, so `struct Root: Scene { var body: some Scene { WindowGroup { … } } }` renders its content and carries the scene modifiers written on either side of it. `WindowGroup` and the wrappers `.palette` / `.appearance` / `.windowToolbarStyle` / `.mouseSupport` produce are primitives — `Body == Never`, rendering through an internal `SceneRenderable` — exactly as a primitive `View` sets `Body == Never` and renders through `Renderable` |
| Scenes | `SceneBuilder` with several scenes (the variadic `buildBlock(_:)`), a scene inside `if #available` (`buildOptional(_:)`, `buildLimitedAvailability(_:)`, `_LimitedAvailabilitySceneMarker`) and `Group` of scenes (`init(content:)`); `Window` (`init(_:id:content:)`, a `Text`, key or string title); `WindowGroup`'s `init(id:makeContent:)`, `init(_:id:makeContent:)` and `init(_:makeContent:)`, and the sixteen `for:` forms of a group that presents data (`PresentedWindowContent`), with and without an identifier, a title and a `defaultValue:`; `\.openWindow` (`OpenWindowAction`: `callAsFunction(id:)`, `(value:)`, `(id:value:)` and their `sharingBehavior:` forms, `SharingBehavior`), `\.dismissWindow` (`DismissWindowAction`: `callAsFunction()`, `(id:)`, `(value:)`, `(id:value:)`), `\.supportsMultipleWindows`; `View.windowDismissBehavior(_:)` (`WindowInteractionBehavior`: `.automatic`, `.enabled`, `.disabled`); `WindowVisibilityToggle` (`init(windowID:)`, `DefaultWindowVisibilityToggleLabel`); `Scene.keyboardShortcut(_:)` and `(_:modifiers:localization:)`; `DismissBehavior` (`.interactive`, `.destructive`) and `Transaction.dismissBehavior`; `Settings` (`init(content:)`), `SettingsLink` (`init()`, `init(label:)`), `DefaultSettingsLinkLabel`, `\.openSettings` (`OpenSettingsAction`); `AlertScene` (its twelve initializers), and on a scene `.dialogSeverity(_:)`, `.dialogIcon(_:)` and `.dialogSuppressionToggle(…)` | ✓ | Every scene of a body is the app's; a terminal shows one surface, so the scenes are presented on it by one presenter, whose policy is the one refusal review O9 was ruled (b) for: every window covers the whole terminal, the one in front drawn and the others held behind it, drawn off-screen so their state, lifecycle and tasks carry on (deviation W9). The first window scene opens at launch, as on macOS, and is drawn filling the terminal, configured by its own modifiers; a modifier written on several scenes at once (on a `Group`, or on a custom scene whose `body` is a block of them) reaches each. `openWindow(id:)` opens a window over the one in front: a `Window`'s one window, brought to the front if it is open, or a new window of a `WindowGroup`, each with its own state and its own view, which the group's builder makes for it as SwiftUI's does, so a closure a window captured writes that window's `@SceneStorage`; an identifier no scene declares opens nothing. A group that presents data gives each window its own value through the binding its content is built with — the value `openWindow(value:)` opened it with, its `defaultValue`, or `nil` — and `openWindow(value:)` with a value a window presents (now: a write through the binding counts) brings that window to the front; `dismissWindow(value:)` closes it alone. No window is ever shared, there being no sharing session: `sharingBehavior: .requested` opens the window as it is, and `.required` opens nothing and throws, as SwiftUI documents for a window that could not be shared. `dismissWindow()` closes the window it was read in, `dismissWindow(id:)` every open window of that scene, each as the transaction's `dismissBehavior` allows: under `.interactive`, the default, a window showing a sheet, a cover, an alert or a confirmation dialog stays open, as on macOS, and under `.destructive` it closes with them; and `dismiss()` at a window's root closes the window, as SwiftUI documents of a window's root view on macOS; the window behind comes back with its focus and its bars. With another window behind it, Escape closes the window in front when nothing in it takes the key (W10), unless its content says `.windowDismissBehavior(.disabled)`, which takes Escape, the Window menu's Close Window and a visibility toggle's Hide away as macOS takes the close button and ⌘W, leaving `dismissWindow` to the app (B-O9-10); a `WindowVisibilityToggle` reads "Show *Title*" while its `Window`'s window is not in front, opening it or bringing it there, and "Hide *Title*" while it is, closing it as the Window menu's Close Window does — never the last window, nor one whose content disabled its dismissal or that shows a sheet — and disabled where it may do neither, as for an identifier no `Window` scene has (B-O9-11); a scene's `.keyboardShortcut` opens it — a `Window`'s window, opened or brought to the front, a new window of a group, the `Settings` sheet in place of its ⌘, (`nil` takes that away) — through `commandKey`, menu bar or none, below the window's own shortcut on the key; a window group has no ⌘N, there being no File ▸ New Window (B-O9-12); the window in front titles the terminal's window or tab (`OSC 2`) — its root `.navigationTitle` where no top bar shows it, else its scene's title — the terminal's own title saved on its title stack before the first and restored for a window with none, on suspend and on exit (B-O9-13; the survey's "The window title"); the last window closing quits the app (W11). `\.supportsMultipleWindows` is `true` (decisions O9, A-O9-1). A `Settings` scene is presented as a sheet over the window in front by `openSettings`, a `SettingsLink` ("Settings…", with a gear) or the menu bar's Settings… (W12), its own modifiers reaching its content alone; with none declared they present nothing. An `AlertScene` is presented over the window in front while its binding is true, as an alert written on a view in it is; the scene dialog modifiers are its alert's when written on it, and reach the alerts a window scene's views present when written on that (decisions A26, O9 B-O9-7). `Settings` and `AlertScene` are in TUIkitScenes (decisions O9, B-O9-5). `@SceneStorage` is one per window: the window opened at launch keeps its values under `scene.`, a `Window`'s under its identifier, and a group's windows under the group's identifier and, past the first of a session, their ordinal (B-O9-8). The commands are the app's: every scene's `.commands`, in declaration order, as SwiftUI's main menu has them (measured on macOS 15.8), a modifier around several scenes counted once. As SwiftUI's builder takes only an availability-checked scene in `buildOptional`, an `if` on a value around a scene does not compile, and `if #available` does |
| Text | `Text(_:)` (`LocalizedStringKey` **and** `StringProtocol`), `Text(verbatim:)`, `Text(_:format:)`, `Text(_:style:)` with `Text.DateStyle`, `Text(timerInterval:pauseTime:countsDown:showsHours:)`, a `Text` interpolated into a key with its styling kept (`Text + Text`, deprecated in SwiftUI since macOS 26, is gone) | ✓ | a literal is a localization key, a computed `String` is not — SwiftUI's rule, and §4a records what it took to reproduce. **A key's Markdown draws**, as in SwiftUI — `**bold**`, `*italic*`, `~~struck~~`, `` `code` ``, links and bare URLs — parsed before its values go in; see §4a. **Every control and modifier that takes a title takes one too** — `Button`, `Toggle`, `TextField`, `Section`, `navigationTitle`, `alert`, … — as does the TUI-specific chrome (`Dialog`, `Alert`, `Card`, `StatusBarItem`, notifications); see §4a. **`Text(_:style:)` is live**: it is formatted when drawn, at the frame's date, in the environment's `\.locale`, `\.calendar` and `\.timeZone`, and `.relative`, `.offset` and `.timer` redraw as the clock moves, each asking the run loop for one frame at the instant its words change (each second for `2 min, 5 sec`, each minute for `1 hr, 2 min`) and none between; `.time` and `.date` never change and ask for nothing.
