The strings are SwiftUI's, measured on macOS 15 and pinned in `TextDateStyleTests` — `.relative` two abbreviated units, truncated, unsigned; `.offset` one wide unit signed `+`/`−`; `.timer` a clock face; `.time` the locale's short time (`06:13` in German, which `Date.FormatStyle`'s `.shortened` does not give). **A span of a month or more on Linux (and Windows, and WASI) is spelt from TUIkit's own tables** (decisions B48): the open-source Foundation declares `Date.ComponentsFormatStyle` — its `Field` type exists — but no initialiser for it, and nothing else there spells years and months (`Duration` has no such units). So their names come from the framework's translation tables, in its seven languages (wide and abbreviated, singular and plural by CLDR's rules: French counts zero as one, Japanese and Chinese have no plural), and the finer units and the joining from `Duration.UnitsFormatStyle`, which is localised there: measured equal to Apple's strings in all seven (`TimeSpanTableTests`). A locale in none of the seven — or Chinese in Traditional characters — takes English names in its own number format and joining; CLDR's French, Italian and Spanish `many` (a million years) is spelt as `other`. **`Text(timerInterval:…)`** is live the same way: counting down it shows the time left rounded up (`0:01` until the end), counting up the time gone rounded down, a frame a second, none once it cannot change; its `pauseTime` is SwiftUI's, which counting down holds the timer when what it SHOWS reaches `pauseTime − start` (SwiftUI's own example: a twelve-minute countdown with a pause ten minutes in stops at `10:00`, measured). A live text handed to something that takes its string (`navigationTitle(Text)`, `help(Text)`) gives the reading from when it was built. `textVariant(_:)` takes `.fixed`, SwiftUI's default and what every text does; `.sizeDependent`, which in SwiftUI picks a narrower spelling of a live text that would not fit (`1h 2m` for `1 hour, 2 minutes`, measured), is not offered yet and does not compile (decisions A32) |
| Text selection | `textSelection(_:)`, `TextSelectability` (`.enabled`, `.disabled`, `allowsSelection`), `EnabledTextSelectability`, `DisabledTextSelectability` | ✓ | What is selected is what is DRAWN, not one `Text` at a time: a drag that no control uses selects the cells it crosses in reading order, a double-click a word (a web address whole), a triple-click the line in its context (the text between the walls or wide gaps either side), a quadruple-click the row, Option-drag a rectangle, kept to the pane the press is in; the app's ⌘C (Ctrl-C by default) or a right-click's Copy copies it, ahead of the focused field, through the clipboard writer (OSC 52, or this machine's clipboard only when the user is at this machine). That selection is on by default wherever a drag does nothing else — SwiftUI's default is `.disabled`, but a terminal selects any text with a modifier held, and an app that reports the mouse took that away. `.enabled` keeps a selection to its view (on one `Text`, that `Text`) and widens it to `List` and `Table` rows as macOS does, measured on macOS 15.8: a press on a row's text is the text's — a drag selects it, kept to the row, a double-click selects a word and activates nothing, a click selects nothing — and a press on the row's blank cells is the row's; on a row's own `Text` (`List(items) { Text($0.name).textSelection(.enabled) }`) it does the same for that text, kept to that `Text`. `.disabled` turns it off. The deviation: inside an `.enabled` container that is not a list, a drag may cross from one `Text` into the next, and a `Table` row's selection crosses its columns, where macOS keeps to one cell — and `.enabled` on a cell's `Text` keeps a selection begun on that text to its cell, as on a row's own `Text`, and makes the rest of the row's text selectable, kept to the row, as around the table, since a table draws the rest of the row as text and keeps no rectangle of it. TUIkit-only: `Scene.textSelectionCopiesOnRelease(_:)`. No keyboard route (copy mode was declined). See `MouseAndGestures` |
| Attributed text | `Text(_:)` for an `AttributedString`; `AttributeScopes.SwiftUIAttributes` (`foregroundColor`, `backgroundColor`, `font`, `underlineStyle`, `strikethroughStyle`, `foundation`) and `AttributeScopes.swiftUI`, set and read through `AttributeDynamicLookup` (`string.foregroundColor = .red`); `Text.LineStyle` (`single`, `init(pattern:color:)`, `Pattern`) | ✓ | each run of the string draws with its own attributes, and they win over the text's modifiers, which fill in what a run does not state — SwiftUI's rule, measured on macOS 15.8: a run's colour beats `.foregroundStyle`, its font beats `.font`, and `.bold(false)` or `.underline(false)` cannot take back what it states. The result is one text of several runs, and interpolates into another like one: each part keeps its attributes and a live part stays live. A font draws as its intensity tier (see `Font`); an underline is drawn in its line style's pattern and colour as far as the terminal draws them (*Terminal compatibility survey*, "Underline shapes and colours"), and a strikethrough is one solid line, coloured only on iTerm2. **A run carrying Foundation's `link` draws as a link**: in the accent colour, which follows `.tint` as SwiftUI's link colour does, unless the run states its own (the text's `.foregroundStyle` does not reach it — measured), underlined as a `Link` is (TUIkit's deviation, for the same reason; `.linkUnderline(false)` removes it), and carrying an OSC 8 hyperlink wherever `Link`'s would be emitted — the host honours OSC 8, `.terminalHyperlinks` allows it, the text is enabled — one balanced pair per line, with an id when it wraps. It is not a control: no focus and no click of its own, so the destination is the terminal's to show and open (decisions B85). On Apple platforms Foundation's `inlinePresentationIntent` draws too (strong as bold, emphasis as italic, strikethrough struck, code as it is); swift-foundation has no such attribute. SwiftUI's `kern`, `tracking`, `baselineOffset`, `lineHeight`, `alignment` and `adaptiveImageGlyph` are absent, so setting one does not compile, and so is `accessibility` (no Accessibility framework on Linux). **On macOS SwiftUI's own scope is visible as well**, because AppKit loads SwiftUICore: setting an attribute binds to TUIkit's (its dynamic-member subscripts are more specific than SwiftUI's generic one), but naming the scope's types in a type position is ambiguous there, and an attribute only SwiftUI has, such as `kern`, compiles and then fails to link (decisions B84). The keys' Codable conformances (macOS 26) are not here: TUIkit's `Color` and `Font` are not Codable |
| Live time formats | `TimeDataSource` (`.currentDate`, `.dateRange(startingAt:)`, `.dateRange(endingAt:)`, `.durationOffset(to:)`) and `Text(_:format:)` over one, for a `DiscreteFormatStyle` into a `String` or an `AttributedString` — live: the source's value at each frame, in the environment's `\.locale`, and a frame asked for where the style's `discreteInput(after:)` says its output next changes; `Text(_:format:)` and `appendInterpolation(_:format:)` for a style into an `AttributedString` (the one-off `Text(_:format:)` draws the output as `Text(AttributedString)` does, attributes and all; the interpolation and the live form over a `TimeDataSource` take its characters, decisions B88); `SystemFormatStyle` — `.Timer` and `.timer(countingDownIn:…)`/`.timer(countingUpIn:…)`, `.Stopwatch` and `.stopwatch(startingAt:…)`, `.DateOffset` and `.offset(to:…)`, `.DateReference` and `.reference(to:…)` — with their `discreteInput(after:)`/`(before:)`, `input(after:)`/`(before:)`, `locale(_:)`, `calendar(_:)` and `Codable` (macOS 15 and up, as SwiftUI's; `DiscreteFormatStyle` is new there); `Text.textVariant(_:)` with `TextVariantPreference`'s `.fixed` (`FixedTextVariant`) and `.sizeDependent` (`SizeDependentTextVariant`) | ✓ | `.sizeDependent` steps a live text down SwiftUI's ladders, measured on macOS 15, and draws the widest spelling that fits its space without a cut word or a line past its limit (wrapping first, so without a line limit the widest is kept, as SwiftUI keeps it): an offset `1 hour, 2 minutes` → `1 hr, 2 min` → `1h 2m`, a relative reference `2 minutes ago` → `2 min. ago` → `2m ago`, an absolute one `Friday, May 8` → `Fri, May 8` → `5/8`, a timer in units `1 hour, 2 minutes` → `1 hr 2 min` → `1:02`, a stopwatch `01:02:05.50` → `1:02:05` → `1:02` → `1h`, and `Text(_:style:)`'s `.relative`, `.offset`, `.date` and `.time` likewise (a timer's face has none), each in the environment's locale, live values in an interpolation stepping down together; the measure and the render choose by one rule, and only a text that asks pays. Off Darwin a year or month in the narrowest rungs is spelt as the abbreviated one, TUIkit's tables holding two widths (decisions A32).  the readings are SwiftUI's, measured on macOS 15 and pinned in `SystemFormatStyleClockTests` and `SystemFormatStyleDateTests`: a timer's `m:ss`/`h:mm:ss` (a countdown rounding up, everything else down), a stopwatch's `00:05.25`, and the units (`1 hour, 2 minutes`) either falls back to when `maxFieldCount` cannot hold the face or the precision is coarser than a second; an offset's truncated, signed units (`−2 minutes, 5 seconds`, a clock face when only minutes and seconds are allowed); a reference's `now`/`in 2 minutes`/`yesterday` near the date — Foundation's anchored relative style — and `Friday, May 8`/`May 2026` past the threshold. The absolute reference is drawn in the calendar's zone, where SwiftUI keeps a zone of its own beside the calendar. On Linux an offset of a month or more is spelt from TUIkit's own tables, `Date.ComponentsFormatStyle` having no initialiser there — see the `Text` row (decisions B48). A live output's attributes are not drawn: a live text reads words at each frame. **SwiftUI's own statics stay out of an app that imports only TUIkit** (decisions B50): AppKit imports SwiftUICore, so nothing TUIkit is built from imports AppKit — the bridges to Apple's colour and image types are the `TUIkitAppKit` module — and `Date.now.formatted(.offset(to: d))` compiles (`CompileCorpusTUIkitOnly.swift` proves it). An app that also imports `TUIkitAppKit`, or AppKit itself, loads SwiftUICore again and sees SwiftUI's statics beside TUIkit's; for it TUIkit declares each static again on `DiscreteFormatStyle`, which wins wherever TUIkit takes the style, and a plain `FormatStyle` context outside TUIkit is ambiguous there, settled by spelling the type (`SystemFormatStyle.DateOffset(to: d)`) |
| Symbol effects (macOS) | `symbolEffect(_:options:isActive:)`, `symbolEffect(_:options:value:)`, `symbolEffectsRemoved(_:)`, `SymbolEffectTransition` with `Transition.symbolEffect` and `.symbolEffect(_:options:)`, `ContentTransition.symbolEffect` and `.symbolEffect(_:options:)` — taking **Apple's own Symbols types** (`PulseSymbolEffect`, `SymbolEffectOptions`, …), so macOS only, and absent elsewhere | partial | **Only pulse and breathe are drawn, and the rest compile and change nothing.** A symbol is one glyph in one cell: pulse fades its ink to a third and back, breathe to two thirds (its swell in size is not drawn), as one replayed run (decisions A35). Bounce, scale, wiggle, rotate and variable colour need the symbol's layers or geometry; being Apple's types they cannot be refused at compile time, so they are told apart by type and not drawn. The options' speed is honoured, and a discrete effect's repeat count; an active indefinite effect breathes for as long as it is active, whatever its count. The transitions are drawn as fades: `Transition.symbolEffect` fades the whole view it is on and `ContentTransition.symbolEffect` cross-fades as `.opacity` does, where SwiftUI animates only the symbol images inside; their directions are not drawn. `WiggleSymbolEffect.custom(angle:)` is absent, a wiggle not being drawn |
| Color values | `Color.red`/`.white`/`.gray`/… (all fifteen of SwiftUI's names), `.clear`, `.primary`/`.secondary`/… and `.opacity(_:)`; `Color(red:green:blue:opacity:)` and `(white:opacity:)` in all three `Color.RGBColorSpace`s, `Color(hue:saturation:brightness:opacity:)`; `Color.Resolved` and `resolve(in:)`; `Color(_:bundle:)` (a named colour from an asset catalogue); `Color.headroom(_:)`, which returns the colour as it is, a terminal being an SDR display whose cap is always met (the rest of SwiftUI's HDR API — `Color.ResolvedHDR`, `resolveHDR(in:)`, `exposureAdjust(_:)` — is declined: no terminal draws above SDR white, decisions A16); `Color(cgColor:)`, `Color.Resolved.cgColor`, `Color(nsColor:)` and `NSColor(_:)` on Apple platforms, **in the `TUIkitAppKit` module** — `import TUIkitAppKit` beside `import TUIkit` (decisions B50: a module that imports AppKit brings SwiftUICore into every app that imports it, so TUIkit itself does not) | ✓ | a `Color` keeps 8-bit sRGB, so SwiftUI's extended range is clamped when a `Color` is made — it survives in a `Color.Resolved`, whose numbers, `#RRGGBBAA` description and `[r, g, b, a]` coding match SwiftUI's, measured, down to its four-place Display P3 matrix. `resolve(in:)` reads a palette role through the palette in force, and a terminal slot as the colour the terminal reported, else xterm's; the terminal's own default, unreported, reads white or black by the scheme. A dynamic `NSColor` is read once, at the appearance in force then; `NSColor(_:)` resolves a palette role against the default palette, having no environment. `Color(_:bundle:)` reads `Name.colorset` from the uncompiled `.xcassets` folder `swift build` copies into a resource bundle, everywhere, and on macOS, where the file imports `TUIkitAppKit`, a compiled `Assets.car` through `NSColor(named:bundle:)`; a missing colour is `.clear`, as SwiftUI's is (measured). **A colour set's light and dark variants are chosen when the colour is made**, by the default palette's colour scheme, not where it is drawn: a `Color` is five bytes, with no room for both (decisions D6M B-D6M-1). The TUI-only constructors (`.rgb`, `.hex`, `.hsl`, `.ansi`, `.palette256`) remain. The fifteen names are fixed RGB at Apple's light values; SwiftUI adapts the hues to a dark appearance and these do not, though `\.colorScheme` says which appearance is in force (→ §2.5). The terminal's own slots are the TUI-only `Color.ansi(_:)`, and `Color.magenta` (#FF00FF) is TUI-only. `Color.opacity(_:)` stores an alpha, composited over whatever the colour is drawn on and multiplied into any alpha already there, as in SwiftUI; it shadows `ShapeStyle.opacity(_:)` for a `Color` receiver, as it does in SwiftUI |
| Shapes | `Shape` (`path(in:)`, `role`, `sizeThatFits(_:)` in TUIkit's layout terms — `ProposedSize` to `ViewSize`, as a `Layout`'s is), `InsettableShape`, `ShapeView` and the views a drawing makes (`_ShapeView`, `FillShapeView`, `StrokeShapeView`, `StrokeBorderShapeView`, chained: `Circle().fill(.yellow).stroke(.red)`), `ShapeRole`, `FillStyle`, `StrokeStyle` (width, caps, joins, miter limit, dashes, phase), `RoundedCornerStyle`, `RectangleCornerRadii`; `Rectangle`, `RoundedRectangle`, `UnevenRoundedRectangle`, `Capsule`, `Circle`, `Ellipse`, `ContainerRelativeShape`, `AnyShape`, `Path` (every builder, `boundingRect`, `contains(_:eoFill:)`, `trimmedPath(from:to:)`, `applying(_:)`, `description` and `init?(_:)`, and `cgPath` on Apple platforms); `fill`, `stroke`, `strokeBorder`, `stroke(lineWidth:)` as a shape, `trim(from:to:)`, `offset`, `rotation`, `scale`, `transform`, `size`, `inset(by:)`, the leading-dot `.rect`, `.circle`, `.capsule`, `.ellipse`, `.containerRelative`; the animatable conformances; on any view `background(_:in:fillStyle:)`, `background(in:fillStyle:)`, `overlay(_:in:fillStyle:)`, `clipShape(_:style:)` and `contentShape(_:eoFill:)` for a `Rectangle`; `containerShape(_:)` (both forms), and macOS 26's `ConcentricRectangle`, `RoundedRectangularShape`, `RoundedRectangularShapeCorners`, `Edge.Corner` (`Set`, `Style`) and the rounded shapes' `corners(in:)` | ✓ | **A shape's unit is square → §2.12**: columns across, `imageCellAspect` units a row down, so a circle is round and a line width or corner radius is in columns. The rectangle family draws in box glyphs — a stroke `┌─┐`, `╭` per rounded corner, a fill in cells with quadrant-cut corners, one row capped `▐ ▌` — and everything else is rasterized at half-block resolution, its edge's coverage composited as alpha (antialiasing). `Rectangle().fill(s)` is `Color` as a view, byte for byte. A shape draws within its frame (Deliberate deviations C6). A shape behind a view shows its quadrant corners and caps through the view's blank cells, so padding round a label does not square it off. `clipShape` keeps a cell where the shape covers at least half of it and clears the rest to what is behind (deviation C9); a `Rectangle` clips to the frame. `contentShape` takes a `Rectangle` alone: a view's hit area is already its frame here (refusal review P3), and a hit area cut to another shape is not drawn yet, so `.contentShape(Circle())` does not compile (decisions W1, B-W1-10); the `ContentShapeKinds` form waits on P3. A `ContainerRelativeShape` or a `ConcentricRectangle` inside a `containerShape(_:)` is the container's shape inset by how far inside it the view lies, the nearest side deciding, so a radius the inset uses up is drawn square (`┌`) (decisions W1, B-W1-12; the macOS 26 types are read from the SDK, not measured, B-W1-13). Absent, failing to compile: `Path`'s boolean operations, `normalized(eoFill:)` and `strokedPath(_:)` (lane W1's cut), and `layoutDirectionBehavior` (decisions A3). A ported shape needs `import Foundation` beside `import TUIkit` for `CGRect` and `CGPoint`, which TUIkit does not re-export (decisions W1, A-W1-1) |

The parity surface above is regression-tested in
`Tests/TUIkitIntegrationTests/SwiftUICompatFixesTests.swift`.

---

## 2. Intentional divergence

TUIkit provides the capability but deliberately shapes it differently because of
what a terminal *is*. **Verdict for every item here: keep as-is.**

### 2.1 Interface measurements are `Int`, not `CGFloat`

```swift
// SwiftUI                              // TUIkit
VStack(spacing: 8) { … }                VStack(spacing: 1) { … }
Text("Hi").padding(12)                  Text("Hi").padding(1)
.frame(width: 120, height: 44)          .frame(width: 20, height: 3)
.frame(maxWidth: .infinity)             .frame(maxWidth: .infinity)   // also supported
```

**Why it's intentional / should NOT change:** a terminal addresses whole
character cells — columns and rows — not points on a bitmap. `0.5` of a column
cannot be drawn. Using `CGFloat` would imply a precision the medium does not
have and would push rounding decisions onto every call site. `Int` says exactly
what it means: *N cells*. This is the canonical example of "terminal constraints
require deviation."

**Porting note:** integer literals just work (`.padding(8)`, and
`.frame(maxWidth: 200)`: a frame's maximum is a `FrameDimension`, which an
integer literal is, and `.infinity` is unchanged). What changes is
fractional/`CGFloat` values (`.padding(geo.size.width * 0.1)`); a maximum held
in a variable is `.frame(maxWidth: .fixed(width))`.

A flexible frame sizes itself as SwiftUI's does (measured,
`Tools/SwiftUIProbes/Flexible frames/frame.out`): a maximum fills the offer up
to it, so `Text("Short").frame(maxWidth: 30)` is 30 columns wide offered 80;
a minimum raises the content to it; and an ideal is read only when the frame
is offered nothing, as under `.fixedSize()`, where a frame with a maximum is
its ideal. One difference: offered nothing, a frame with an ideal and **no**
maximum is as wide as its content offered that ideal, where SwiftUI's is
exactly the ideal — TUIkit lays the frame out at the width it answers, and
offered that width such a frame is its content's size. Add a maximum —
`.frame(idealWidth: 20, maxWidth: .infinity)` — for SwiftUI's size. (Until
2026-10-15 a finite maximum only capped the content, and an ideal capped it
whatever the frame was offered, wrapping a long `Text` at the ideal.)

**One sub-point worth knowing (not a defect):** stacks need a *concrete* default
where SwiftUI uses an adaptive system metric. `spacing:` is `Int?` and defaults
to `nil`, as SwiftUI's does, and `nil` is what views prefer between them —
`ViewSpacing` — which in cells is `0` rows in a `VStack` and `1` column in an
`HStack` (terminals are dense; one blank column reads as a word gap, zero blank
rows reads as contiguous lines). A row resolves it per pair of neighbours, as
SwiftUI's does: no column beside a custom `Layout` whose
`spacing(subviews:cache:)` asks for none on that edge (decisions B20). Pass an
explicit `spacing:` for SwiftUI-like air.

### 2.2 Geometry is integer, never `CGPoint`/`CGSize`/`CGRect`/`UnitPoint`

```swift
// SwiftUI                              // TUIkit
.onContinuousHover { … } // CGPoint     .onContinuousHover { … } // .active(CellPoint)
.onTapGesture { /* () */ }              .onTapGesture { … }  // count defaults to 1, as there
.onTapGesture { loc in … } // CGPoint   .onTapGesture { cell in … } // CellPoint, .local or .global
.onTapGesture(count: 2) { /* () */ }    .onTapGesture(count: 2) { … }
DragGesture().onChanged { $0.location } // CGPoint  →  the same source; the location is a CellPoint
```

**Why it's intentional / should NOT change:** the same cell-grid reasoning as
§2.1, applied to composite values. A tap or drag resolves to a `(column, row)`
cell, full stop. Surfacing a `CGPoint` of `Double`s would be a fiction. TUIkit
deliberately exposes integer coordinates (`x`/`y` ints, `DragGesture.Value` with
integer fields). The same goes for `UnitPoint` anchors — there is no sub-cell
anchor to express.

The same rule shapes the layout read-back API: `GeometryProxy.size` is a
`CellSize` of `Int` cells and `frame(in:)` returns a `CellRect`, not `CGSize`
and `CGRect`. A custom `Layout` sees the same pair — `placeSubviews(in:)` takes
a `CellRect`. Two consequences a layout author should know, both of which
floating point used to hide:

- **Divide last.** `let each = total / count` then `i * each` silently drops up
  to `count - 1` cells (2 of them for an ordinary 80-wide, 3-column split);
  `i * total / count` drops none and stays within one cell of even.
- **`CellRect` has no `midX` / `midY`.** A midpoint is fractional whenever the
  extent is odd, so flooring it to a cell *before* subtracting the subview's
  half-size floors twice — and that moves a centred placement by a cell in
  **210 of 861** (extent, size) combinations. `place(in:anchor:proposal:)` does
  the whole thing in one step instead.

A RATE is not a measurement: `ScrollPhaseChangeContext.velocity` is a
`CellVector` of `Double` components, cells per second, the counterpart of
`CGVector` (always `(0, 0)` at a phase change, as SwiftUI reports it, a
terminal's scrolling having no speed to measure). `DragGesture.Value.velocity` is one too: cells per second, estimated from
the times the motion reports arrive.

The one deliberate exception is an **alignment guide's value**, which is
`Double`. A guide is not a size — it is a reference line in a view's own
coordinates (usually inside the view, but SwiftUI uses one outside it as given,
measured 2026-10-11, and so does TUIkit), and it
only ever appears subtracted from another guide before being floored to a cell.
That distinction is load-bearing rather than pedantic: centring a 3-cell view in
10 cells has always placed it at `(10 - 3) / 2 == 3`, whereas whole-cell guides
would compute `10/2 - 3/2 == 4`, because flooring does not distribute over
subtraction. **190 of 820** width combinations would have shifted by a cell.
Fractional guides reproduce today's arithmetic in all 820. The fraction exists
only between two guides and never survives to a coordinate.

### 2.3 Reactive model is `@Observable` only (no `ObservableObject` family)

```swift
// SwiftUI (legacy, Combine)            // TUIkit (and modern SwiftUI)
class M: ObservableObject {             @Observable class M {
  @Published var count = 0                var count = 0
}                                       }
@StateObject var m = M()                @State var m = M()
@ObservedObject var m: M                 // pass via @Environment(M.self) / init
@EnvironmentObject var m: M             @Environment(M.self) var m
@FocusedObject var m: M                 @FocusedValue(M.self) var m
```

**Why it's intentional / should NOT change:** `ObservableObject`/`@Published`/
`@StateObject`/`@ObservedObject`/`@EnvironmentObject` are the older,
Combine-based paradigm. Apple's own guidance is to use the Observation framework
(`@Observable`) for new code. TUIkit is new code with no legacy burden, so it
supports **only** the modern, best-practice path. This is a conscious choice to
keep one clear way to do it, not an omission. (These SwiftUI wrappers are
*not* deprecated — they coexist — so this is a deliberate non-support, not a
"correctly omitted deprecated API.")

**`.onReceive(_:perform:)` follows from this, and from where TUIkit runs.**
Its signature is `onReceive<P: Publisher>(_ publisher: P, perform:)` — the
parameter type IS a Combine protocol, so there is no version of that function
to write on a platform without Combine, and TUIkit's CI builds and tests on
Linux with Windows a port in progress. The three ways to ship it anyway are all
worse than not:

- `#if canImport(Combine)` would make it exist on Apple platforms only. That is
  worse than absent, not better: source that compiles on a Mac would fail on
  Linux, which is the exact portability trap a cross-platform package exists to
  prevent. (The `#if canImport` rule of thumb is for *choosing an
  implementation* of something that works everywhere — ImageIO vs `stb_image`
  — not for making public API appear and disappear.)
- An `AsyncSequence` overload under the same name would be a different API
  wearing SwiftUI's, which the parity rule forbids. The deviations TUIkit does
  take are ones a *terminal* forces; Combine's absence is not one of them.
- Reimplementing Combine is not a terminal UI kit's job.

What replaces it is already here, cross-platform, and is what modern SwiftUI
would use anyway — `task(id:)` with `for await`:

```swift
// SwiftUI, Combine                     // TUIkit (and modern SwiftUI)
.onReceive(model.events) { e in         .task {
    handle(e)                               for await e in model.events { handle(e) }
}                                       }

.onReceive(timer) { _ in tick() }       .task {
                                            while !Task.isCancelled {
                                                try? await Task.sleep(for: .seconds(1))
                                                tick()
                                            }
                                        }
```

The `.task` is cancelled when the view disappears, which is the lifetime
`onReceive`'s subscription had — so the thing being replaced is the spelling,
not the behaviour. For "a value changed", `onChange(of:initial:_:)` already
covers it (§1).

### 2.4 `Image` is text/ASCII; SF Symbols render as glyphs, in narrow circumstances

```swift
// SwiftUI                              // TUIkit
Image("Logo")        // asset catalog   Image(.file("logo.png"))   // rasterised → ASCII art
                                        Image(.url("https://…/x.png"))
AsyncImage(url: url) { … }              AsyncImage(url: url) { … }               // supported, matches
Label("Star", systemImage: "star.fill") Label("Star", systemImage: "star.fill")  // glyph, Apple only
Image(systemName: "star.fill")          Image(systemName: "star.fill")            // the glyph alone
Button("Save", systemImage: "tray") { } Button("Save", systemImage: "tray") { }   // and on the controls
```

**`AsyncImage` ships in all three forms** (`url:scale:`, `content:placeholder:`
and the phased `transaction:content:` with `AsyncImagePhase`). It loads through
the same loader and per-session cache as `Image(.url(_:))`. The success phase's
`Image` carries the decoded pixels, so it draws at once rather than loading
again; a nil URL stays `.empty`; a failure carries the loader's error; and the
phase changes under the given transaction. `scale` is honoured: the loaded
image's natural size is its pixels over `scale`, in points (see "An image takes
its natural size" below).

**`Image.interpolation(_:)` and `antialiased(_:)` ship** as how many samples
each cell's sub-pixels take: `.none` (or `antialiased(false)`) picks the
nearest pixel, `.low`, `.medium` and `.high` average two, three and four. That
is `imageSupersampling(_:)`'s factor, set on the one image and winning over
the environment's. **`renderingMode(_:)`** with `Image.TemplateRenderingMode`:
`.template` draws a raster's pixels in the foreground style (else the
palette's foreground), each at its own alpha, so an icon takes the colour of
the text beside it; a template draws as cells, never as a terminal picture.
A symbol is a single-colour glyph in the foreground already, and stays one.

**`resizable(capInsets:resizingMode:)` ships**, with `Image.ResizingMode`. A
resizable image fills its frame, its aspect not kept, as SwiftUI's does — unless
a content mode around it (`scaledToFit()`, `scaledToFill()`,
`aspectRatio(_:contentMode:)`) keeps it, which is the usual
`.resizable().scaledToFit()` and draws as an `Image` always has. Where SwiftUI's
resizable image takes its natural pixel size on an axis it is offered nothing,
TUIkit's takes the extent its aspect gives it. Cap insets are in the image's
pixels; the caps keep the scale the whole image fits its frame at, and the edges
and centre stretch, or with `.tile` repeat at that scale, between them — where
SwiftUI's caps are in points and its tiles repeat at the natural size (lane
D3M's decisions, B-D3M-3). An image in slices draws as cells, never as a
terminal picture. An image that is NOT resizable takes its natural size, below.

**`Image(_:scale:orientation:label:)`, `Image(decorative:scale:orientation:)`
and `Image(nsImage:)` ship where AppKit exists, in the `TUIkitAppKit` module**
(`import TUIkitAppKit` beside `import TUIkit`; see the `Color` row for why they
are a module of their own), and `Image.Orientation` everywhere, in TUIkit, with
SwiftUI's raw values. The pixels are
drawn out the first time the image is drawn, turned as SwiftUI turns them
(measured on macOS 15), and drawn as any raster is. `scale` is honoured — pixels
per point, so a `CGImage` of 200 × 100 pixels at scale 2 is 100 × 50 points at
its natural size — and an `NSImage`'s natural size is its `size`, in points, as
SwiftUI's is. `label` is accepted for the accessibility a terminal does not have.

> **Deviation — an image made from a `CGImage` or an `NSImage` is named by the
> object's address, and its pixels are read once (decisions B43).** The first
> time such an image is drawn its pixels are read out of the object and kept,
> under a name made from the object's address (with, for a `CGImage`, its
> orientation and scale, and for an `NSImage` its `size`); every later frame
> draws what was read then, and an image of the same object at the same size
> compares equal to it. An `NSImage` given a new `size` is a new image, drawn
> out afresh at that size. The view holds the object for
> as long as it draws it, so a freed address cannot come back as another
> picture under the same name. **What this means for an app:** an `NSImage`
> drawn into in place after it was first shown — `lockFocus`/`unlockFocus`, a
> representation added or replaced — **keeps showing its first pixels**, where
> SwiftUI shows the new ones. Make a new `NSImage` (or `CGImage`, which is
> immutable anyway) for a new picture, and it draws at once. Noticing an edit in
> place would mean reading and hashing the pixels on every frame, at a cost in
> proportion to the picture's size, for every such image on screen.

**`Image(_:bundle:)`, `(_:bundle:label:)` and `(decorative:bundle:)` read an
asset catalogue**, as SwiftUI's do, in the bundle given (the main bundle for
`nil`). `swift build` copies an `.xcassets` folder in a target's resources into
its resource bundle as it is (measured: only Xcode compiles one into
`Assets.car`), and TUIkit reads that on every platform: `Name.imageset`'s
largest picture it can decode, drawn at the scale it was made for, so a `@2x`
file has its `1x` sibling's natural size. A set (an image's or a colour's) has
the name `actool` gives it, measured: `Colors/Brand` inside a folder that
provides a namespace, plain `Brand` inside any other. On macOS, a file that imports
`TUIkitAppKit` also reads a compiled `Assets.car` through AppKit, for a name the
uncompiled catalogue lacks. Failing both, a plain image file among the bundle's
resources: the name as given if it has an extension, else the name with the
first of `png`, `jpg`, `jpeg`, `gif`, `bmp` and `tiff` there. A name not found
draws the missing-file error any raster image draws. An image set's dark variant
is chosen when the image is made, as a named colour's is (see the colour row in
§1).

**A raster `Image` renders as text everywhere, and as REAL PIXELS where the
terminal has a graphics protocol.** The glyph renderer is the universal path and
the one this section used to describe as the only one: a raster source converted
to ASCII/ANSI art with its own controls (`.imageCharacterSet`, `.imageColorMode`,
`.imageDithering`). On a terminal that answers TUIkit's startup handshake — it
will place an image in its **cell grid**, through the Kitty protocol's Unicode
placeholders — the same `Image` draws the actual picture instead, at roughly
fifty times the pixels and in full colour, with no palette quantisation and no
contrast floor. Nothing else changes: the picture occupies the same cells, in the
same place, measured by the same function, so a layout does not move when the
renderer does. Detection is a handshake rather than a host table, and `false` is
the default before one answers, so an unmeasured terminal draws glyphs. TUI-specific
`.terminalGraphics(false)` keeps the glyphs on a terminal that could draw a
photograph, which an app built around a ramp genuinely wants — see
`Documentation/Images and terminal graphics/Terminal graphics protocols.md`.

**An image takes its natural size, where the terminal's cell size in points is
known (decisions B42).** As in SwiftUI, an image that is not resizable is its
size in points — its pixels over its scale, so a pixel a point for a file, a URL
or a bundle resource — whatever it is offered: a 140 × 70-pixel picture in a
terminal whose cell is 7 × 14 points takes 20 × 5 cells, and a frame around it
does not change that. Larger than the space it is given, it is cut to that space
from its top-leading corner (every view's buffer is), where SwiftUI's overflows
its frame and the window cuts it. The cell's size in points is
`\.imageCellPoints`, which the render loop publishes from what the host reports
(`Terminal compatibility survey.md`, "A cell's size in points"): Apple Terminal and Warp
report points, iTerm2 answers `CSI 14 t` in points, and Ghostty reports device
pixels, divided by the main display's scale when the app runs on the Mac the
terminal is on. **Elsewhere — under tmux, which does not forward the cell's
size, over ssh to Ghostty, and on any terminal not measured — it is unknown, and
an image that is not resizable fits what it is offered instead**, which is what
every image did before: aspect-fitted to the size its layout proposes. An app
can state the cell's size for a terminal TUIkit cannot ask with the TUI-specific
`.imageCellPoints(_:)`, and `.imageFitTarget(.proposedSize)` fits an image to
what it is offered on every terminal. `.resizable().scaledToFit()` fits it
everywhere, as in SwiftUI.

Where the image fits (unknown cell, `.proposedSize`, a resizable image): inside
a vertical scroll view the width bounds it, so the image is width driven and
scrolls at full size. Inside one that scrolls horizontally — alone or both ways
— NOTHING bounds it: the content is asked how big it would be if nothing stopped
it. There the image fits the visible viewport, as `.imageFitTarget(.viewport)`
fits it explicitly — on every axis a `.frame` inside the scroll view leaves
open: `.frame(width: 300)` is 300 wide, and its height is the viewport's unless
the frame states one too. (Before that it fitted the scroll view's probe budget,
thousands of cells, and was drawn the size of a wall.)

**`.mono` is the two-tone version of a template image.** `renderingMode(.template)`
(above) draws each pixel in the foreground style at its own alpha;
`.imageColorMode(.mono)` draws a picture in two tones instead, and its ink is the view's `foregroundStyle`, so it is
re-inked wherever `Text` would be (a button's label, a menu row's highlight). Its
paper is the view's `backgroundStyle`, which is TUI-specific: a template image's
unlit pixels are transparent, but a half-block cell's unlit half is the cell's
background colour and has to be stated.

A cell grid still cannot blit a bitmap *itself*, which is why this is a protocol
and not a drawing API: TUIkit hands the terminal an image and a rectangle of
cells, and the terminal draws. Vector glyphs are unchanged — see below.

**`Image(systemName:)` now ships**, reversing what this section used to say. The
old reasoning — an SF Symbol is a character rather than a resizable image, so it
is modelled as text — describes the value correctly and was the wrong conclusion
to draw from it: that a symbol cannot be scaled is a property to document, not a
reason to withhold the spelling every ported SwiftUI file uses. It draws exactly
the glyph `Label(_:systemImage:)` draws, under the same conditions, and the
raster rendering modifiers simply have nothing to do to a character. Where the
symbol would not really draw it renders **nothing** — the honest output, since
there is no title to fall back to, and the one place `Label` still does better:
it can close the gap it was going to leave, and a bare `Image` in an `HStack`
cannot, the spacing being the stack's.

**`Label(_:image:)` and `ContentUnavailableView(_:image:description:)` take an
image resource's name** (a `String`), found as TUIkit's `Image(_:bundle:)`
finds it — an uncompiled asset catalogue, then a plain image file, among the
main bundle's resources — and drawn as a raster
stretched into the two cells a symbol takes, beside the title. Their
`ImageResource` forms stay declined: an asset-catalogue symbol is a build
product a SwiftPM app does not have.

**SF Symbols DO render as glyphs — but only in very limited circumstances.**
`Label(_:systemImage:)` matches SwiftUI's signature, and `SFSymbol.glyph(named:)`
/ `SFSymbol.all` expose the mapping directly. Each SF Symbol lives in the
Plane-16 Private Use Area, so it renders **only** where a font supplies its
glyphs: an **Apple platform**, in a terminal using a font that has them
(**Terminal.app with SF Mono**, with the **SF Symbols font installed** — not the
default). Everywhere else — Linux, or a terminal without the font —
`Label(_:systemImage:)` shows just its title, `Image(systemName:)` shows nothing,
and `SFSymbol` resolves nothing, so code stays correct; the glyph simply appears only where it can. The name →
codepoint table is Apple's own, extracted deterministically from the SF Symbols
app (`Tools/GenerateSFSymbols`), and the Private-Use width/advance is handled the
same way as VS-16 emoji. See `SFSymbol` for the full rules.

**The `systemImage:` shorthand now reaches the controls**, not just `Label`:
`Button(_:systemImage:action:)` and `Button(_:systemImage:role:action:)`,
`Toggle(_:systemImage:isOn:)`, `Picker(_:systemImage:selection:content:)` and
`ContentUnavailableView(_:systemImage:description:)` (Sources/TUIkitFoundation/SFSymbols/
`ControlSymbolInitializers.swift`). Each builds the `Label` the long spelling
would have built, so each inherits the fallback above whole — an unresolvable
name renders the plain control, byte for byte, which is what the tests pin
rather than a glyph they could only see on some hosts. `Button` is the one that
had to adapt: it is not generic over its label, so the icon reaches it through
the composed-label path (`ButtonStyleConfiguration.labelView`) rather than the
string one — the two paths are otherwise interchangeable, which that byte
comparison now also proves.

**The `image:` spelling of all five stays out**, and is the clearer half of the
rule: it names an `ImageResource` — a constant Xcode generates from an asset
catalogue, whose value is a bitmap at some scale factor. A terminal app has
neither the catalogue nor anywhere to put a bitmap, so there is no honest
implementation, and by the second of the two rules at the top of this document
the call must therefore fail to compile rather than quietly drop the icon.
Recorded as a family in `parity-map.json`. The difference
between the two spellings is exactly the difference between a *character* and a
*picture*: an SF Symbol is the former, and that is the whole reason it can be in
a terminal at all.

### 2.5 `colorScheme` is a read; the write is `palette` / `appearance`

```swift
// SwiftUI                              // TUIkit
@Environment(\.colorScheme) var scheme  @Environment(\.colorScheme) var scheme  // supported, matches
.environment(\.colorScheme, .dark)      .environment(\.colorScheme, .dark)      // supported, matches
.preferredColorScheme(.dark)            .palette(SystemPalette(.blue))     // View or Scene — colour roles
                                        .appearance(.rounded)              // border/figure set
.tint(.blue)                            .tint(.blue)                       // supported, matches (→ §1)
```

**What matches:** `\.colorScheme` asks whether the surroundings are light or dark,
and TUIkit answers it. The answer is the palette's: the page it paints where that
can be measured, what the terminal reported about its own colours (OSC 11, a
`CSI ?997` theme report, `COLORFGBG`) where the palette leaves the page to the
terminal, and `.light` where nothing said anything — nothing is assumed white and
nothing is assumed dark. Writing it pins the scheme for the subtree exactly as
SwiftUI's does, and the pin survives a `.palette(_:)` or `.tint(_:)` written
inside it, because it is re-applied wherever a palette enters the environment.
TUIkit's named colours do not yet adapt to it (→ the Color values row in §1).

**Why the write half is intentional / should NOT change:** a terminal's "look" is
a set of colour roles and box-drawing styles, not a light/dark bitmap theme, so
the thing a view sets is a whole palette. The `.palette` (colours) and
`.appearance` (border/figure style) model maps cleanly to that reality and themes
the window's bars too; `preferredColorScheme(_:)`
stays absent, since what it would ask for is one of those palettes. SwiftUI's
`.tint(_:)` itself **is** supported and matches — it overrides the accent role for
the subtree (§1); `.palette` is the broader, TUI-only superset (§4c).

**One stated limit.** Chrome drawn outside the content — the status bar, the app
header, a modal's backdrop — renders against the app's ROOT palette, so a
`\.colorScheme` pin (or a `.palette`) written inside content does not reach it,
just as a SwiftUI subview's pin does not reach its window.

### 2.6 Toolbars are the window's bars; commands are its menu bar

```swift
// SwiftUI, and TUIkit: the same source, the same meaning
NoteList()
    .navigationTitle("Notes")
    .toolbar {
        ToolbarItem(placement: .primaryAction) { Button("New") { … } }
        ToolbarItem(placement: .status) { Text("3 notes") }
    }
```

`.toolbar { … }` — both forms, `ToolbarItem` (with and without `id:`),
`ToolbarItemGroup` (with and without a `label:`, the labelled one collapsing to
its label — a pull-down of its views — when the bar has no room for them, as
SwiftUI's collapses into a menu), `ToolbarSpacer` with `SpacerSizing`, `DefaultToolbarItem`,
`ToolbarContent` and its builder, `ToolbarContent.hidden(_:)` — joins the
**window's** bars, as on
macOS: items declared anywhere in the page go to the bar their placement names.
The window has a top bar (above the page) and a bottom bar (below it), and each
exists while something is placed in it:

| Placement | Where |
|---|---|
| `.navigation` (`.topBarLeading`) | top bar, leading |
| `.principal` | top bar, centre, in place of the title — iOS's reading; macOS centres it and says nothing of the title |
| `.automatic`, `.primaryAction` (`.topBarTrailing`) | top bar, trailing |
| `.secondaryAction` | top bar, trailing, before the primary items |
| `.confirmationAction`, `.cancellationAction`, `.destructiveAction` | top bar, trailing (macOS's reading outside a sheet) |
| `.bottomBar`, `.status` | bottom bar — `.status` takes iOS's reading, where macOS centres it in the window toolbar |
| `.keyboard` | bottom bar, leading, while a text input inside the view that declared it has the focus — where SwiftUI puts it above iOS's keyboard or in a Mac's Touch Bar |
| `.accessoryBar(id:)` | an accessory bar: a row of the top bar under its own items, one per identifier, in the order first placed — macOS's accessory bars under the toolbar |

**Every bar is opt-in.** An app that places nothing in a bar draws on every row
of the terminal. TUIkit's line of key hints is a view the app places in the
bottom bar — `ToolbarItem(placement: .bottomBar) { StatusBar() }` — and the
keys it names work whether or not it is shown: an item's action fires on its
key with no bar, and `q` quits. (The bare `a` and `t` keys, which cycle the
appearance and the theme, are opt-in with their items,
`statusBarSystemItems(theme:appearance:)`.) There is no `.appHeader`: what it
held is a `.navigationTitle` and `.toolbar` items.

The window's title is the outermost `.navigationTitle` drawn: the page's own
over a navigation stack's inside it, a pushed screen's when the stack is the
page, never a sheet's. Outermost by ancestry — a title inside another's view
never displaces it, nor does the inner of two stacked on one view
(`.navigationTitle("Inner").navigationTitle("Outer")` shows Outer, as SwiftUI's
outer preference writer overrides its content), and of two side by side the
later one stands, however each is wrapped. Its subtitle is the title's own — a
`.navigationSubtitle` on the titled view or inside it, never an outer view's
while the title has its own — else the outermost on a view round the title,
else one beside it, as SwiftUI combines a sibling's preference upward
(`VStack { Text("H").navigationTitle("Notes"); List(…).navigationSubtitle("3 notes") }`
shows both): the one that stands as a title does, the later of two side by
side, among those no other title would take — not one on another titled view,
inside one or round one (`HStack { Detail().navigationTitle("Inbox").navigationSubtitle("42 unread"); Inspector().navigationTitle("Info") }`
shows Info alone). TUIkit's rule; SwiftUI's window
title has no published one to measure against. It is shown in the top bar's
title slot when there is a top bar; a title alone draws no bar. An item is drawn
in the environment of the view that declared it (a `.disabled` above
`.toolbar` disables it), keeps its `@State` from frame to frame — at its place
in the declaration, as a view in a `ViewBuilder` does, so an `if` before it
moves nothing, and two `.toolbar`s on one view keep their items apart — and its
controls are in the Tab ring after the page's, in a section of their own that
never takes the first focus; their keyboard shortcuts are live. While a modal
holds the input both bars are inert — no focus, keys or clicks — except the
line of key hints, which shows the modal's keys and answers a click as the key;
an item declared inside a `.dimmed()` subtree is drawn inert likewise. A view
nobody sees declares nothing: a `NavigationStack`'s root, while a screen is
pushed over it, has no items in the bars and hides no bar — and its items keep
their `@State` meanwhile, as the root does, to come back to. The screen pushed
over it puts its top-bar items in the stack's own bar (the A28 study's step 7,
as iOS's navigation bar holds a pushed view's items): the leading ones after
the trail or ‹ Back, the principal, secondary and primary ones at the bar's
trailing end, the title truncated to what they leave, all on the bar's one
row and in the screen's focus section; its bottom-bar items, accessory bars and
title menu stay the window's, and the root's items are the window's top bar's,
as on macOS (decisions TOOLBARS, B-TOOLBARS-2). In a terminal too
short for both bars the bottom bar stays whole and the top bar gives way, cut
from its end; a cut bar lays its items out in the rows of it that show, and one
that shows none of them draws no item — none is in the Tab ring or on its key
while nobody can see it — keeping their `@State` for when the terminal grows.

**In a sheet** (and a full-screen cover, and a popover) the window's bars are
inert while it holds the input, so a `.toolbar` in its content draws its items
itself, round the view it is attached to, live in the sheet: the modal actions
and the bottom bar's placements in a button row below that view —
`.destructiveAction` leading, `.cancellationAction` then
`.confirmationAction` trailing, the confirmation item the default button
(Return) and the cancellation item the cancel button — and the other
placements in a row above it (decisions D5C, B-D5C-3). Escape presses the cancel
button, as on macOS: the sheet's own Escape runs its action in place of
dismissing, so a Cancel that discards a draft discards it, and the status bar
names the key `⎋ cancel` — unless a `dismissalConfirmationDialog` would ask,
when Escape asks first.

**The one difference that follows from the medium:** the page is what declares
the items, and a bar's rows have to be known before the page is laid out, so a
bar that appears or goes does so **one frame after** the page that changed it
(the frame between is drawn at the old reservation). An app that declares its
bars once, at its root, never sees it.

**One style for every bar**, set on the scene with SwiftUI's
`.windowToolbarStyle(_:)`: `.automatic` and `.expanded` box the bars,
`.unified` rules each on the page's side, `.unifiedCompact` draws the items
alone; the `showsTitle:` forms say whether the top bar's title slot shows the
title. (A TUIkit addition, `windowToolbarStyle(_: any WindowToolbarStyle)`,
takes a style chosen at run time, since a scene's modifiers cannot be chosen
with an `if`.)

`.toolbarVisibility(_:for:)` takes a bar away (`.windowToolbar` or
`.automatic` the top bar, `.bottomBar` the bottom one) — a frame later, like
any change to the bars; the statement closest to the content wins, a
hidden line of key hints leaves its keys working, and a hidden bar's items keep
their `@State` for when it comes back.
`.toolbarBackground(_:for:)` fills a bar with any style — a colour, a gradient
laid across the bar, a `Material` blended over the page — in place of the
palette's surface for it (`appHeaderBackground`, `statusBarBackground`), and
`.toolbarBackgroundVisibility(.hidden, for:)` draws it with no fill, on the
page's own background; both this frame, since a fill costs no rows, and the
statement closest to the content wins. (The deprecated
`toolbarBackground(_: Visibility, for:)` is not there; its replacement is.)
`.toolbarRole(.editor)` keeps the title at the top bar's leading end beside
the `.principal` items, as macOS and iPadOS draw an editor's toolbar;
`.automatic` is TUIkit's default, iOS's reading, where a principal item takes
the title's place. `Scene.windowToolbarLabelStyle(fixed:)` and
`windowToolbarLabelStyle(_:)` (a binding — `ToolbarLabelStyle` is `Codable`
here, a TUIkit addition, so `@AppStorage` keeps it) draw every `Label` in the
window's bars' items as `.iconOnly`, `.titleOnly` or `.titleAndIcon` says,
under any `.labelStyle` the item states itself; `.automatic` leaves the
items as declared, and `\.toolbarLabelStyle` reads the scene's choice.
`.toolbarItemHidden(_:)` hides one view of a toolbar item — a `ControlGroup`'s
button, a `ToolbarItemGroup`'s view — which closes up round it and keeps its
`@State`; outside a toolbar it changes nothing, as in SwiftUI.
`ToolbarTitleMenu { … }` in a `.toolbar`, or `.toolbarTitleMenu { … }`, makes
the top bar's title a pull-down of its items, opened and walked as a `Menu`
is; it is the title's, so it goes where the title goes (not under a principal
item, nor with the title removed). SwiftUI fills `ToolbarTitleMenu()`'s menu
from the app's commands; TUIkit's commands are in the menu bar, and the empty
form leaves the title as it is (decisions TOOLBARS, B-TOOLBARS-1).
`.contentToolbar(for: .tabViewSidebar) { … }` puts a row of views at the foot
of a `.sidebarAdaptable` tab view's sidebar, under its tabs and footer, above
its bottom bar (iPadOS's placement, which macOS marks unavailable; TUIkit reads
it on every platform). `ToolbarContent.sharedBackgroundVisibility(_:)` is
honoured as it stands: a terminal bar sets no glass under a group of items,
so every item already sits on the one background they share, the bar's fill.

`ToolbarPlacement.accessoryBar(id:)` names an accessory bar to the bar
modifiers: `.toolbarVisibility(.hidden, for:)` takes that row away (hiding
`.windowToolbar` takes the top bar's own row and leaves its accessory bars; the
menu bar's Hide Toolbar takes them all), and `.toolbarBackground(_:for:)` fills
it.

**Customizable toolbars** (`toolbar(id:content:)`, the A28 study's
customisation family, built as its option 1): the person customizes the items
made with an identifier — `ToolbarItem(id:)` — from the top bar's context menu
(a right-click on it, or Shift+F10 while one of its items has the focus) or the
menu bar's View ▸ Customize Toolbar… (`ToolbarCommands`). A panel hangs from the
bar listing every such item, those out of the bar included, each with a
checkbox that puts it in the bar or takes it out and ▲ ▼ that move it among the
others — Tab, Space and Return, or the mouse — then the label styles where the
scene bound its `windowToolbarLabelStyle(_:)` (the context menu offers them
too, and the choice is written back through the binding), Reset and Done.
`customizationBehavior(.reorderable)` items cannot be taken out and
`.disabled` ones cannot be moved either; `defaultCustomization(.hidden)` starts
an item out of the bar, and `options: .alwaysAvailable` keeps one the person
removed in an overflow menu, the `»` at the bar's trailing end;
`CustomizableToolbarContent.hidden(_:)` keeps an item out of the bar whatever
the person chose, listed in the panel as SwiftUI lists it. What the person
chose is kept per identifier in the app's default storage, under
`"TUIkit Toolbar Configuration <id>"`, as macOS keeps a toolbar's
configuration; items move among the others of their own placement (decisions
TOOLBARS, B-TOOLBARS-4).

**Commands are the window's menu bar** (TUIkitCommands; refusal review O13,
ruled 2026-10-12). A scene that declares commands — `.commands { … }` with
`CommandMenu`s and `CommandGroup`s, or a `Commands` type of the app's own —
has a menu bar on the window's top row, above the top bar; one that declares
none has no bar and loses no row. Its menus are SwiftUI's on macOS, in
SwiftUI's order (measured on macOS 15.8, the main menu read back: session
notes `O13/probe/menus.swift`): the standard menus that have something in
them (the app's, File, Edit, Format, View, Window, Help — a `CommandGroup`
goes into its placement's), with the app's own menus between View and
Window in order of declaration. The scene's own commands are TUIkit's: the
app's menu, titled with the process's name, holds About (an alert with the
name, and the version where the app is bundled) and Quit (the quit key's quit,
so it quits when `q` would, whatever has the focus); the Edit menu holds Undo
and Redo (the app's `UndoManager`, showing the scene's undo keys) and Cut, Copy
and Paste (⌘X, ⌘C and ⌘V, through `commandKey`, to the focused view).
With a `Settings` scene the app's menu holds Settings…, presenting it, and
shows its key: ⌘, through `commandKey`, or the one the scene's
`.keyboardShortcut` states, bound whether or not the app shows a menu bar; and
an app
that can have more than one window open — more than one window scene, or a
window group that `openWindow` or its key opens more windows of — has a Window
menu: Close Window (the
window in front, when another is open behind it), the open windows with the
one in front checked (choosing one brings it to the front), and the `Window`
scenes (choosing one opens it, and showing its `.keyboardShortcut`). All the scenes' commands count, in declaration
order, whichever window is in front (decisions O9, B-O9-9).
Each item whose key acts on
the focused view presses that key once the menu has closed and handed the
focus back. SwiftUI's opt-in sets fill the View and Edit menus:
`SidebarCommands` (Show/Hide Sidebar, the split view's ⌃⌘S), `InspectorCommands`
(Show/Hide Inspector, ⌃⌘I), `ToolbarCommands` (Hide/Show Toolbar, ⌥⌘T, the
person's choice over the page's top bar; no Customize Toolbar…) and
`TextEditingCommands` (Find…, a `TextEditor`'s ⌃G); `TextFormattingCommands`
waits for the rich-text editor (A42) and `ImportFromDevicesCommands` is
declined, both failing to compile. Within a group the newest `before:` comes
first, the `after:`s follow, and the last `replacing:` stands; commands on
several modifiers add up, the innermost first, and `commandsRemoved()` and
`commandsReplaced(content:)` take away what is inside them, keeping what is
outside. A command's keyboard shortcut works whether its menu is open or not,
and a command reads the focused view's `@FocusedValue` as of the frame its key
is pressed after: the bar is drawn after the page. A click on a title opens its
menu, F10 opens the first (fn-F10 on a Mac keyboard; a page's own handler of
F10 is asked first), and Alt with a title's first letter opens that menu where
the terminal sends Option as Meta
([Terminal compatibility survey.md](<../Terminals/Terminal compatibility survey.md>),
"The menu bar's keys"); with one open, Left and Right step to the menus beside
it, F10 and Escape close it, and the page has its focus back. An open menu is a
pop-up `Menu`'s, its rows and keys the same, hung from its title over the whole
window; the titles are not in the Tab ring. Two differences, each a choice:

- **The page's own shortcut on a command's key is asked first**, as AppKit
  sends a key equivalent to the key window before the main menu (documented;
  UNMEASURED in SwiftUI, whose probe could not make its window key here).
- **While a modal holds the input, the menu bar is inert** and no command's
  key fires from behind it, as the top bar is inert; the modal's own keys
  still work.

In a terminal too short for every bar, the bottom bar keeps its rows, the
menu bar its one, and the top bar gives way.

`.toolbar(removing: .sidebarToggle)` takes away `NavigationSplitView`'s own
toggle handles (the ◀ on the leftmost divider and the ▶ edge column), and any
sidebar button a bar draws for it. As on macOS, the window's top bar shows a
sidebar button at its leading edge for the window's first split view (one per
window, as SwiftUI measured with two split views side by side and nested,
2026-10-14) — but only where the page already has a top bar: a bar is three
rows of a terminal's, and the split's own ◀ and ▶ already do the job. `.toolbar(removing: .title)`
(refusal review O17, 2026-10-14) empties the top bar's title slot while the
view is drawn, anywhere in the window but in presented content, as one
toolbar's title item serves the whole window in SwiftUI. A `NavigationStack`'s
bar over a pushed screen then draws its Back button alone, without the
screen's title, subtitle or trail, when the modifier is in that screen or on
or above the stack; a collapsed `NavigationSplitView`'s bar does the same. The
window's title, the outermost `.navigationTitle`, is unchanged: only the
title item goes.

`DefaultToolbarItem(kind:placement:)` (refusal review O17, 2026-10-14) places
an item the framework draws by default in the bar its placement names:
`.search` the window's `searchable` field, with its scopes, instead of at the
top of the view the modifier modifies (the first searchable drawn is the
window's; a second one's field is drawn nowhere while the item claims the
first's), and `.title` the window's title and subtitle, instead of in the top
bar's title slot. Declared inside the searchable's content the field moves a
frame after the item is first declared, as a bar appears a frame after its
first item; the first frame of all has it. The field in a bar is a Tab stop
after the page's, as every bar item is. `.toolbar(removing: .search)` draws the
field nowhere, on or above the searchable or anywhere else in the window. In a
sheet, a cover or a popover a `DefaultToolbarItem` draws nothing and moves
nothing. `.sidebarToggle` (2026-10-14) draws a button that toggles a split
view's sidebar as ⌃S does, ◀ while it shows and ▶ while it is hidden: the
split's whose column it is declared in, else the window's first split view. A
split whose sidebar button is drawn leaves out its ▶ edge column, which the
button stands in for; the ◀ on its divider stays, as it also says the column
can be hidden. Several split views can each have one, declared in their own
columns. TUIkit's own `DefaultToolbarItem(kind:placement:label:)` (2026-10-14)
names the panel a button works — with a sidebar on each side, two arrows alone
do not say which is which: the button draws the label after its arrow, and its
text names the panel in the button's help and key hint and in the key hints of
the panel's own handles ("hide Folders"). TUIkit's own `.contentColumnToggle`
(2026-10-16, owner's ruling on FB8) is a three-column split's second button,
which the top bar draws beside the sidebar's by default: SwiftUI's split view
has the sidebar's alone and reaches detail-only by dragging. The two are coupled
so that every state is one press from any other and none leaves the sidebar
showing with no content column after it — a sidebar whose choices change
nothing on screen: the content button goes `.all`/`.doubleColumn` → `.detailOnly`
→ `.doubleColumn`, and the sidebar button's `.detailOnly` → `.all` opens both.

### 2.7 Fixed-`frame` default alignment is `.topLeading`, not `.center`

```swift
// Both compile; result differs:
Text("hi").frame(width: 20, height: 3)   // SwiftUI: centered    TUIkit: top-left
```

**Why it's intentional / should NOT change:** SwiftUI centres content in a larger
fixed frame because that's the GUI norm. A terminal reads from the top-left, and
in practice a fixed `.frame(width:)` is used to build a **left-aligned,
fixed-width column** — a label gutter, a channel slider, a table cell — far more
often than to centre something in slack space. Defaulting to `.center` would
silently shift every such column and surprise TUI authors. The default is
therefore `.topLeading`; pass an explicit `alignment:` when you do want centring.
(`ColorPicker`'s per-channel `Slider.frame(width:)` relies on this left
alignment.) The *flexible* `frame(maxWidth:…, alignment:)` **does** default to
`.center`, matching SwiftUI, because slack-space distribution is exactly when
centring makes sense.

### 2.8 Lazy stacks window the render; `List` is the scalable container

SwiftUI's lazy stacks (per the `LazyVStack` docs and the WWDC26 session
"Dive into lazy stacks and scrolling") are defined by **deferred view
creation inside a scroll view**: "the stack view doesn't create items until
it needs to render them onscreen"; scrolled-off views are released after a
few updates; the stack's main-axis extent is *estimated* ("based on the
average size of views that have been placed before, and the estimated number
of remaining subviews") and corrected as real views scroll in; the ideal
cross-axis size is **that of the first subview**; and `pinnedViews:` pins
section headers/footers.

TUIkit re-renders the tree every frame and retains no view objects, so
"creation cost" *is* render cost — and its lazy stacks are a **render
window**, not a deferred-creation machine:

- **Standalone** (the stack itself is the clipping container), they are
  genuinely lazy: children render top-down until the next would overflow
  `availableHeight`; the first child that does not fit whole renders
  **clipped at the cell** — the same overflow picture as the eager
  stacks — and children past it are *never rendered* (so their
  `onAppear`/`task` correctly never fire; a child that would show zero
  lines is not rendered either). A saturated stack measures as the
  limit, exactly as `VStack`'s `min(total, proposal)` does — a
  row-boundary measure used to end the natural-extent ladder early and
  silently truncate scrollable content.
- **Inside a `ScrollView`** (as the *direct* content), a `LazyVStack` now
  **windows to the visible viewport**: the ScrollView publishes its scroll
  slice and the stack renders only the rows intersecting it (into a
  full-height buffer, off-window rows blank), so `onAppear`/`task` fire on
  visibility — matching SwiftUI's model rather than materialising everything.
  A `LazyVStack` nested *below* other scroll content (not at the content
  origin) is left un-windowed — as is one alone inside an outer lazy stack,
  which takes the window itself — measured and drawn over every row, as a
  `VStack` is, so its cost per frame tracks its row count, including in a row
  of an outer lazy stack that is out of sight. A `Section`'s header and
  footer do not make a stack nested: the
  section passes the window to its content moved below its header
  (`ScrollWindowRelay`), so `ScrollView { Section("Feed") { LazyVStack { … } } }`
  windows, answers `scrollTo` and `.scrollPosition`, holds an
  `.anchorPosition(.row)` and fires `onAppear` on visibility, as the stack
  alone does. Other wrappers that draw lines around their content at its own
  identity — `.padding(.vertical)` — do not relay it yet, and a lazy stack
  under one bands itself as if it were all the content: the scroll view takes
  the stack's height for the content's, and End stops short of the stack's
  last row and the padding below it.
- **Cross-axis sizing** hugs the widest *placed* child (identical to
  `VStack`), which is stabler than SwiftUI's first-subview ideal — TUIkit
  has rendered every visible child anyway, so it knows the real width.
  "Placed" is what a walk at the given height budget reaches: a stack asked
  how wide it would be in eight lines answers for the rows those eight lines
  hold, not for one far below the fold. A **windowed** stack cannot measure
  every row (that is the point), so beyond the fold this is a heuristic — the
  widest row it has ever drawn or sampled — but it is one heuristic, shared by
  every path that answers, and it never shrinks while the content is
  unchanged. It is also never narrower than the band on screen: a stack
  scrolled to a wide row reports that row, however narrow the first rows are.
- **`pinnedViews:`** (`LazyVStack(alignment:spacing:pinnedViews:content:)`,
  `PinnedScrollableViews`) pins by SwiftUI's rule, measured on macOS 15.8: a
  `Section`'s header stays at the top of the enclosing vertical `ScrollView`
  while its section's content is under it, and the content's last row pushes
  it up and away, so the next section's header takes over; a footer stays at
  the bottom, never above its section's first row. Only the stack's own
  sections pin — written in its content or made there by a `ForEach`, however
  wrapped — not one inside a row's `VStack`; outside a scroll view nothing
  does. The stack keeps its own sections whole, where any other container
  takes a section apart, and each lays its views out as the stack lays out
  its children — `spacing` between the header and the first row too, as
  SwiftUI spaces them (measured) — but centres a centre-aligned section's
  views within the section's widest view, which can be a cell from where the
  stack would centre them (decisions D1S); a modifier on such a section wraps
  it whole, where SwiftUI applies it to each part. It holds wherever the stack sits in the content — under a title,
  where it is not windowed, as well as on every windowed path — because the
  stack does none of it (below). **Deviations:** SwiftUI's header is
  transparent, so rows show through it wherever it draws nothing; a cell
  holds one glyph, so here the header's cells replace the rows' glyphs
  across the section's width and keep only their background. Under the "N
  more above / below" lines (`.scrollIndicatorStyle(.text)`) a pinned header
  sits below the line, as a revealed control does, rather than under it. A
  control in a pinned header is clicked where it is drawn, and a key it takes
  does not scroll the view back to the section's top. Focus moving onto a
  row scrolls it clear of a pinned header or footer, as it scrolls it clear
  of the "N more" lines: under the header it would be focused and unseen.
  A programmatic seek does not: as in SwiftUI, `scrollTo(_:anchor: .top)`
  puts the row at the viewport's top edge, under a pinned header (measured).
  `LazyVGrid(pinnedViews:)` pins the same way: a grid lays a section out as
  SwiftUI does — the header spanning the columns, the views from the row
  below, the footer spanning after them — so it is a section of its own
  around a grid of its views, in a pinned stack. `LazyHStack(pinnedViews:)`
  and `LazyHGrid(pinnedViews:)` pin by the same rule turned on its side, as
  SwiftUI's do (measured): a section lies along the row, its header pinned
  to a horizontal scroll view's leading edge while its content is under it
  and pushed off by its last column, its footer to the trailing edge. A
  focused column is not kept clear of a pinned header, as a row is in a
  column.

**Practical guidance (differs from SwiftUI's):** for large scrollable data
sets use `List` — its row materialisation is windowed to the viewport
(O(visible) row boxes per frame, id resolution included), making it TUIkit's
actual lazy container. Reach for `LazyVStack`/`LazyHStack` when the *stack
itself* is the clipped region (a fixed-height pane showing "as many whole
rows as fit"). Viewport-driven lazy rendering inside `ScrollView` now works
for a `LazyVStack` that is the direct scroll content; the remaining gap
(windowing a stack nested below other content) is → §4a.

---


**How `pinnedViews:` works, and why the stack does none of it.** A pinned
header has to be drawn at the viewport's top while its section scrolls under
it, and the three things that takes live in three places:

1. **Where the header is.** Only the `Section` knows which of its lines are
   header: it draws header, content and footer into one buffer — kept whole
   for it, where any other container takes a section apart — laid out as the
   stack lays out its children. So a section that is one of a pinned stack's
   own (the stack hands its rows a scope in
   the environment entry every stack replaces for its own children, so a
   section in a row's `VStack` never sees it) emits a copy of its header as a
   floating layer marked with a **scroll pin** — the edge it pins to, and how
   far it may travel: until its bottom meets the content's last row.
2. **Where the viewport is.** Only the `ScrollView` knows its offset. Every
   combining operation already carries a floating layer to it at the right
   place, however deep in the content the section sits — under a title, in a
   windowed band, in an off-band graft — so the scroll view, once its offset
   is final, draws each pinned copy at its edge, as far as its section
   reaches, and takes the layers off the buffer.
3. **What a pinned header covers.** Drawn over the rows after windowing,
   without changing any height (anything that changes height while scrolling
   reflows the content under the cursor): it replaces the rows' glyphs, runs
   and fades, and their hit regions, and takes its own controls' regions with
   it, so a reveal finds a focused control in it on screen. A drop-down opened
   from a pinned header moves with it. A layer that reaches the screen root
   having passed no vertical scroll view is dropped there: the flow already
   draws it where it belongs.

The windowing paths are untouched: the section whose content is under an
edge is always in the band, because its rows are.

### 2.9 What clips a floating layer, and how far a spring may carry a view

A view's drawing can leave its own bounds three ways here: `.offset`,
`.position`, and — since a spring goes past its target — a `.move` / `.offset`
transition on the way back. All three become a **non-opaque overlay layer**:
the view's own cells, floating, with nothing painted where they came from
(a terminal has no transparency, so an in-place blank box would erase the page).

**Which containers clip one is SwiftUI's answer, not a terminal's.** Two doc
comments in the SDK settle it:

- `View.clipped(antialiased:)` — a view's bounding frame is used only for
  layout, and content extending beyond it is still visible. So a plain
  `VStack`/`HStack`/`ZStack` clips nothing, and neither does `.frame()` alone.
- `View.scrollClipDisabled(_:)` — a scroll view clips its content to its bounds
  by default, which is what the modifier exists to turn off.

TUIkit clipped **none** of them until now: `.offset(x: 7)` on a row of a
12-wide `ScrollView` painted three columns of the page beside the viewport, and
the same row in a 14-wide `List` painted over the list's own right border. Both
now clip a layer that is a piece of their content (`ScrollView`'s viewport,
`List`'s bounds), and a plain stack still does not. **`.clipped(antialiased:)`
ships** to ask for the same cut on any view: the layers that are pieces of its
content stop at its frame, while presentations draw whole; `antialiased` changes
nothing, a cell's edge being whole.

**`.scrollClipDisabled(_:)` ships (2026-10-05, decisions A10).** A `ScrollView`
under it stops cutting its content's floating layers at the viewport, and draws
the content's own lines past it — above and below, and either side of a
horizontal one — as layers floating around the view, with their hit regions:
all of an eager stack, the band a lazy stack draws (the viewport's rows for a
uniform one, a row past each edge for the others; SwiftUI's lazy stack drew
nothing past its viewport in the same probe). A line draws from its first
painted cell to its last — a glyph, or a space styled to show (a background,
reverse video, an underline, a strikethrough or an overline), or a cell an
animated run repaints, such as a caret: the unstyled blank cells at either end
draw nothing and take no click, so a short, centred or indented line neither erases what is
beside it nor takes that view's clicks, and a fade on the content fades only
the cells it draws. **Stacking is SwiftUI's (measured on macOS
15):** the overflow draws at the scroll view's own place in the order — over the
views before it, under the views after it — so a sibling below the scroll view
hides what runs down into it, and what runs up over the sibling above is drawn
over that sibling. Every layer is composited at the screen root, over the
in-flow content, which gives the first half; the second is a rule in the
combining operations (`appendVertically`, `appendHorizontally`, `composite`):
content placed after a scroll view cuts that scroll view's overflow away beneath
the cells it paints (`OverlayLayer.isOverflow`,
`FrameBuffer.coverOverflow`) — on each line, from its first painted cell
to its last — at the cost of one test of a hint per operation (a buffer knows,
after each combining step, that its layers hold no overflow). The
blank space either side is not the later view drawing: a short `Text` padded to
its stack's width, an overlay's `Spacer`, or an `.offset` view's empty footprint
hides nothing, as in SwiftUI, and a click on the overflow drawn there is the
overflow's (decisions-D2S B-D2S-5). No other layer is cut, so
a view's own `.offset` drawing keeps the stacking it has always had: over the
views after it as well. `List` and `Table` keep clipping (decisions-D2S
B-D2S-4), where SwiftUI's `List` on macOS honours the modifier (measured). So
do the scrollers the framework puts around a tall menu, a sheet's or a framed
container's content: their content belongs inside the surface they scroll in,
and a 60-item inline menu inside a `.scrollClipDisabled()` page drew its items
over its own border and the page; the scroll views in that content still
answer the modifier. A clipping scroll view pays one environment read a frame
for it.

**Drawing past a region at a view's place in the order** is the same
mechanism, generalised (decisions-GUIDE B-GUIDE-3, 2026-10-12): a single child
that its explicit guide places outside its `.frame`, `.overlay` or
`.background` draws the part the region holds in it and the rest as overflow
layers, marked and cut under later content exactly as a scroll view's are,
with the hit regions of the cells they draw, and under what the view itself
floats (an `.offset` mark on it); so does a `.move` or `.offset`
transition's overshoot (below). Nothing that fits its region pays anything:
the placement asks whether the view fits (four comparisons) before it splits
anything. What clips still clips: a `ScrollView` cuts these layers at its
viewport, `.clipped()` at its frame, a `List` inside its border, a `Table` at
the cell, and a surface — a menu, a popover, a sheet, a dialog — at its edge
(decisions-GUIDE3 B-GUIDE3-3). A border, a box's divider and its footer are
drawn over what the content draws past its region, and past the border it is
still drawn, as SwiftUI's border is (measured; decisions-GUIDE3 B-GUIDE3-5).
Content larger than its frame or base is still
cut to it, as before (decisions-GUIDE3 B-GUIDE3-1).

**A presentation is not content and is never clipped.** A menu, a drop-down, a
dialog or a toast is a window over the page — the layer flag is `isOpaque` —
and clipping one to the scroller its trigger happens to sit in is how a picker
on the last visible row loses every option. SwiftUI does not clip its
presentations to a scroll view either.

**Overshoot.** `Animation.bouncy` peaks at phase **1.0460**, `.snappy` at
**1.0063**, `.smooth` at exactly 1 (measured from `Animation.fraction(at:)`).
Past 1, `.move`/`.offset` transitions draw the view a whole number of cells
clear of the slot it has just arrived in; `.opacity` and `.scale` stop at 1,
because nothing is more opaque than opaque and `.scale` uncovers a buffer that
IS the slot. There is deliberately **no minimum**: `.snappy`'s 0.13 of a cell
across a twenty-cell view draws no bounce, matching a SwiftUI snappy that does
not visibly bounce, and a bounce needs a panel eleven rows tall before it moves
vertically at all.

Ordering is **relative**, and the compositor already expressed it: layers sort
by level (`popover < modal < alert < notification`), so an overshoot on the page
draws under a dialog, while an overshoot INSIDE that dialog rides in the
dialog's own buffer and is drained in the next compositing pass — above it. The
only thing that had to be added was a sub-level ordinal: displaced drawing takes
a negative `zIndex` (`OverlayLayer.displacedDrawingZIndex`), so a bouncing label
sorts under an anchored drop-down at the same level instead of under whatever
the tree happened to emit first. No case of the level enum moved.

**Inside the overshoot** the view is drawn as content past its region is
(2026-10-12, decisions-GUIDE B-GUIDE-3): at its own place in the stacking
