order, over the views before it and under the views after it, as SwiftUI draws
a view past its frame (measured for a frame, an overlay and a scale effect),
where it used to float over every sibling. Its hit regions travel with the
cells it draws; a later sibling cuts the picture, and its regions, away
beneath the cells it paints, so a peer drawn over the bounce keeps its clicks
(the reason the regions used to stay at the slot). Only the cells it paints
are drawn: the unstyled blanks at either end of each line are left out, so a
slot-width row of padding erases nothing as it passes. Two deviations remain,
because a cell grid is not a compositor: blanks INSIDE a line still paint (a
`Spacer` between two labels covers what is under the gap while the bounce
lasts), and a view bouncing at the screen edge loses the columns that fall off
it rather than sliding back on, as a menu would. A bounce inside a container
that keeps its content inside it — a `List`, a `Table`'s cell, a sheet — stops
at its edge.

**While a view is arriving** (phase 0…1) the slot still clips it, which is a
deviation: by the `clipped(antialiased:)` rule above, SwiftUI would let it
cross its siblings. Here crossing them means *erasing* them, one glyph per
cell, so the slot is the boundary until the view has arrived.

### 2.10 `VStackLayout` / `HStackLayout` / `ZStackLayout` default alignment is `.leading` / `.top` / `.topLeading`, not `.center`

```swift
// Both compile; result differs:
VStackLayout { Text("ab"); Text("abcdefgh") }   // SwiftUI: "ab" centred    TUIkit: "ab" flush left
```

**Why it's intentional / should NOT change:** SwiftUI's three `Layout`-value
stacks default `alignment` to `.center` — `init(alignment: HorizontalAlignment
= .center, …)`, `init(alignment: VerticalAlignment = .center, …)` and
`init(alignment: Alignment = .center)` — exactly matching `VStack` / `HStack` /
`ZStack`. TUIkit's own `VStack`, `HStack` and `ZStack` keep that `.center`
default too; only these three `Layout`-value spellings diverge, defaulting to
`.leading`, `.top` and `.topLeading` respectively. **Owner's decision
(2026-09-20):** "Yes, we deliberately diverge on this one, to fit more
naturally into the terminal environment" — the same top-left-reading argument
§2.7 makes for a fixed `.frame`'s default, extended here to the three
`Layout`-value stacks (§2.7 itself is about `.frame` alone and does not, on its
own, cover these). Pass an explicit `alignment: .center` (or `.top` /
`.topLeading`, as the type needs) to get the SwiftUI default.

One consequence worth knowing: `AnyLayout(isWide ? HStackLayout() :
VStackLayout())` — the line the type exists for (§1, Custom layout) — is not a
silent drop-in for switching between `HStack` and `VStack` themselves if the
call site relied on the centring they default to; add an explicit `alignment:`
if it did.

### 2.11 A plain click on the selected row CLEARS a single selection, where macOS keeps it

```swift
// Both compile; result differs:
Table(people, selection: $selected) { TableColumn("Name", value: \.name) }
// click "Mei", then click "Mei" again —
//   SwiftUI: selected == mei.id   (a plain click never deselects)
//   TUIkit:  selected == nil
```

`List(selection:)` with a `Binding<SelectionValue?>` is the same code path
(`ItemListHandler.toggleSelectionAtFocusedIndex`, `.single`), so this applies
to both containers.

A selection that cannot be emptied — `List(selection:)` with a plain
`Binding<SelectionValue>` — has no "nothing selected" to get back to, so there a
re-click (or Space) on the selected row leaves it selected, as SwiftUI's does.

**Why it's intentional / should NOT change:** SwiftUI inherits the
`NSTableView` convention, where a plain click always selects the row it lands
on and never deselects — clearing a selection is command-click's job. **Owner's
decision (2026-09-21):** the second half of that convention is unavailable
here. Command-click does not come through in many terminal clients, so a port
that copied only the reachable half would ship a single-selection list with no
easy, obvious way to get back to "nothing selected" at all. Toggling on a
re-click is worth more than the convention when the convention's escape hatch
cannot be reproduced.

**Multi-selection does not diverge**, and that asymmetry is deliberate rather
than an oversight: with a `Binding<Set<…>>`, a plain click assigns
`[clickedID]` — it ensures the clicked row is selected and clears every other
row, exactly as macOS does — because there the standing-in modifiers do reach
us. Ctrl-click and option-click both toggle a single row, the terminal-visible
stand-ins for command-click (no terminal reports the command key; see
`Terminal compatibility survey.md`), and shift-click selects the span from the anchor,
re-pivoting around it like Finder. So the macOS rule is followed in full
wherever it is reproducible in full.

The keyboard path is unaffected in both modes: Space toggles at the focus
cursor, which is a good terminal affordance and is also what makes the
single-selection divergence a small one — the mouse is simply being given the
same way out that the keyboard already has.

Pinned by `MultiSelectionClickTests.plainClickReplaces` (the macOS rule,
including a click on an already-selected row),
`MultiSelectionClickTests.singleModeTogglesByDesign` (the divergence itself,
on the mouse path where it lives) and `ItemListHandlerTests.singleDeselect`
(the keyboard toggle it borrows its behaviour from).

### 2.12 A shape's unit is square: a column across, the same down

```swift
// SwiftUI: points                        // TUIkit: square units
Circle().frame(width: 100, height: 100)   Circle().frame(width: 10, height: 5)     // round
RoundedRectangle(cornerRadius: 8)         RoundedRectangle(cornerRadius: 2)        // two columns, one row
Circle().stroke(lineWidth: 2)             Circle().stroke(lineWidth: 1)            // one column wide
```

**Why it's intentional:** a shape is coordinates, and a cell is about twice as
tall as it is wide. In cells a circle drawn by `path.addEllipse(in: square)`
would come out twice as tall as it is wide; in terminal pixels the numbers are
large and, where the terminal does not report its cell size, wrong by up to
2.3×. So `path(in:)` is handed a rectangle `columns` wide and `rows ×
imageCellAspect` tall — the cell's height over its width, about 2, reported by
the terminal (where it is not, 2) — and a ported shape, computed from
`rect.midX` and `rect.width * k` as most are, keeps the proportions it was drawn
for. Ruled by the owner, refusal review O0 (B), 2026-10-12. Literal lengths —
`lineWidth`, `cornerRadius`, `dash`, an `offset` — are then in columns, the same
deliberate choice `.padding(20)` is (§2.1): a ported `lineWidth: 2` is two
columns, so most ported strokes want `lineWidth: 1`, the default.

**Layout stays in cells.** The frame a shape fills is laid out like any view,
and `Shape.sizeThatFits(_:)` answers in TUIkit's layout terms: a `ProposedSize`
of cells in, a `ViewSize` out (the `Layout` spelling, §2.2), filling whatever it
is offered unless a shape says otherwise.

**Porting note:** a shape of your own names `CGRect` and `CGPoint`, which are
Foundation's; TUIkit does not re-export Foundation, so such a file adds `import
Foundation` (SwiftUI re-exports it). On Linux and Windows `CGAffineTransform`,
`CGLineCap` and `CGLineJoin` are TUIkit's own, declared where CoreGraphics is
absent.

## 3. Open divergence

### A modifier on multi-view content reaches the members through some wrappers, not yet all

SwiftUI states this rule for the two types whose whole purpose is to carry a
modifier for several views. `Group`: "The modifier applies to all members of the
group --- and not to the group itself." `GridRow`: "If you apply a view modifier
to a row, the row applies the modifier to all of the cells... if you apply the
`border(_:width:)` modifier to a row, SwiftUI draws a border on each cell in the
row rather than around the row."

**What SwiftUI actually does, measured.** The rule above is about paint in
Apple's wording, so it was checked against the real framework with an
`NSHostingView` probe rather than inferred. Layout distributes: `.frame(width:
80)` on a two-member `Group` produces frames byte-identical to writing the
frame on each member separately, `.padding(20)` pads each member, and a
`Spacer` inside a MODIFIED `Group` still reaches the enclosing stack as a
spacer. A lifecycle effect does **not** distribute: `.onAppear` fires once on a
two-member `Group` and once on a `ForEach` of three, not per member. So the
rule is per member for anything cosmetic, spatial or environmental, and once
for anything that performs an effect — which is why closing this is a judgement
per wrapper rather than a blanket conformance. A blanket one would leave every
layout test green while turning one `.onAppear` on a fifty-row `ForEach` into
fifty calls.

TUIkit does that for two kinds of wrapper today.

**Through `ModifiedView`**, which forwards `ChildViewProvider` (and
`GridRowProviding`) when its content is one, re-wrapping each resolved member in
the same modifier. In the framework that is **four** `BufferModifier` types —
`.padding(_:)`, `.background(_:)`, `.id(_:)` and `Link`'s internal hyperlink
modifier — plus everything an app writes with `.modifier(_:)`, which is the
whole point of that spelling.

**Through `SingleContentWrapper`**, for a wrapper that is not a `BufferModifier`
at all. A `BufferModifier` is a VALUE, so `ModifiedView` can carry it to each
member; a bare wrapper has no such value and must call its own initialiser,
whose content type is its generic parameter and so cannot be bound to an
`any View`. `SingleContentWrapper` is that initialiser call, written once per
wrapper and generic over the new content, which is what lets the caller's
existential be opened rather than converted. A wrapper that conforms picks up
child resolution and grid rows with an empty
`extension X: ChildViewProvider where Content: ChildViewProvider {}`.
**Nineteen** wrappers conform, in three families whose equivalence is provable
or measured rather than assumed:

- *Environment publishers* — `EnvironmentModifier` (`.environment(_:_:)`),
  `TransformEnvironmentModifier`, `ObjectEnvironmentModifier`,
  `_StyleEnvironmentView` (`.foregroundStyle`, `.backgroundStyle`),
  `ThemeModifier`, `TintModifier`, `StyleCascadeModifier`, `RedactionScope`,
  `DisabledModifier`, `_AnimationValueModifier`, `_ValueScopedTransactionView`.
  A value reaches a subtree whether it was set one level up or two, so
  publishing around each member is the same value in the same places.
- *Paint* — `_OpacityView` (`.opacity`), `_ColorEffectView` (`.brightness`,
  `.grayscale` and friends), `DimmedModifier`, `_HiddenView`,
  `_HitTestingView`, `AnimatedCellsModifier`. These rewrite cells the content
  already drew, and members of a stack do not overlap.
- *Geometry* — `FlexibleFrameView` (`.frame`), `OffsetView` (`.offset`).
  Measured: `.frame(width: 80)` on a two-member `Group` gives frames
  byte-identical to writing the frame on each member.

Content with NO members gets nothing, which is the rule's other edge and the
one that bites: an `Optional` that is `nil` — an `if` whose condition is
false — has no members, so a `.frame(height: 5)` written on it keeps no rows,
and nor does one on a `Group` whose only content is such an `if`. SwiftUI
agrees, measured with the same `NSHostingView` probe (macOS 15.8, 2026-09-24):
in a `VStack(spacing: 0)` of two `Text`s, `(nil as Text?).frame(height: 50)`
and `Group { nil }.frame(height: 50)` each add 0 to the stack's 32-point
height, while `VStack { nil }.frame(height: 50)` adds 50. A slot that must
keep its space whether or not its view is there frames a container that is
always there, with the `if` inside it; the Example's Animation page ("Keep the
space") is written that way, and was not until this distribution reached
`.frame` and took its reserved rows away.

**`EmptyView` does not follow it, and there the two diverge.** SwiftUI treats
`EmptyView` as it treats the `nil`: `EmptyView().frame(height: 50)` adds 0 in
the same probe, and so does `EmptyView().padding(20)`. In TUIkit `EmptyView`
is a view that draws nothing rather than content with no members — it is not
a `ChildViewProvider` — so a frame on it is an ordinary `FlexibleFrameView`,
whose minimum height keeps its 5 rows, and a padding on it pads its empty
buffer as `PaddingModifier` pads any other: `EmptyView().padding(2)` is 4
blank rows, the two insets (it drew 3 until 2026-09-24, keeping back a row
for content that draws nothing, though it measured 4). Left open rather than closed: making `EmptyView` flatten to
nothing would change it in every container that counts what it is given,
and the framework gives a modifier on one a meaning of its own —
`EmptyView().overlay { … }` draws the overlay whole (`OverlayModifier`).
Nothing in the framework or the Example writes a frame or a padding on an
`EmptyView` itself; write a kept slot as a stack, as above, and it is right
in both. Pinned by
`EmptyContentLayoutTests`, which holds the halves that match beside the two
that do not.

**Every other wrapper is still opaque to child resolution** — `.badge` builds
`BadgeModifier`, `.position` builds `PositionView`, `.tag` builds `_TaggedView`,
and so on. The scale is dozens, not a handful — walking
every `extension View` factory that returns `some View` and taking the first
`View`-conforming type each body builds names 85 distinct types, which is an
upper bound rather than a total: a few of those (`Text`, `Image`, `ForEach`,
`AnyView`) are the content a factory builds rather than a wrapper it puts
around `self`. Through any of them the members arrive at the enclosing
container as ONE opaque child, which the `TupleView` beneath has already
stacked vertically with no spacing. So

```swift
HStack { Group { Text("A"); Text("B") } }                       // "A B"
HStack { Group { Text("A"); Text("B") }.foregroundStyle(.red) } // "A B"  (conforms)
HStack { Group { Text("A"); Text("B") }.badge(2) }              // A over B
```

— a purely cosmetic modifier rotates the row 90° — and a `GridRow` carrying one
stops being a row, leaving the grid's columns to be laid out by `GridRow`'s own
fallback `HStack`.

It is the same forwarding decision `.alignmentGuide(_:computeValue:)` and
`layoutValue(key:value:)` are still waiting on — they are read off the outermost
view, which is why each is documented as "apply it as the outermost modifier".
`.zIndex(_:)` is no longer among them: single-content wrappers forward its
static witness, so a z-index survives any number of them. The difference is that
a z-index is dimension-INDEPENDENT while a guide is a closure evaluated against
the dimensions the view laid out at, so reading a guide through a wrapper would
resolve it against the outer, wrapped size. The judgement per wrapper is not
only about effects: `presentationDetents(_:)` and the other presentation traits
are deliberately read through a wrapper list of their own, and a trait must not
be handed to each member of a group.

`GroupRenderTests.modifiedGroupKeepsTheRowAxis`,
`GroupRenderTests.modifiedGroupKeepsStackSpacing` and
`GridTests.modifiedRowKeepsTheLattice` now assert the rule directly for
`.foregroundStyle`; they were `withKnownIssue` until it forwarded.

### A child with no extent takes no stack spacing

SwiftUI charges a stack's spacing on both sides of every child that is a view,
whatever its size. Measured with the `NSHostingView` probe above (macOS 15.8,
2026-09-24): a `VStack(spacing: 10)` of two `Text`s is 42 points; an empty
`VStack { if false { … } }` between them makes it 52, and so does
`Color.clear.frame(height: 0)`. A `nil` or an `EmptyView` in the same place
adds nothing, because neither is a child at all.

TUIkit's stacks charge spacing only between children that take a row (a
column, in an `HStack`) or are spacers — `totalLinearSpacing(occupiedChildren:
spacing:)`, which counts what the render charges: it appends a zero-height
child without a gap, and the measure has to agree with it. So the empty stack
adds nothing here, exactly as the `nil` does. Pinned by
`EmptyContentLayoutTests.emptyStackTakesNoSpacing`.

The Example's Animation page leans on it. Its slot, with the space not kept,
is an empty stack between two siblings of a `VStack(spacing: 1)`, and the page
closes up completely as the panel goes; under SwiftUI's rule a blank row
would be left where the panel was. Matching SwiftUI means charging spacing to
every child except one that is nothing. A `nil` already contributes no child;
an `EmptyView` does, and would have to be told apart from a view of no size,
which a child's measured size cannot do.

Under an animation the empty stack's place moves with the gap it gives back
(a row, in the Example), and a view leaving it would drift with it. It does
not: a container holds a child that has come to draw nothing where it was
while the change plays, and places one that has come to draw something
directly, so the view leaves where it was and arrives where it lands
(decisions [B-FB16-13](<Decisions/Insertion and removal model (FB16).md#b-fb16-13-a-stack-emptied-by-a-removal-stands-where-it-was-while-the-change-plays>)).

### Closed: `foregroundStyle` takes `some ShapeStyle`

How this one fell is the fifth instance of the pattern §4b warns about, so it
stays on the record.

The claim was that the only thing a `Color?` parameter lost was "non-colour
`ShapeStyle`s — gradients, materials — which are bitmap concepts that don't
render in cells". Less of that held than it seemed: `Shader` genuinely has no
meaning in a cell grid, and is still absent **as a type**, so a ported shader
fails to compile rather than compile and do nothing; a `Material` turned out to
have one (a translucent surface, below). But a gradient is not a bitmap concept. It is a function from a
position to a colour, and a cell has a position — so `t` is answerable per cell
and the ramp is drawable. The row described a real terminal limitation
accurately and then used it to withhold a spelling that did not depend on it.

`foregroundStyle<S: ShapeStyle>(_:)` and `background<S: ShapeStyle>(_:)` now
take any style, with `@_disfavoredOverload` `Color` twins so `.foregroundStyle(.red)`
and `.background(.palette.accent)` still infer (Apple marks `tint` the same
way). What ships:

| | |
|---|---|
| `ShapeStyle` | the protocol, conformable — `resolve(in:)` with public defaults, as since macOS 14. SwiftUI's underscored `_apply`/`_makeView` are omitted: they are over types that exist only inside SwiftUI |
| `Gradient`, `Gradient.Stop` | source-identical, `location` a `Double`. A bare `Gradient` as a style is a **vertical** linear gradient, measured |
| `LinearGradient`, `RadialGradient`, `EllipticalGradient`, `AngularGradient` | all four, with their `colors:`/`stops:` twins and the `.linearGradient(…)` static-member spellings |
| `MeshGradient` (`BezierPoint`, `Locations`, `Colors`) | every initialiser and stored property; each cell takes the mesh's colour at its centre, and on a graphics terminal a mesh behind blank cells is a picture. The rules SwiftUI draws by — Coons patches, bisector control points, limited Hermite smoothing, the cone space for `.perceptual`, premultiplied alpha — are measured and set out below the table |
| `AnyShapeStyle` | erasure — the runtime style-choice idiom |
| `AnyGradient`, `Color.gradient` | a colour with a little depth in it: `.foregroundStyle(.blue.gradient)`. A derived gradient carries its BASE colour and works its stops out at paint time, because `Color.palette.accent` is a reference with no palette to resolve against until something paints. The lightening is `+0.15` HSL lightness with hue and saturation held — measured against SwiftUI's own renderer and recorded in the API docs, along with the one row it misses (black) |
| `Color.mix(with:by:in:)` | a two-stop gradient evaluated at the fraction, in whichever space you name — default `.perceptual`, as SwiftUI's is |
| `Gradient.ColorSpace` (`.device`/`.perceptual`), `Gradient.colorSpace(_:)`, `AnyGradient.colorSpace(_:)` | both return `AnyGradient`, as SwiftUI's do. **Additive beside them**: the space is also stored on `Gradient` (`init(stops:colorSpace:)`, `init(colors:colorSpace:)`), because in SwiftUI a space can ONLY ride on `AnyGradient` — measured: `LinearGradient(gradient:startPoint:endPoint:)` refuses one — and a terminal's gradients are overwhelmingly directional. SwiftUI's own default asymmetry is kept rather than tidied: a bare `Gradient` is `.device`, `mix` is `.perceptual` |
| `ShapeStyle.opacity(_:)`, `.in(_:)` | `.in(_:)` takes a `CellRect` (§2.2) |
| `Color: View`, and the four gradients too | a style used where a view goes fills the space it is offered — flexible in both axes with a minimum of zero, so it claims slack and never demands any. A bare `Gradient` is deliberately not one, as in SwiftUI |
| `backgroundStyle(_:)`, `background()`, `BackgroundStyle` (`.background`) | one names the surface, the other paints it; with nothing named it is the palette's own background, a cell grid's answer to the system background material. `ignoresSafeAreaEdges:` is omitted — a terminal has no safe area for it to talk about: `safeAreaInset` lays its bar beside the view rather than growing one (decisions B6), so nothing ever runs under a bar for a background to extend beneath |
| `Material` (`.ultraThin` … `.ultraThick`, `.bar`, and the `.thinMaterial`-style spellings), `MaterialActiveAppearance`, `materialActiveAppearance(_:)` on a material, a style and a view, `\.materialActiveAppearance`, `\.backgroundMaterial` | a palette surface at a fraction of its opacity, composited cell by cell over what it covers: what shows through is tinted, and the glyphs behind stay. **No blur** — a cell has nothing below it to blur (refusal review O5 (b), Deliberate deviations C5). Thickness is opacity, `.ultraThin` 0.35 to `.ultraThick` 0.92; `.bar` is the bars' surface. A material that does not appear active is opaque; `.automatic` and `.matchWindow` follow `\.appearsActive`. Nothing of TUIkit's sets `\.backgroundMaterial` |
| `Glass` (`.regular`, `.clear`, `.identity`, `tint(_:)`, `interactive(_:)`), `glassEffect(_:in:)`, `DefaultGlassEffectShape` | a material's surface in the shape, behind the view — `.regular` the regular material's, `.clear` the ultra-thin one's, a tint that colour — with a **rim**: the shape's outline in the border's ink lifted (the tint's, tinted), drawn in the appearance's lines (a Braille outline under `.braille`, a band under `.block`, none under `.blank`; a circle or a path of your own its rasterized border). Layout is untouched and the rim sits in the frame's outermost cells, showing through the view's blank ones, so pad the view as SwiftUI's examples do: `.padding().glassEffect()` is `╭────╮` / `│Flag│` / `╰────╯`, one row `( … )`. **No blur** (Deliberate deviations C11). Interactive glass lifts its rim under the pointer and takes no click; tinted glass is the surface its content's ink lands on. Glass that does not appear active is opaque, as a material is. `.identity` draws nothing. `GlassEffectContainer(spacing:content:)` (spacing in cells, an `Int?`) draws glass of one kind that touches, overlaps or comes closer than its spacing — and every member of a `glassEffectUnion(id:namespace:)` — as one shape: the smallest rectangle round them, in the first one's shape, from the pass they come together; a glass in a scroll view, a clip, a hidden view or an opacity below 1 inside it draws itself. In a container, glass arriving under an animation fades in by itself, joining the glass beside it once arrived (`glassEffectTransition(_:)`: `.materialize`, and `.matchedGeometry`, whose growing out of a nearby glass is not drawn; `.identity` at once), and `glassEffectID(_:in:)` keeps a glass that moves to another view from arriving again (decisions GLASS) |
| `shadow(color:radius:x:y:)`, `ShadowStyle` (`.drop(color:radius:x:y:)`, `.inner(color:radius:x:y:)`) | a shadow in whole cells, cast by the cells the view paints — its glyphs, and the cells it gives a field of its own — moved `x` columns and `y` rows; it darkens what it falls on by compositing the colour (black at a third, as SwiftUI's) over it, glyphs kept. `radius` is an alpha falloff across that many cells, `1 / (radius + 1)` less a cell, round at the corners, and a radius past 32 cells is drawn as 32 (a shadow costs the square of its radius). A drop shadow is behind the view, hidden where it paints a field and showing where it leaves one clear; past its frame it is drawn at the view's place in the stacking order, over the views before it and under the ones after, and a `ScrollView` clips it as SwiftUI's does. Layout is untouched. **The units are cells**: a ported `radius: 8` is eight cells, as `.padding(8)` is (refusal review O6 (b), Deliberate deviations C10). `ShapeStyle.shadow(_:)` — the one consumer of a `ShadowStyle`, and so the only route to `.inner` — waits on shapes (decisions O6) |
| `HierarchicalShapeStyle` (`.primary` … `.quinary`), `HierarchicalShapeStyleModifier` (`.red.secondary`), `foregroundStyle(_:_:)` and `(_:_:_:)` | the content levels. With no foreground style above, they are the palette's tiers (`foreground`, `foregroundSecondary`, `foregroundTertiary`, `foregroundQuaternary`, and `.quinary` that at half strength), which each palette picks to stay legible. Under a `foregroundStyle`, they are that style at macOS's label-colour strengths (1, 0.5, 0.26, 0.1, 0.05), composited at the cell. The two- and three-style forms name the second and third levels outright, and a single-style `foregroundStyle` below clears them again. The extra styles reach whatever draws in `.secondary`/`.tertiary`; a terminal glyph has one layer, so there is no palette-mode symbol for them to colour as well (decisions B22). Over a raised background — `\.backgroundProminence` of `.increased` — the levels are the palette's tiers whatever style is above, the inks a list's row fills are chosen to carry |
| `BackgroundProminence` (`.standard`, `.increased`), `\.backgroundProminence` | a `List` and a `Table` raise it for the content of their focused, selected row (the cursor row of a focused list or table, when it is selected), so a row of the app's own — or a table's view cell — can read it, and the content levels resolve to the palette's tiers there. A list row the list builds lazily — every `ForEach` and `OutlineGroup` row — is raised; a hand-written row beside them is handed to the list already drawn and is not (decisions B73) |
| `ForegroundStyle` (`.foreground`), `TintShapeStyle` (`.tint`), `SelectionShapeStyle` (`.selection`), `SeparatorShapeStyle` (`.separator`), `FillShapeStyle` (`.fill`), `PlaceholderTextShapeStyle` (`.placeholder`), `LinkShapeStyle` (`.link`), `WindowBackgroundShapeStyle` (`.windowBackground`) | the roles, resolved where they are drawn: the foreground style in force (else the palette's), the tint in force (else the accent) for both `.tint` and `.selection`, the palette's border, field surface, tertiary foreground (a field's prompt colour), accent (a `Link`'s colour) and page background |
| `ShapeStyle.red` … `.black`, `.clear` | the named colours as shape-style statics, as SwiftUI declares them, so `.foregroundStyle(.red, .blue)` infers |
| `View.gradientExtent(_:)` | **TUI-specific**, and the reason this was worth doing: SwiftUI has no way to run one ramp across a *set* of views, and a terminal list wants exactly that |

Layering a fill behaves as it does in SwiftUI: `ZStack { Color.red; Text("hi") }`
draws the letters on the red. A cell's glyph and the colour behind it are two
statements, and an overlay cell that names no background has said nothing about
the field it lands on, so it keeps the one that is there; a cell that names its
own background keeps that instead. "The one that is there" is the one under
that cell: a label laid over two colours is drawn on each, as SwiftUI draws it
(since 2026-09-25; before, a whole overlay row took the colour under its first
cell — `Opacity as composition.md` §99). Compositing is otherwise opaque per
cell — blank cells paint, which is what dialog interiors and the modal dim
depend on.

Two deviations, both stated in the API docs. `RadialGradient`'s radii are `Int`
cells like every other dimension here (§2.1), and — because a cell is about
twice as tall as it is wide — a radius is measured along the **horizontal**
axis with the vertical derived through `imageCellAspect`, so a circle looks
like one. `EllipticalGradient` is the geometry that follows the box's own
shape, and takes no such correction for the same reason.

One spelling stays out, because a terminal cannot honour it and the rule is
that what cannot be honoured must not compile:

- **`Text.foregroundStyle<S>` returning `Text`.** A gradient on a `Text` works
  — it returns `some View` through the `View` modifier. What cannot be had
  is a gradient on one fragment: `Text("a").foregroundStyle(gradient)` is a view,
  not a `Text`, so `+` refused it, and interpolating it takes the catch-all
  `appendInterpolation(_:)`, which writes a value's description, as SwiftUI's
  (deprecated) catch-all does. A ramp given to the
  whole text now bands across its fragments, cell by cell, so the
  original reason ("a run carries one colour") no longer holds; what still
  does is that a run's stored attributes name ONE colour, and a ramp on one
  FRAGMENT would need a paint per run rather than a style per run. Until that
  exists, the spelling that would quietly lose the gradient is the one that
  fails. (`Text("a").foregroundStyle(.secondary)` therefore takes `Color`'s
  `.secondary` — the palette tier — rather than the level of a style above.)

Two used to stay out with it and no longer do. `HierarchicalShapeStyle` was
declined because a second `.secondary` beside `Color.secondary` was expected to
make `.foregroundStyle(.secondary)` ambiguous; it does not, since the `Color`
overload is the disfavoured one, exactly as in SwiftUI. And
`foregroundStyle(_:_:)` was declined as painting a symbol's second layer, which
a cell does not have — but the levels are also what `.secondary` below resolves
to, which a terminal honours fully (decisions B22).

`MeshGradient` **ships** (it was declined here as "not worth it at 80×24";
the Wave 17 review, P11, found that wrong — a mesh is a function from a
position to a colour, exactly as the four gradients are). All of SwiftUI's
surface: the `points:`, `bezierPoints:` and `locations:` initialisers with
`colors:` or `resolvedColors:`, `MeshGradient.BezierPoint`, `.Locations`,
`.Colors`, and the stored properties, settable. It is a style and a view: a
cell takes the mesh's colour at its centre (a staircase on a glyph terminal),
and an opaque mesh used as a view, or behind blank cells, is a picture where
the terminal draws them, as the other gradients are. Every rule was measured on
macOS 15.8 by reading back SwiftUI's pixels — meshes painted with colours that
encode each pixel's place in the mesh give the patches' shape directly — and
the answers are not the obvious ones: a patch is a **Coons patch**; an implied
control point lies a third of the way to its neighbour along the line that
**bisects** the vertex's two edges (not Catmull–Rom, not straight thirds); a
smooth blend is per-channel Hermite with Catmull–Rom slopes that are **zero at
the mesh's edges and at a peak, and limited** so a monotone run never
overshoots; `.perceptual` decides those slopes in OKLab's **cube-root cone
space**, not in OKLab itself; translucent colours blend **premultiplied**; and
`background` fills what the patches leave uncovered. A malformed mesh (fewer
than two vertices across, or a point count other than `width × height`) draws
its background alone, and a missing colour is `.clear`, as in SwiftUI. Inside
`withAnimation` a mesh's vertices slide and its colours and background fade,
each vertex's numbers on their own animation entries as a gradient's stops are;
SwiftUI's does the same (measured: a column moved at even steps, its colours
stepping exactly as a `LinearGradient`'s stops beside it), and a mesh that
changes shape snaps.

---

## 4. No overlap

### 4a. SwiftUI has it · TUIkit should add it

Possible in a terminal, just not built yet. **Verdict: add over time**, roughly
in this priority order. (Proposals are one-liners; trade-offs noted where
non-obvious.)

| Feature | Why it matters | Design sketch / trade-off |
|---|---|---|
| **Presentation: `popover`, `fullScreenCover`, `presentationDetents`** | Common modal patterns. | **All shipped.** `popover(isPresented:attachmentAnchor:arrowEdge:content:)` is an ANCHORED presentation, not another centred sheet: a bordered panel beside the view that presented it, over an undimmed page, closed by Escape or a click outside — the same presentation `.contextMenu` and the `Picker` drop-down use, with your content instead of menu rows. `arrowEdge` picks the side (a terminal draws no arrow); `attachmentAnchor` takes `.rect(_:)` with an `Anchor<CellRect>.Source` — `.rect(.bounds)`, the default, centres it on the view, `.rect(.rect(_:))` on a rectangle in the view's cells, and it flips around that rectangle — or `.point(_:)`. Where an app writes a `.compact` size class it is a sheet instead, as on iOS, or what its content's `presentationCompactAdaptation(_:)` / `(horizontal:vertical:)` asks (`PresentationAdaptation`: `.automatic` a sheet, `.none` and `.popover` the popover, `.sheet`, `.fullScreenCover`; the vertical class's answer where both are compact — decisions A21). `fullScreenCover(isPresented:onDismiss:content:)` fills the content area, dims nothing (there is nothing showing through) and cannot be dragged. `presentationDetents(_:)` / `(_:selection:)` size a sheet: `.medium`, `.large`, `.fraction(_:)`, `.height(_:)` in **Int rows** (a terminal has no `CGFloat`), and `.custom(_:)`, whose `CustomPresentationDetent` answers in rows too from a `PresentationDetent.Context` (`maxDetentValue`, the content area's height, and any environment value by dynamic member) — `nil` makes it unavailable, so it is neither the smallest nor a selection that sticks. `PresentationDetent` is a struct with static members, as SwiftUI's is, which is what lets an app's own type be one; the fraction is floored, once. The height belongs to the sheet's own **box** — SwiftUI's sheet is a card with its own material, so its detent is always visible as that card, whereas a terminal sheet has no material and the content IS the sheet, so the outermost container in it (`Dialog`/`Panel`/`Card`/`.border()`) renders that tall and encloses the slack as empty interior. Fillable content (a `List`, a `Spacer`) fills it as its available height; two boxes stacked in a `VStack` or side by side in an `HStack` share it, as flexible views share a stack, and boxes in a `ScrollView` are their content's size along the axes it scrolls and take the sheet's size across them — a vertical scroll view's boxes span the sheet's width — as SwiftUI's are (macOS 15.6 measured: two `GroupBox`es in a scroll view in a `.form` sheet 400 points tall are 43 points each) — in a `ZStack`, a `Grid`, or a lazy stack or lazy grid outside a scroll view each box still takes all of it (decisions D2C, B-D2C-5); content with no box keeps its own size, since there is nothing to stretch. A detented sheet rests on the **bottom edge** of the content area, centred across it, as on a phone, and is not dragged by its border; detents that a short terminal floors to one height are one. Read off the sheet content's **outermost** view — the same rule as `.alignmentGuide`, and for a harder reason: the detent IS the height being rendered into, so it has to be known before the subtree renders, which rules a preference out. A sheet with two detents or more has a **grabber** (`presentationDragIndicator(_:)`, decisions A19): a rule across its top edge, a `VSplitView` divider between the sheet and the page with `.userResizable`'s handle in its middle, inside the detent's height. It is a focus stop after the sheet's controls — ↑ and ↓ move the sheet to the next detent, ↖ and ↘ (Home, End) to the ends where the terminal sends them (not yet measured on every host — Terminal compatibility survey.md; ↑ and ↓ always arrive), ↩ and Space round them — and a mouse drags it, snapping to the nearest detent as it moves rather than on release (iOS moves freely and snaps on release; one relayout per detent crossed is what a docked live sheet can afford). A step is written through the `selection:` binding when there is one; without one the sheet keeps it, and opens at the smallest, SwiftUI's own initial state — as does a `sheet(item:)` presented for another item, which is another sheet (its sticky size, its drag and a live sheet's hold on the focus start over with it). `.automatic` and `.visible` draw it where there are two heights to move between — a single detent has nowhere to drag to, so `.visible` draws nothing there (decisions-D7S B-D7S-1) — and `.hidden` takes the rule, the stop and the drag away together, leaving the binding. **A live page** (`presentationBackgroundInteraction(.enabled)` / `.enabled(upThrough:)`, decisions A19, design C): the sheet docks at the bottom of the content area as a band across its whole width (its box takes the width, at every detent, unless the content names a `presentationSizing` — `.fitted` is a card centred in the band; decisions-D7S B-D7S-3), and the page is laid out in the rows above it (down to the rows the sheet draws, where its content has no box to fill the detent), live — **a deviation from iOS, where the page keeps its height under the sheet**: a terminal cannot draw a live page under a sheet without Tab and the arrow keys reaching what it hides, so the page is shortened as a terminal resize would shorten it (`GeometryReader` and `containerRelativeFrame` see the shorter height; a lazy stack's rows `onAppear`/`onDisappear` as the sheet moves). Tab crosses between the page and the sheet; `.keyboardShortcut`, the framework's own chords (a split view's sidebar chord) and the claim on Escape are the pane's the focus is in (a key equivalent the sheet does not bind reaches the page's — not while an alert in the sheet holds the keyboard; Return and Escape do not — decisions-D7S B-D7S-2), and Escape on the page is the page's. Above `upThrough`, where the page would keep fewer than three rows or the sheet have fewer than three, for an undetented sheet, for one presented from inside a modal sheet, a cover or a popover (decisions-D7S B-D7S-4), and for one presented from a lazy row that the shortened page leaves out of its window (it comes back as a modal, for as long as it stays presented, through an `item:` sheet's switches too; a presenter undrawn for any other reason, behind a modal's backdrop or away with its tab for a frame, docks again), it is a modal as before. The page's own pop-ups open over it. The height is resolved before the walk, from the sheet's detent; a `sheet(item:)` that opens is drawn one frame over the page's bottom rows, and the frame after the presenting view stops being drawn (its page navigated away) is laid out short once. The wheel over the sheet is its content's unless the content's `presentationContentInteraction(_:)` says `.scrolls` (the sheet moves a detent where the content can scroll no further — once the content's scroller passes the wheel on, after the `scrollChainingDelay(_:)` hold every nested scroller keeps, two seconds by default) or `.resizes` (the sheet grows before the content scrolls) — one detent at most every quarter of a second, so a trackpad's flick does not sweep them all. `presentationSizing(_:)` (`PresentationSizing`, with `.automatic`, `.fitted`, `.form`, `.page`, `fitted(horizontal:vertical:)`, `sticky(horizontal:vertical:)` and `proposedSize(for:context:)`; `AutomaticPresentationSizing`, `FittedPresentationSizing`, `FormPresentationSizing`, `PagePresentationSizing`, `PresentationSizingRoot.sizeThatFits(_:)`, `PresentationSizingContext`) **ships, in cells** (decisions A20): the content is proposed what the sizing returns and the sheet's box takes it, the height as a detent's and the width likewise — `.form` 64 columns by 25 rows and `.page` 96 columns by the content area's height, each capped by the terminal (macOS 15.8 measured: 470 by 400 points and 520 by 735); a fitted axis is the content's own; `sticky` keeps the largest size while the sheet stays presented — for one item, with `sheet(item:)`, whose sheet for another item starts from its own content, as SwiftUI presents another sheet; a detent's height wins over a sizing's. **`.automatic` stays the fitted sheet** every TUIkit sheet has been, where macOS resolves it to `.form.fitted(horizontal: false, vertical: true)` (measured) — so no sheet that does not ask changes size. macOS's minimum sheet (100 by 80 points) is not imposed. `proposedSize(for:context:)` returns TUIkit's `ProposedSize` and `sizeThatFits(_:)` a `ViewSize`, as `LayoutSubview`'s do (§2.2). `confirmationDialog` **shipped** — an action sheet (centred dimming overlay, ESC-dismiss, `titleVisibility`) with **vertically-stacked** buttons and the `.cancel` role sorted last; it reuses the alert host via a `verticalButtons` flag + a new `AlertButtonColumn`. `popover`/`fullScreenCover` ≈ `modal` variants; detents → fractional-height modal. |
| **`.scrollPosition` (bindable scroll state)** | Observable/bindable scroll position. | **Shipped**, both directions, with SwiftUI's whole vocabulary (2026-10-03). `ScrollPosition` + `.scrollPosition(_:anchor:)` and `.scrollPosition(id:anchor:)`; every initializer (`init(idType:)` and its `edge:`/`point:`/`x:`/`y:`/`x:y:` forms, `init(id:anchor:)`); `scrollTo(id:anchor:)` / `(edge:)` / `(point:)` / `(x:)` / `(y:)` / `(x:y:)`; `viewID`, `viewID(type:)`, `edge`, `point`, `x`, `y`, and a settable `isPositionedByUser`. Points and offsets are cells (`CellPoint`, `Int`), and `.leading`/`.trailing` and `x` move the horizontal axis. **The readings divide as SwiftUI's do (measured):** `edge`/`x`/`y`/`point` are the REQUEST, kept until the user scrolls (`point` only when both coordinates were asked for — after `scrollTo(y:)` alone it is `nil`); `isPositionedByUser` is set by a user scroll and by nothing else, not by a programmatic one and not by a frame being drawn (it used to be set by every report, so it read `true` on the first frame). One deliberate difference: `viewID(type:)` also answers with the row the view reports after a programmatic scroll and on the first frame, where SwiftUI's is still `nil` until the user scrolls past a row — a reading SwiftUI would give is never different, only present sooner. `viewID` (type-erased, `any Hashable & Sendable`) returns a REPORTED row only when the position was made with `idType:` — a `ForEach` id is not `Sendable` by signature, and `Sendable` is a marker no run-time cast can recover; an id the app passed in comes back either way. Writing rides the same seek machinery `ScrollViewProxy.scrollTo` drives, so a row request lands the frame it is made; an edge or offset moves the view before the content renders (`.bottom` via the tail-seek flag the End key raises, so the glue pins it against the real height). **Reading needed a new channel**: a seek matches on a stringified key, which is one-way — so `ForEach` now publishes the id VALUE alongside the key (`ChildViewCollection.anyID(at:)`), and the windowed stacks report the row under the anchor. The sample is the first row a person can actually SEE, not the one the "N more above" indicator is covering. A request is acted on once (a token, or the value differing from what was last reported), so scrolling away by hand sticks; the write-back only fires when the row changed, or a render-time write would re-trigger itself every frame. `scrollTargetLayout(isEnabled:)` is the mark `.scrollTargetBehavior(.viewAligned)` settles on (2026-10-05, decisions A6); SwiftUI needs it to know which views to report too, and TUIkit reports the windowed stack's rows with or without it, so `.scrollPosition(id:)` over a `.scrollTargetLayout()` stack — SwiftUI's own pairing — seeks and reads back unchanged (decisions B29). `ScrollPosition` is not `Sendable` (a reported id is whatever the app's id type is). A row seek is vertical: it rides the row-windowing handshake, so `scrollTo(id:)` scrolls rows, while an `x` offset or a leading/trailing edge is arithmetic and moves columns. **Seeking reaches `.id(_:)`-tagged non-`ForEach` targets too**, on the machinery the Scrolling row describes: `scrollTo(id:)` and a `.scrollPosition(id:)` binding both land on a tag carried by one of the content stack's own children, in eager `VStack` and windowed `LazyVStack` content alike. **What remains is the READ direction**, and it is narrower than "tagged targets": the sample travels through the content's keyed collection, so the write-back reports a row only where a windowed stack's content is a *single* `ForEach`. A tagged target is never reported back — but neither is an ordinary `ForEach` row in a stack holding two `ForEach`es, and an eager `VStack` reports nothing at all, so this is not the tag's doing. Seeking is per-child; reading is per-collection. **The `Transaction` scroll members ship (2026-10-03):** `scrollTargetAnchor` places the row a change made under it seeks to — preferred to a `.scrollPosition(id:anchor:)` modifier's anchor, as SwiftUI documents, and taken by a `ScrollPosition` request or a `ScrollViewProxy.scrollTo` that names no anchor of its own; `scrollContentOffsetAdjustmentBehavior = .disabled` stops a following `ScrollView`, `List` or `Table` from following for that change — which releases the follow, as a user scroll does — and lets a held row go — a `List`'s, a `Table`'s or a lazy stack's `.anchorPosition(.row)`, or the row at the top that the top-row hold keeps — to be held again from the line the change left it on (decisions B30); `scrollPositionUpdatePreservesVelocity` is recorded and met by the medium, nothing here ever having a velocity to stop.

**`defaultScrollAnchor(_:for:)` + `ScrollAnchorRole` ship** beside it, and the pair divides as *where you are* versus *what you are holding onto*. `.scrollPosition` is a POSITION: seek there now, read back where you ended up. An anchor is a POLICY: what survives the data changing underneath you. Reaching for the first when you want the second is the easy mistake, and it is silent — a seek lands once and then the next append walks away from it.

The role names which of three questions an anchor answers: **`.initialOffset`** (where the view opens), **`.sizeChanges`** (what it holds as its content grows) and **`.alignment`** (where content *shorter* than the view sits). The unlabelled form answers all three, as SwiftUI's does. Splitting the first two is worth having on its own merits — `.bottom, for: .initialOffset` is "join the log at the end, then leave me alone", which had no spelling — but it had to be done without disturbing `Documentation/Layout and scrolling/Scroll anchoring.md` §1.1, and the shape of the implementation is a consequence of that. TUIkit's follow is **positional**: being at the tail *is* the engagement, with no stored flag, and the opening placement is that rule's own first frame (a view with no content yet is at its own bottom). So the split is not an extra seek; it is one sentence — *the opening frame consults the opening anchor, every frame after it the standing one* — which for any view that used the unlabelled modifier substitutes a value for itself and cannot change anything. **Precedence is SwiftUI's (measured on macOS 15, 2026-10-05):** a role's anchor wins over the unlabelled one wherever either is written — SwiftUI's own example is `.defaultScrollAnchor(.bottom).defaultScrollAnchor(.topLeading, for: .alignment)` — and a `nil` role anchor states nothing, deferring to the unlabelled one (`.defaultScrollAnchor(nil, for: .initialOffset)` beside an unlabelled `.bottom` still opens at the tail). Until 2026-10-05 the unlabelled modifier wrote every role's anchor itself, so whichever call was nearer the view won, and a `nil` role meant "none"; both now read as SwiftUI's. A direct write of `\.defaultScrollAnchor` (TUIkit's, from before the roles) is a write of the unlabelled anchor.

**`ScrollAnchorRole.alignment` ships (2026-10-05, decisions A8).** A `ScrollView` whose content is shorter than its viewport draws it the anchor's `y` of the way down the room left over, rounded down to a line — and the unlabelled `.defaultScrollAnchor(.bottom)` states this role too, so three rows of a chat sit at the bottom of the view and push upwards as lines arrive, as in SwiftUI (measured). Unlike the other two roles it is not quantised: `.center` centres, `UnitPoint(x: 0, y: 0.6)` places at 60%. The placement is a slide of the drawn viewport, not a scroll offset — content that fits has an offset of 0 and a maximum of 0, and keeps them — so every consumer that maps the offset to a row (seeks, the reveal, the follow, the reported position) is untouched, while the slide carries the hit regions, layers and animated runs down with the lines; `ScrollGeometry` reports the room above as a top inset with the offset above it, SwiftUI's reading (measured: `contentOffset.y == -240`, `contentInsets.top == 240`), and the room does not come off `containerSize`, so `visibleRect` is taller than the view by it, as SwiftUI's is (measured: a 300-point view reports a container of 300 and a visible rect 540 tall). `List` and `Table` ignore the role, unlabelled or not, as macOS's do (measured). The anchor's `x` is ignored here as for the other roles: a horizontal scroll view keeps narrow content at its leading edge, where SwiftUI's places it by `x` (decisions-D2S B-D2S-1). **The two growing-content idioms, side by side:** a chat that starts at the bottom and grows upwards is `.defaultScrollAnchor(.bottom)`; a log that starts at the top, grows downwards and follows its tail once it fills the view is `.defaultScrollAnchor(.bottom, for: .initialOffset)` with `.defaultScrollAnchor(.bottom, for: .sizeChanges)` — the unlabelled anchor's behaviour before 2026-10-05. One measured SwiftUI quirk is not copied: bottom-aligned content that grows past the viewport by more than its room in one step lands SwiftUI's view at the top; here it stays at the tail, as it does in SwiftUI one row at a time.

**Two deviations, both from scrolling in rows rather than points.** The opening and standing anchors **quantise to three edges**: `y ≥ 0.75` is the bottom, `y ≤ 0.25` the top, and anything mid-content anchors *nothing* — SwiftUI's `.center` keeps the centre fixed, here it means "no anchor" (the alignment role, a placement rather than a policy, is not quantised). And **`x` is ignored**, for every role; horizontal scrolling exists, but the anchor modes are defined over rows.

**`.anchorPosition(_:)` is TUI-specific and stays that way** — not a stopgap for something SwiftUI does better, but a capability SwiftUI has no spelling for, in the same category as `MenuStyle.inline` and `.onMenuOpen(_:)`. Two things block folding it into the SwiftUI surface. **Row anchoring is an identity policy and SwiftUI's anchors are positional**: a `UnitPoint` cannot name a row, and the two are provably not interchangeable — on an append `.bottom` re-targets to whatever the last row now is, while `.row(lastID)` pins that row and lets new ones arrive below it; a prepend leaves `.top` on the new first row and `.row(firstID)` on the old one. `ScrollPosition(id:)` does not rescue it either, being a seek rather than a standing hold. **And the anchor mode is BOUND, not merely declared**: `defaultScrollAnchor` is one-way in both frameworks, but §1.2's shadow modes, §1.3's user restore and §1.4's code-side restore all run through the binding — the read-back is what makes "am I still following the log?" answerable as `anchor == nil`, `.window`-vs-`nil` is what makes a release observable, and writing `nil` *is* the restore. SwiftUI offers nothing here; `isPositionedByUser` is one Bool that says the user moved it, not which policy now holds. |
| **Viewport windowing for a nested `LazyVStack`** | A `LazyVStack` that is the *direct* content of a `ScrollView` now windows to the viewport (§2.8) — the offset-publishing + render-only-visible policy landed — and `LazyVStack(pinnedViews:)`, `LazyVGrid(pinnedViews:)`, `LazyHStack(pinnedViews:)` and `LazyHGrid(pinnedViews:)` pin their sections' headers and footers wherever they sit (§2.8; the horizontal pair since decisions A44). Remaining: a `LazyVStack` nested *below* other scroll content isn't at the content origin so it can't map the offset yet. | Thread the stack's own y-offset within the scroll content so a non-top stack can window too. |
| **`@FocusState` as a property wrapper** | *(shipped)* `@FocusState var x: Bool` / `var f: Field?` + `.focused($x)` / `.focused($f, equals:)` + `.defaultFocus($f, value)`, and `@FocusState.Binding var f: Field?` in a child that is handed `$f` (its `projectedValue` passes it on), matching SwiftUI. **Focus scopes ship too:** `.focusScope(_:)` with a `@Namespace`, `.prefersDefaultFocus(_:in:)` on the control that should take the focus by default there, and `@Environment(\.resetFocus)` (`ResetFocusAction`) to put it back. The preference applies on the frame it first appears if nothing holds the focus but the manager's own first-control choice — at launch, in a freshly presented sheet, or later, for as long as nobody has moved, clicked or pressed a key on that choice and neither the app nor a `.defaultFocus` has moved it — and is then spent until its scope leaves the screen; a reset moves the focus to it (or to the scope's first control) only from inside the scope. Without a `.focusScope` the scope is the whole screen. See decisions B35. The value is derived from the persistent `FocusManager`; the imperative handle formerly called `FocusState` is now `FocusReference`. Remaining: `@FocusState`'s `Bool` form on several focusables in one `.focused` — SwiftUI's is `true` while the focus is anywhere in the view it modifies, TUIkit's binds the first focusable inside it (`focused(_:equals:)` binds one control in both) — and re-applying a default when a dismissed focus scope re-appears. |
| **`.keyboardShortcut` (general key equivalents)** | Bind an arbitrary key to any action. | The SEMANTIC actions shipped: `.keyboardShortcut(.defaultAction)` makes a Button the default (Return/Enter fires it whenever the focused control lets the key fall through — a `TextEditor` keeps its newline, a list keeps its row activation, a submit-less `TextField` lets Return through) and `.cancelAction` binds Escape. Arbitrary equivalents **shipped** too: `.keyboardShortcut("s", modifiers:)` with `KeyEquivalent` and `EventModifiers`. A shortcut activates a `Button` (and the controls built on one: `Link`, `NavigationLink`, a menu row) or flips a `Toggle`, as SwiftUI's "activate a button or toggle"; on other controls it does nothing, as SwiftUI documents no other. `KeyboardShortcut`'s `key`, `modifiers` and `localization` read back as written and are settable, as SwiftUI's are; `.defaultAction` is Return with no modifiers and equal to `KeyboardShortcut(.return, modifiers: [])`; `init(_:modifiers:localization:)`, `.keyboardShortcut(_:modifiers:localization:)` and the optional `.keyboardShortcut(_: KeyboardShortcut?)` (whose `nil` assigns nothing) are there too. `\.keyboardShortcut` reads the shortcut assigned to the view or an ancestor (`nil` where none was), read-only as SwiftUI's is, for a button style that draws the default button differently; a change of shortcut reaches a memo below the modifier. Every `Localization` behaves as `.custom`: a terminal reports the character a key typed, not its place on the keyboard, so there is no layout to remap from, and TUIkit lays out left to right only, so nothing mirrors. `EventModifiers` has SwiftUI's raw values (so a stored `rawValue` reads back the same set) and all six members; `.capsLock` and `.numericPad` are in a `KeyPress`'s modifiers where the terminal speaks the kitty keyboard protocol (`\.keyboardReporting`) and not in its legacy encodings (a Caps-Locked letter arrives as the uppercase character), except `.numericPad` on a keypad in application mode, which TUIkit turns off at startup and reads anyway (`Terminal compatibility survey.md`, "Caps Lock and the numeric pad"); a shortcut ignores both, as AppKit's key equivalents do, so one that requires either never fires, and `isDeliverableInTerminal` says so. A terminal reports Control, Option and Shift but never ⌘, so the SwiftUI default `modifiers: .command` is remapped at registration to whatever the TUI-specific `.commandKey(_:)` names — `.control` (default), `.option`, `.bare`, or `.unavailable` — which is what lets one `View` source carry ⌘-shortcuts under both frameworks. Shift on a printable key is the character's CASE, not a modifier bit (that is all a terminal sends), so a shortcut reads `"A"` as Shift-A while the `KeyEquivalent` itself keeps its case, as SwiftUI's does; and Control shortcuts on `h i j m [` are undeliverable in the legacy encodings because the C0 range spends those bytes on Backspace/Tab/Return/Escape (they fire where the kitty keyboard protocol is spoken, and an unbound one still acts as that key) (Ctrl-C and Ctrl-Z do arrive: raw mode turns them off as signal characters, so a ⌘C or ⌘Z shortcut fires — and a ⌘Z one takes the suspend key while it is registered; Ctrl-\ does too, though it is the user's quit key: while the app binds it the driver's quit is masked, and an unbound one still quits; Ctrl-4 and Ctrl-| are undeliverable in the legacy encodings, xterm, VTE, Konsole and the Linux console sending Ctrl-\\'s byte for them, though a binding on one masks the quit and fires where no Ctrl-\ binding takes the byte first — decisions F2 A-F2-1) — see `KeyboardShortcut.isDeliverableInTerminal`. |
| **List editing: `onDelete`/`onMove`, `.listRowInsets`/`.listRowBackground`/`.listSectionSeparator`** | Editable lists. | `EditMode` + `\.editMode` + `EditButton` **shipped**, and `ForEach.onMove(perform:)` / `onDelete(perform:)` now **shipped**: attach the actions to a `ForEach` inside a `List` and press **Delete**/**Backspace** on the focused row to delete it, or drag a row with the mouse to reorder it (mirroring SwiftUI's drag trigger; `.rowReorderFeedback(_:)` chooses live-shuffle / dimmed-at-the-slot / row-on-the-pointer feedback). `move(fromOffsets:toOffset:)` / `remove(atOffsets:)` collection helpers ship alongside (constrained `Self.Index == Int` so they beat the Foundation⇄SwiftUI cross-import overlay's own unlinkable copies). `.onDelete` belongs to the `ForEach` that produced the ROW, wherever that `ForEach` sits — the list's whole content, a `Section`'s content, or the content of a `Section` an outer `ForEach` produced, with or without a `Group`, an `if`/`else` or a view of your own between the loop and the `Section` or `List` holding it — and the offsets it hands the action are indices into THAT collection, not the list's row numbering (which counts section headers and every earlier section's rows). A `Group` or an `if`/`else` contributes exactly its content's rows, so the `List` and the `Section` look straight through any number of them, as SwiftUI does (measured: with a `Group`, two, or an `if`/`else` around the loop every row is still deletable, and a delete removes the element that row draws); an `if`/`else` is looked through under its branch's identity step, so each arm's rows keep their own `@State`, and the same look finds a wrapped loop's `dropDestination(for:action:)` and a wrapped `OutlineGroup`. Until 2026-09-23 both stopped at the wrapper: Delete was claimed on no row, and the rows were keyed by position rather than by element, so a `String` or `UUID` selection could not select one either. **A view of your own whose `body` is the loop** — `List { Rows() }`, SwiftUI's own way to factor a list's rows out — is looked through the same way since 2026-09-24, as SwiftUI does (measured the same way: three elements are three rows, each selected by its element and each deletable, flat, in a `Section`, with `@State` on the view, one such view inside another, and with an `if`/`else` body). So is a view whose body is a `Section`, an `OutlineGroup`, or any of these inside `Group`s, `if`/`else`s, lone `if`s and further views of your own. The list evaluates the body exactly as drawing the view would — its `@State` bound at the view's own identity, its `@Environment` resolved, an `@Observable` it reads observed — and a long loop that is the `List`'s whole content stays windowed, built only as far as the rows on screen (inside a `Section` it is built whole, as a `Section { ForEach(…) }` already was, and the body is evaluated twice a frame). Until then the whole loop was drawn as ONE row, answering to no `String` selection and taking no Delete, drop or pick-up. All of that is as the `List`'s or a `Section`'s whole content. A view of your own whose body is anything else is still one row, as in SwiftUI when that body is one view (a stack, a `Text`). A body that is several views is taken apart into rows since 2026-10-05 (a three-`Text` body is three rows), alone or beside other rows, and `List { Section("Other") { … }; ItemsSection() }` is two sections with the second's rows selected by element and deletable, as SwiftUI's are (measured). **Gaps remain**: a modifier on a loop (`ForEach(…).padding()`) is still one row; and a view of your own around a LOOP beside other rows — `List { Rows(); Text("end") }` — draws the loop's rows, but SwiftUI's are deletable and selected by element, where here they keep the hand-written rule: the child walk that reads mixed content cannot attribute rows inside a view's body to its loop. So do two more: a modifier on the view itself (`List { Rows().foregroundStyle(.red) }`, or an `.environment(…)` or `.task {}` there, makes the content a modified view, which is flattened as one child), and a view of your own whose body is a `Section`, looped (`ForEach(regions) { RegionSection(region: $0) }` — whether a loop's rows are sections is decided from the row type, so each region draws as one row with no header). Two exceptions, both refusals rather than wrong deletes, and both covering `.onDelete` and `.onMove` alike: a `ForEach` that is not its container's whole content — a `Section`'s or the `List`'s own, one flattening and one rule, and a `Group` holding a hand-written row beside the loop is the same arrangement — where the children arrive already flattened and no row can be attributed to the `ForEach` (its rows do carry their selection ids since 2026-09-24, but an id is a selection value, not a position in a collection — a tag can replace it and a second loop can share it — so it attributes nothing); wiring it up anyway measures the offset from the CONTAINER's first row rather than from the loop's, so a press on the second looped row deletes the third element. A hand-written row beside the loop is the obvious way to reach that, but two `ForEach`es sharing one container reach it too, and there the guess is worse still: with nothing to choose between two sets of actions, a press on a row of the second loop edits the FIRST loop's collection. And an editing action on an outer `ForEach` whose rows are `Section`s — that addresses the sections, and neither TUIkit nor SwiftUI shows an affordance for deleting or dragging one; reaching it from a row would delete or reorder whole sections while the cursor was pointing at a row inside one. Two wrappers still hide a loop's actions from every row — gaps, not decisions, for SwiftUI edits the right element through both: a modifier written after `.onDelete` / `.onMove`, as in `ForEach(…).onDelete(…).foregroundStyle(.red)`, which has to reach every row and so hands the list flattened ones (write it on the row, or on the `List`; the rows answer to their elements' ids there since 2026-09-24, not to their positions, but still to no editing action); and `AnyView`: a container sees through one to the views it erases since 2026-10-05, so `List { AnyView(ForEach(…)) }` draws the loop's rows, each selected by its element, but they answer to no editing action (it drew the whole loop as ONE row before). After a delete the cursor keeps the row that slid up into the slot, clamped to the deleted row's own section so it never lands on the chrome below it — and when the delete emptied that section, it walks to the nearest row it can stand on (down first, then up) rather than settling on the header the section left behind. "Can stand on" is the same test the cursor's own Up/Down applies, so a `.selectionDisabled()` row is walked past as well, not just a header or a footer. `.onMove` follows the same rule and adds one of its own: a row cannot be reordered OUT of its `ForEach`. Its rows are the only drop targets the gesture publishes, so the pointer, the landing slot, a `.live` shuffle and the keyboard Home/End all stop at the section's edge instead of handing an offset to a collection the row never belonged to — which is SwiftUI's behaviour too (a row stays in its section). A multi-row drag likewise picks up only the selected rows inside the grabbed row's own section. **`.listRowInsets(_:)`, `.listRowInsets(_:_:)` and `.listRowBackground(_:)` shipped.** Insets are padding around the row's content (`nil` = the list's default, i.e. the identity — NOT zero), and the rest of the list still sees a full-width row; the edge form insets the edges it names in cells and leaves the rest at the default, and two insets on one row add up where SwiftUI's inner one replaces the outer on the edges they share. A background fills the **row**, not the text: the full width offered, every line of a multi-line row, with the content composited over it and the content's hit regions kept, so a button in a backed row is still clickable. There are two overloads because **TUIkit's `Color` is a value, not a `View`** (a palette is a table of them; cells are painted with them, not composed of them) — the generic `<V: View>` one SwiftUI has, plus a `Color?` one so `listRowBackground(Color.red)` compiles and means the same thing, exactly as `background(_:)` already does. A selected row still draws the selection highlight over the background, as in SwiftUI. `.listRowSeparator` / `.listSectionSeparator` remain inert stubs: the terminal `List` draws no per-row rules to show or hide. |
| **Common modifiers: `.opacity`(View), `.truncationMode`(View), `.onSubmit`, `.refreshable`, `.contextMenu`** | Frequently used; each terminal-expressible. | `.id(_:)`, `.focusable(_:interactions:)` (an `.edit` view is focused by a click as well as by Tab, as SwiftUI's is; an `.activate`-only view, which SwiftUI makes focusable only under all-controls keyboard navigation, is always a Tab stop and not focused by a click), `.searchable(text:placement:prompt:)`, `.onSubmit(of:_:)`, `.tag(_:includeOptional:)` and `.contextMenu(menuItems:)` **shipped** (and `.contextMenu(menuItems:preview:)`, the same menu with the preview not shown, as SwiftUI documents for macOS) — `.contextMenu` is a right-click (or Ctrl-click, where a terminal swallows right-click) pop-up of `Button`s anchored at the click point, dismissed by selection, Escape, or an outside click; a right-click bubbles to an ancestor menu when a child doesn't handle it (the input dispatcher was extended to bubble the secondary button like the wheel), and reliable right-click needs Apple Terminal / Ghostty / Warp (iTerm2 claims it by default — see Terminal compatibility survey.md); the TUI-specific `.onMenuOpen(_:)` reports a pop-up (a `contextMenu` or a `Menu`) OPENING to any view above it, which is how an app clears whatever the last choice left on screen so choosing the same item twice reads as two choices — SwiftUI has no equivalent, its menus being system-drawn. **`Menu`'s `primaryAction:` initialisers shipped** (decisions B11): the label performs the action — Return, Space or a click — and the menu opens from the caret beside it by a click, or by the Down arrow; the control stays one focus stop. The refusal that stood here named a platform's rendering (a chevron and a long press) rather than SwiftUI's one-line contract. The `systemImage:` forms ship too; the `image:` forms name an asset-catalogue `ImageResource` and join that family's decline. `.menuStyle(.button)` (`ButtonMenuStyle`) draws as the default does — on macOS too it is a button with an arrow, press-drag-release choosing — and its trigger takes the button style in force. **`Menu(configuration)` shipped**: inside a custom `MenuStyle`'s body it draws the menu as the style outside the custom one would, as the other configuration initialisers do. **`.menuIndicator(_:)` and `.menuOrder(_:)` shipped**: the first takes away (`.hidden`) the caret a `Menu` draws beside its label; the second records an order every menu here already keeps — top to bottom as written, which is what `.automatic` resolves to on macOS too. **`.menuActionDismissBehavior(_:)` shipped**, with `MenuActionDismissBehavior` (`.automatic`/`.enabled`/`.disabled`) — the switch between a menu of COMMANDS, which closes behind a choice, and a menu of SETTINGS, which should not make you re-open it after every flip. It rides the environment, so it scopes to whatever subtree it is written on: on the `Menu` it governs every item, on one `Button` only that item, which is how a sticky toggle and an ordinary `Done` share a menu. **That sticky toggle is a real `Toggle` as of 2026-09-20**, and until then it could only be hand-rolled — a `Button` whose label was a ``ToggleCharacterSet`` mark glued to the title, which is what the Example's own sticky-menu demo did. A pop-up menu's rows are not focus stops: each claims an ORDINAL from the menu's row sink and publishes its action there, and only `_ButtonCore` made that claim, so a `Toggle` written in a menu drew on a line the arrows and the sliced click map both walked past — a control by appearance and nothing by behaviour. It is now a menu ROW: inside a pop-up `Menu` or a `.contextMenu` a `Toggle` renders as the row `Button` it has to be, carrying its own on/off mark where a plain row carries nothing, which is also SwiftUI's shape (a menu item with a state mark, not a switch drawn in a menu). It therefore takes the row's whole look for free — the breathing bar across the menu's interior, the key-equivalent column at the trailing edge, the hover wash — none of which a toggle drawing its own focus glow would have picked up, and all of which lives in `_MenuItemButtonStyle` and so reaches Buttons only. **Two things have no row to draw into and do not apply there:** a custom `ToggleStyle` (a menu draws menu items, as SwiftUI's does) and the TUI-specific `.toggleContent(_:)`. An INLINE menu is untouched — its rows really are page focus stops, so a `Toggle` in one stays the ordinary control, registered by its own focus id. **A `Picker` in a pop-up menu is still the unfixed half of this:** SwiftUI turns one into a SUBMENU, TUIkit has no submenu, and a `Picker` written in a menu draws its own collapsed drop-down on a line the menu cannot reach. It cannot be composed into one row the way a `Toggle` can — it is N choices, not a flip — so it waits on either submenus or a decision to spell it as SwiftUI's `.pickerStyle(.inline)` (the title as an unselectable heading row, one marked row per option). Until then: put the choice on the page, or give each option its own `Button`. `.automatic` is SwiftUI's deferral to "the policies of the component", and every menu a terminal draws has the same policy, so it resolves to dismissing — but the three spellings stay distinct rather than collapsing `.automatic` into `.enabled`, and the resolution lives in one computed property instead of a `!= .disabled` at the call site. One deviation, deliberate: `.disabled` is `@available(macOS, unavailable)` in SwiftUI — a Mac menu always closes behind a choice — so a desktop terminal has no platform behaviour to copy for the stay-open case, and TUIkit defines one. A **press-and-hold** release closes the menu whatever the behaviour says: `.disabled` keeps a menu up for more CLICKS, and a held gesture has no second act — the button is up, the tracking session that opened the menu is over, which is also how AppKit ends every menu session. A click on a row of an already-open menu still leaves it up. It reaches only menu ITEMS: Escape and an outside click still close an open menu, and an `.alert`'s buttons are unaffected — they answer a question rather than issue a command, and the internal channel `Button` consults happens to be shared by both presentations, so the alert re-asserts `.enabled` on its own subtree. — `.id` splices a keyed identity step (resets `@State`, re-fires `onAppear`/`task`, drops the cached buffer), and its key is the tag `ScrollViewProxy.scrollTo(_:anchor:)` seeks by — the shape SwiftUI's own `ScrollViewReader` example is built out of, which reached nothing here until 2026-09-20 because the seek only ever compared `ForEach` row keys; `.focusable` makes any view a Tab stop + click-to-focus (`.activate`) and lets `.focused($x)` bind to a plain view, and `.focusable(false)` is the other half of the parameter rather than the absence of the first: it takes the content OUT of the ring, so a `Button` told it is not focusable is no longer a Tab stop and no longer answers Return (it went on taking both until 2026-09-20, the modifier having only declined to add a stop of its OWN). A click still activates it, as in SwiftUI — `isFocusable` names focus, not interaction. **Two deviations, both from the fact that a terminal modifier wraps a SUBTREE where SwiftUI's speaks for one node:** it reaches every control inside, where SwiftUI's `.focusable(false)` on a `VStack` leaves the buttons in it focusable; and it is ADDITIVE, so an inner `.focusable(true)` does not re-open a subtree an ancestor closed. Additive is not a preference — the suppression is `\.isFocusSuppressed`, the same flag `.hidden()` sets (see the `.hidden()` row below), and a `true` case that cleared it would put a hidden view's `.focusable()` back in the ring, which is exactly the Tab stop with no picture behind it that the flag exists to prevent. `.disabled(_:)` composes the same way and for a related reason, and so does `.focusEffectDisabled(_:)` — there additive is SwiftUI's OWN rule rather than a deviation, Apple documenting that "the higher views in a view hierarchy can override the value you set on this view" and working the example (an inner `false` inside an outer `true` draws no focus effect); it was a plain environment write until 2026-09-20, so an inner `false` put the indication back; `.searchable` composes a glyph + bound `TextField` above the content (filtering is app-driven; `placement` is inert in a terminal, but the TUI-specific `.searchFieldIconPlacement(.leading/.trailing)` moves the magnifier and swaps it 🔎↔🔍 so its lens faces the field). **`.searchSuggestions(_:)` and `.searchCompletion(_:)` ship**, feeding the combo-box drop-down `.textInputSuggestions(_:)` already had — the same `DropdownMenu` the `Picker` uses — so a suggestion is a `Text`, a view carrying a completion, or a `Divider`, and the menu opens on demand (Down at the caret, or the `▾`) rather than on focus. They reach the SEARCH field only: the suggestions arrive on an environment key of their own and are handed to that one field, because sharing `\.textInputSuggestions` would silently arm any `TextField` the caller happens to have in the content. With no `.searchSuggestions` of its own the field inherits an outer `.textInputSuggestions` like any other text field. **Omitted: `searchSuggestions(_:for:)` and `SearchSuggestionsPlacement`** — SwiftUI has two places to put suggestions (a menu, or the content below), so it needs a per-placement visibility; a terminal has the drop-down, and honouring one case of that OptionSet while ignoring the other is the partial-seam shape this file declines elsewhere. And the two companions ship as well: `\.isSearching` is true in the CONTENT while the field holds the keyboard, and `\.dismissSearch` empties the query and hands the keyboard back — SwiftUI's also takes the field away, which here it cannot, the field being part of the layout rather than presented into a navigation bar; `.onSubmit(of:)` cascades a submit action through the environment (`.text` for `TextField`/`SecureField`, `.search` for the `.searchable` field) and composes additively with a field's own `.onSubmit(_:)` closure — the combined action stays nil when empty so Return still falls through to a default button, and `TextEditor` doesn't submit (Return = newline); **`.submitLabel(_:)` is deliberately ABSENT** — it names an on-screen keyboard's Return key, and a terminal has neither. It shipped first as a value stored in the environment that nothing read; that is the stub shape rule 2 forbids, so `SubmitLabel`, the modifier and `\.submitLabel` were all removed and the call now fails to compile. The behaviour it travels with, `.onSubmit`, is fully implemented. **The substitute was evaluated and declined**, so it is settled rather than pending: a status-bar hint ("⏎ Send") while the field holds focus, riding the per-focus-section registration `StatusBarState` already has. It fails on four counts — a `StatusBarItem`'s identity IS its shortcut and the merge drops globals sharing one, so it would evict an app's own "⏎ select" item on focus and restore it on blur; it could lie, because Return only submits when an action exists (`combinedSubmitAction` returns `nil` otherwise, on purpose, so Return reaches a dialog's default button) and a label with no action would advertise "Send" while Return fired "Cancel"; items joining and leaving on every Tab churn the bar and can flip it into compact rendering at narrow widths; and `\.statusBar` is Optional, so it would be inert in an app that does not use the bar. If it is ever wanted, the shape is opt-in app-level chrome — a TUI-specific `.submitLabelHint(_:)`, default off — which makes `.submitLabel` data the app chooses to render and the collision the app's own decision. The full reasoning is the `why` on `SubmitLabel` in `parity-map.json`, where `--stale` keeps it honest. **`.truncationMode(_:)` on `View` shipped** — the cascading one; `Text` and `TableColumn` had their own all along, and a `Text`'s own still wins inside a subtree that cascaded a different mode (`TextStyle.truncationMode` became Optional so "this view was told" and "nobody said" stay distinguishable). It applies wherever text is clipped, not only under a line limit. **`.lineLimit(_:)` on `View` shipped** — the cascading one, alongside `Text`'s own. It needed a decision `.truncationMode` did not: SwiftUI's `.lineLimit(nil)` means *unlimited*, which a plain `Int?` cannot distinguish from *unset*, and getting that wrong makes an inherited limit unresettable. So what is stored is a `LineLimit` (`.unlimited` / `.lines(_:)`) and the `Optional` around it carries "was one stated" — the modifiers still take `Int?`, matching SwiftUI. Both passes resolve it the same way, so a cascaded limit caps the measured height as well as the rendered one. **`Text.bold(_:)`, `.italic(_:)`, `.underline(_:)`, `.strikethrough(_:)` shipped** — and needed a model change first, recorded until now as §9 of `Parity work blocked on a decision.md`. `TextStyle`'s emphasis flags were plain `Bool`, so a `Text` could not tell "I said no" from "I said nothing", and the merge against the style cascade could only be an OR — an attribute any layer could turn on and none could turn off.
